**Proofs.**

*Proof of Lemma 2.3.2.7f.*

1. *Finite exponent $0 < q < \infty$ (`Complete.lean:27–55`, `Lebesgue/Add.lean:202–229`):* On the conull set where $f_n(a) \to f_{\lim}(a)$ in $E$, continuity of the extended norm `continuous_enorm` and of $t \mapsto t^q$ on $[0, \infty]$ (`ENNReal.continuous_rpow_const`) implies $\lVert f_n(a)\rVert_e^q \to \lVert f_{\lim}(a)\rVert_e^q$, so $\lVert f_{\lim}(a)\rVert_e^q = \liminf_{n \to \infty} \lVert f_n(a)\rVert_e^q$ (`Tendsto.liminf_eq`). Integrating against $\mu$ via `lintegral_congr_ae` gives the equality in (1) (`eLpNorm'_lim_eq_lintegral_liminf`, `:27–36`). Next, each $g_n(a) := \lVert f_n(a)\rVert_e^q$ is `AEMeasurable` (`(hf n).enorm.pow_const q`), so Fatou's lemma `lintegral_liminf_le'` (`Lebesgue/Add.lean:202–229`, which rewrites $\liminf_{n \to \infty} g_n(a) = \sup_{N \in \mathbb N} \inf_{i \ge N} g_i(a)$, applies the a.e.-monotone convergence theorem `lintegral_iSup'` proved in §2.3.2.6 to the increasing sequence $N \mapsto \inf_{i \ge N} g_i$, and bounds $\int^- (\inf_{i \ge N} g_i) \le \inf_{i \ge N} \int^- g_i$) yields
   $$\int^- a,\, \liminf_{n \to \infty} \lVert f_n(a)\rVert_e^q\,d\mu \le \liminf_{n \to \infty} \int^- a,\, \lVert f_n(a)\rVert_e^q\,d\mu.$$
   Because $q > 0$, $t \mapsto t^q$ is strictly monotone (`ENNReal.strictMono_rpow_of_pos`) and surjective (`ENNReal.rpow_left_bijective`) on the complete lattice $[0, \infty]$, hence an order isomorphism commuting with `liminf` (`OrderIso.liminf_apply`, `:48–51`):
   $$\left(\liminf_{n \to \infty} \operatorname{eLpNorm}'(f_n, q, \mu)\right)^q = \liminf_{n \to \infty} \operatorname{eLpNorm}'(f_n, q, \mu)^q = \liminf_{n \to \infty} \int^- a,\, \lVert f_n(a)\rVert_e^q\,d\mu.$$
   Taking $q^{-1}$-th powers via `ENNReal.le_rpow_inv_iff` (`0 < q⁻¹`) gives $\operatorname{eLpNorm}'(f_{\lim}, q, \mu) \le \liminf_{n \to \infty} \operatorname{eLpNorm}'(f_n, q, \mu)$ (`:43–54`).

2. *Exponent $p = \infty$ (`Complete.lean:56–71`, `EssSup.lean:364–369`, `Order/Filter/ENNReal.lean:236–244`):* Composing $f_n(x) \to f_{\lim}(x)$ with `continuous_enorm` gives $\lVert f_{\lim}(x)\rVert_e = \liminf_{m \to \infty} \lVert f_m(x)\rVert_e$ $\mu$-a.e., so `essSup_congr_ae` gives the equality in (2) (`:56–63`). For the inequality, we prove `ENNReal.essSup_liminf_le` (`EssSup.lean:364–369`, backed by `limsup_liminf_le_liminf_limsup` at `Order/Filter/ENNReal.lean:236–244`): for each individual index $b \in \iota$, $\lVert f_b(x)\rVert_e \le \operatorname{essSup}_y \lVert f_b(y)\rVert_e$ holds on a conull set $S_b \in \mathrm{ae}\,\mu$ (`ENNReal.ae_le_essSup`, `EssSup.lean:348`). Because $\iota$ is **countable** and $\mathrm{ae}\,\mu$ is closed under countable intersections (`CountableInterFilter (ae μ)`, `eventually_countable_forall`), $S_\infty := \bigcap_{b \in \iota} S_b$ belongs to $\mathrm{ae}\,\mu$. For every $x \in S_\infty$, $\lVert f_b(x)\rVert_e \le \operatorname{essSup} \lVert f_b\rVert_e$ holds for **all** $b \in \iota$ simultaneously, so monotonicity of `atTop.liminf` (`Filter.liminf_le_liminf`) gives $\liminf_{b \to \infty} \lVert f_b(x)\rVert_e \le \liminf_{b \to \infty} \operatorname{essSup} \lVert f_b\rVert_e$ for $\mu$-a.e. $x$. By definition of `essSup` as the infimum of $\mu$-a.e. upper bounds (`sInf_le`), $\operatorname{essSup}_x (\liminf_b \lVert f_b(x)\rVert_e) \le \liminf_b \operatorname{essSup} \lVert f_b\rVert_e$.

3. *Unified sequential and filter bounds (`Complete.lean:73–105`):* Splitting on $p = 0$ (both sides $0$), $p = \infty$ (part 2), and $0 < p < \infty$ (part 1 at $q = p.\mathrm{toReal} > 0$, `ENNReal.toReal_pos`) gives `eLpNorm_lim_le_liminf_eLpNorm` (`:73–84`). For `eLpNorm_le_of_ae_tendsto` (`:89–105`), since $u$ is proper and countably generated, `exists_seq_tendsto u` (§2.3.2.8) selects $v : \mathbb N \to \iota$ with $v \to u$; then $f_{v(n)}(x) \to g(x)$ $\mu$-a.e., so $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(g, p, \mu) \le \liminf_{n \to \infty} \operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_{v(n)}, p, \mu)$. Because $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_{v(n)}, p, \mu) \le C$ holds eventually (`hv.eventually bound`), every eventual lower bound $b$ of $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_{v(n)}, p, \mu)$ satisfies $b \le \operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_{v(n_0)}, p, \mu) \le C$ at some large $n_0$, whence $\liminf_n \operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_{v(n)}, p, \mu) \le C$ (`liminf_le_of_le`, `:102–104`). $\square$

*Proof of Lemma 2.3.2.7g.*

1. *Case $1 \le p < \infty$ (`Complete.lean:193–289, 312–319`):* Since $p \neq \infty$ and $1 \le p$, `ENNReal.ofReal_le_iff_le_toReal` gives $1 \le q := p.\mathrm{toReal} < \infty$, and `eLpNorm_eq_eLpNorm'` rewrites the hypothesis as $\operatorname{eLpNorm}'(f_n - f_m, q, \mu) < B_N$ for $N \le n, m$ (`:312–319`).
   - *Step 1a (`eLpNorm'_sum_norm_sub_le_tsum_of_cauchy_eLpNorm'`, `:193–208`):* Set $d_i(x) := \lVert f_{i+1}(x) - f_i(x)\rVert \ge 0$. Each $d_i$ is `AEStronglyMeasurable` (`((hf (i + 1)).sub (hf i)).norm`). Because **$1 \le q$**, Minkowski's finite-sum inequality `eLpNorm'_sum_le` (`TriangleInequality.lean:120–125`, proved by `Finset` induction from two-term `eLpNorm'_add_le`, `:29–35`) applies to $\sum_{i=0}^n d_i$, and at $m = i$ we have $\operatorname{eLpNorm}'(d_i, q, \mu) = \operatorname{eLpNorm}'(f_{i+1} - f_i, q, \mu) \le B_i$ (`eLpNorm'_norm` + `h_cau i (i + 1) i`):
     $$\operatorname{eLpNorm}'\!\left(\sum_{i=0}^n d_i, q, \mu\right) \le \sum_{i=0}^n \operatorname{eLpNorm}'(d_i, q, \mu) \le \sum_{i=0}^n B_i \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty B_i.$$
   - *Step 1b (`lintegral_rpow_sum_enorm_sub_le_rpow_tsum` and `lintegral_rpow_tsum_coe_enorm_sub_le_tsum`, `:209–252`):* Raising both sides of Step 1a to $q > 0$ (`ENNReal.le_rpow_inv_iff`) and using nonnegativity $\lVert \sum_{i=0}^n d_i(a)\rVert_e = \sum_{i=0}^n \lVert f_{i+1}(a) - f_i(a)\rVert_e$ gives $\int^- a, \bigl(\sum_{i=0}^n \lVert f_{i+1}(a) - f_i(a)\rVert_e\bigr)^q\,d\mu \le \bigl(\sum_{i=0}^\infty B_i\bigr)^q$ for every $n \in \mathbb N$. In $[0, \infty]$, an infinite series `∑' i, u i` is the `liminf` of its shifted partial sums: $\sum_{i=0}^\infty u_i = \liminf_{n \to \infty} \sum_{i=0}^n u_i$ (`ENNReal.tsum_eq_liminf_sum_nat` + `liminf_nat_add`). Commuting the order isomorphism $t \mapsto t^q$ with `atTop.liminf` pointwise (`:241–249`) and applying Fatou's lemma `lintegral_liminf_le'` (`:250–251`) yields
     $$\int^- a,\, \left(\sum_{i=0}^\infty \lVert f_{i+1}(a) - f_i(a)\rVert_e\right)^q d\mu \le \liminf_{n \to \infty} \int^- a,\, \left(\sum_{i=0}^n \lVert f_{i+1}(a) - f_i(a)\rVert_e\right)^q d\mu \le \left(\sum_{i=0}^\infty B_i\right)^q.$$
   - *Step 1c (`tsum_enorm_sub_ae_lt_top` and Banach telescoping, `:253–289`):* Because $\sum_{i=0}^\infty B_i \neq \infty$ and $q > 0$, $(\sum_{i=0}^\infty B_i)^q < \infty$ (`ENNReal.rpow_lt_top_of_nonneg`), so the `lintegral` in Step 1b is strictly finite (`≠ ∞`). Note that $H(x) := \bigl(\sum_{i=0}^\infty \lVert f_{i+1}(x) - f_i(x)\rVert_e\bigr)^q$ is `AEMeasurable` (`AEMeasurable.tsum` + `.pow_const`). By `ae_lt_top'` (`Markov.lean:148–152`: if $Z := \{x : H(x) = \infty\}$ had $\mu(Z) \neq 0$, Markov's inequality on a measurable representative would force $\int^- H\,d\mu = \infty$, **requiring neither $\sigma$-finiteness nor finiteness of $\mu$**), $H(x) < \infty$ on a conull set $\Omega_1 \in \mathrm{ae}\,\mu$, and taking $q^{-1}$-th powers (`ENNReal.top_rpow_of_pos`) gives $\sum_{i=0}^\infty \lVert f_{i+1}(x) - f_i(x)\rVert_e < \infty$ for all $x \in \Omega_1$. On $\Omega_1$, `ENNReal.tsum_coe_ne_top_iff_summable` gives `Summable (fun i => ‖f (i + 1) x - f i x‖₊)`, so **`[CompleteSpace E]`** yields `Summable (fun i => f (i + 1) x - f i x)` in $E$ via `Summable.of_nnnorm` (`InfiniteSum.lean:180–198`, direct comparison test reducing absolute summability to Cauchy finite partial sums in the complete space $E$). Finally, `Finset.sum_range_sub` telescopes $\sum_{i=0}^{n-1} (f_{i+1}(x) - f_i(x)) = f_n(x) - f_0(x)$, so adding the constant $f_0(x)$ to `hx.hasSum.tendsto_sum_nat` gives $f_n(x) \to \bigl(\sum_{i=0}^\infty (f_{i+1}(x) - f_i(x))\bigr) + f_0(x)$ for every $x \in \Omega_1$ (`:285–288`).

2. *Case $p = \infty$ (`Complete.lean:294–311`):* For each triple $(N, n, m) \in \mathbb N^3$ satisfying $N \le n$ and $N \le m$, the hypothesis $\operatorname{essSup}_x \lVert f_n(x) - f_m(x)\rVert_e < B_N$ implies that $\Omega_{N,n,m} := \{x \in \alpha : \lVert f_n(x) - f_m(x)\rVert_e < B_N\}$ belongs to $\mathrm{ae}\,\mu$ (`ae_lt_of_essSup_lt`, `EssSup.lean:248–251`). Because $\mathbb N^3$ is countable, three applications of `ae_all_iff` (`:296–298`) intersect all $\Omega_{N,n,m}$ ($N \le n, m$) into **one single common conull set** $\Omega_\infty \in \mathrm{ae}\,\mu$ on which $\forall N \le n, m,\ \lVert f_n(x) - f_m(x)\rVert_e < B_N$. Meanwhile, $\sum_{i=0}^\infty B_i \neq \infty$ implies both $B_N \neq \infty$ for every $N$ (`ENNReal.ne_top_of_tsum_ne_top`) and $B_N \to 0$ in $\mathbb R_{\ge 0\infty}$ (`ENNReal.tendsto_atTop_zero_of_tsum_ne_top`), whence $b_N := (B_N).\mathrm{toReal} \to 0$ in $\mathbb R$ (`:308–311`). At every $x \in \Omega_\infty$ and $N \le n, m$, `ENNReal.ofReal_le_iff_le_toReal` converts $\lVert f_n(x) - f_m(x)\rVert_e \le B_N < \infty$ into $\operatorname{dist}(f_n(x), f_m(x)) = \lVert f_n(x) - f_m(x)\rVert_E \le b_N$ (`:302–307`). Hence $(f_n(x))_{n \in \mathbb N}$ is Cauchy in $E$ (`cauchySeq_of_le_tendsto_0`, `MetricSpace/Cauchy.lean:102–105`), and **`[CompleteSpace E]`** supplies $l \in E$ with $f_n(x) \to l$ (`cauchySeq_tendsto_of_complete`, `:300`).

3. *Constructing a globally `StronglyMeasurable` limit (`exists_stronglyMeasurable_limit_of_tendsto_ae`, `AEStronglyMeasurable.lean:711–724`, `Metrizable.lean:106–125`):* Equip $E$ with its Borel $\sigma$-algebra (`borelize E`; every normed group is `PseudoMetrizableSpace`). Parts (1) and (2) yield $\forall^{\mathrm{ae}} x\ \partial\mu,\ \exists l \in E,\ f_n(x) \to l$. First, `measurable_limit_of_tendsto_metrizable_ae` (`Metrizable.lean:106–125`) defines a raw pointwise function $g_0 : \alpha \to E$ by setting $g_0(x) := h.\mathrm{choose}$ if $h : \exists l,\ f_n(x) \to l$ holds and $f_0(x)$ otherwise; then $f_n(x) \to g_0(x)$ $\mu$-a.e., so Theorem V of §2.3.2.8 (`aemeasurable_of_tendsto_metrizable_ae`, which patches measurable representatives of all $f_n$ and $g_0$ outside a single measurable null hull `aeSeqSetᶜ`) proves `AEMeasurable g_0 μ`, and $g := h_{g_0}.\mathrm{mk}\,g_0 : \alpha \to E$ is Borel `Measurable` with $f_n \to g$ $\mu$-a.e. (`:114–124`). Second, Lemma W of §2.3.2.8 (`aestronglyMeasurable_of_tendsto_ae`, `AEStronglyMeasurable.lean:693–708`) extracts for each $n$ a **separable** set $t_n \subseteq E$ with $f_n(x) \in t_n$ $\mu$-a.e.; on the countable intersection of those conull sets with $\{x : f_n(x) \to g(x)\}$, $g(x)$ lies in the closed set $\overline{\bigcup_{n \in \mathbb N} t_n}$, which is separable (`IsSeparable.iUnion` + `.closure`, requiring **no** separability of $E$ itself!), proving `Hg : AEStronglyMeasurable g μ` (`:720`). Third, setting $f_{\lim} := Hg.\mathrm{mk}\,g : \alpha \to E$ yields `StronglyMeasurable f_lim` (`Hg.stronglyMeasurable_mk`), and intersecting $\{x : f_n(x) \to g(x)\}$ with $\{x : g(x) = f_{\lim}(x)\}$ (`Hg.ae_eq_mk`) produces a single common conull set $\Omega \in \mathrm{ae}\,\mu$ on which $f_n(x) \to f_{\lim}(x)$ (`:721–723`). $\square$

*Proof of Theorem 2.3.2.7h.*

1. *$L^p$ convergence and `MemLp` (`Complete.lean:321–376`):* To prove `cauchy_tendsto_of_tendsto` (`:321–343`), use the order characterization `ENNReal.tendsto_atTop_zero`: let $\varepsilon > 0$ in $\mathbb R_{\ge 0\infty}$. Since $\sum_{i=0}^\infty B_i \neq \infty$ implies $B_k \to 0$ (`ENNReal.tendsto_atTop_zero_of_tsum_ne_top`), there exists $N \in \mathbb N$ with $B_N \le \varepsilon$ (`:328–332`). Fix any $n \ge N$. On the common conull set $\Omega$ of Lemma 2.3.2.7g(3), as $m \to \infty$ we have $f_n(x) - f_m(x) = f_n(x) + (-f_m(x)) \to f_n(x) - f_{\lim}(x)$ (`Tendsto.add tendsto_const_nhds (Tendsto.neg hx)`), and each $f_n - f_m$ is `AEStronglyMeasurable` (`(hf n).sub (hf m)`). Applying Lemma 2.3.2.7f(3) (`eLpNorm_lim_le_liminf_eLpNorm`) to the sequence $m \mapsto f_n - f_m$ gives
   $$\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_n - f_{\lim}, p, \mu) \le \liminf_{m \to \infty} \operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_n - f_m, p, \mu).$$
   For every threshold $N_1 \in \mathbb N$, taking $m := \max(N, N_1) \ge N_1$ satisfies $N \le n$ and $N \le m$, so $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_n - f_m, p, \mu) < B_N \le \varepsilon$; thus $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_n - f_m, p, \mu) \le \varepsilon$ holds frequently (indeed eventually for $m \ge N$), whence `liminf_le_of_frequently_le'` gives $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_n - f_{\lim}, p, \mu) \le \varepsilon$ for all $n \ge N$ (`:339–342`), proving $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_n - f_{\lim}, p, \mu) \to 0$.

   Next, for `memLp_of_cauchy_tendsto` (`:344–360`), `f_lim` is already `StronglyMeasurable` (hence `AEStronglyMeasurable f_lim μ`). Specializing $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_n - f_{\lim}, p, \mu) \to 0$ at $\varepsilon = 1 > 0$ gives $N \in \mathbb N$ with $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_N - f_{\lim}, p, \mu) \le 1 < \infty$ (`:349–350`). Writing $f_{\lim} = (f_{\lim} - f_N) + f_N$, using $f_{\lim} - f_N = -(f_N - f_{\lim})$ (`eLpNorm_neg`), and applying Minkowski's inequality `eLpNorm_add_le` (`1 ≤ p`, `:353–359`) gives
   $$\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_{\lim}, p, \mu) \le \operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_N - f_{\lim}, p, \mu) + \operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_N, p, \mu) \le 1 + \operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_N, p, \mu) < \infty$$
   since `hf N : MemLp (f N) p μ` supplies $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_N, p, \mu) < \infty$. Together with Lemma 2.3.2.7g, this proves `cauchy_complete_eLpNorm` (`:361–375`).

2. *Extracting a geometric subsequence and returning to the original Cauchy sequence / filter (`Complete.lean:156–190, 378–380`, `MetricSpace/Cauchy.lean:41–47`, `UniformSpace/Cauchy.lean:838–914`):*
   - *Sequential proof on `Lp E p μ`:* Let $(F_k)_{k \in \mathbb N}$ be any Cauchy sequence in `Lp E p μ`. For each $n \in \mathbb N$, the Cauchy condition at $2^{-n} > 0$ furnishes $K_n \in \mathbb N$ such that $\lVert F_m - F_r\rVert_{L^p} < 2^{-n}$ for all $m, r \ge K_n$. Define $k_0 := K_0$ and $k_{n+1} := \max(k_n + 1, K_{n+1})$ (`Metric.exists_subseq_bounded_of_cauchySeq`, `MetricSpace/Cauchy.lean:152–160`). Then $k_0 < k_1 < k_2 < \cdots$ is strictly increasing, and whenever $N \le n, m$, we have $k_n, k_m \ge k_N \ge K_N$, so $\operatorname{dist}(F_{k_n}, F_{k_m}) = (\operatorname{eLpNorm}(\Uparrow F_{k_n} - \Uparrow F_{k_m}, p, \mu)).\mathrm{toReal} < 2^{-N}$. Because $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(\Uparrow F_{k_n} - \Uparrow F_{k_m}, p, \mu) \neq \infty$ (`Lp.eLpNorm_ne_top`), `ENNReal.lt_ofReal_iff_toReal_lt` (`Complete.lean:187–189`) converts this to $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(\Uparrow F_{k_n} - \Uparrow F_{k_m}, p, \mu) < B_{1,N} := \operatorname{ofReal}(2^{-N})$, where $\sum_{N=0}^\infty B_{1,N} = \operatorname{ofReal}(2) < \infty$ (`summable_geometric_two` + `ENNReal.ofReal_tsum_of_nonneg`, `:170–181`). Applying part (1) to $f_n := \Uparrow F_{k_n}$ (`Lp.memLp`) produces $f_{\lim} : \alpha \to E$ with `hf_lim : MemLp f_lim p μ` and $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(\Uparrow F_{k_n} - f_{\lim}, p, \mu) \to 0$, i.e., $F_{k_n} \to [f_{\lim}] := hf_{\lim}.\mathrm{toLp}\,f_{\lim}$ in `Lp E p μ` (`tendsto_Lp_of_tendsto_eLpNorm`, `:169`). **To return from $(F_{k_n})$ to the full Cauchy sequence $(F_k)$**, let $\varepsilon > 0$ be given. Choose $N_1$ so that $\lVert F_k - F_r\rVert_{L^p} < \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ for all $k, r \ge N_1$, and choose $n \ge 0$ large enough that both $k_n \ge N_1$ (possible since $k_n \ge n$) and $\lVert F_{k_n} - [f_{\lim}]\rVert_{L^p} < \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$. Then for **every** $k \ge N_1$,
     $$\lVert F_k - [f_{\lim}]\rVert_{L^p} \le \lVert F_k - F_{k_n}\rVert_{L^p} + \lVert F_{k_n} - [f_{\lim}]\rVert_{L^p} < \frac{\varepsilon}{2} + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \varepsilon,$$
     so $F_k \to [f_{\lim}]$ in `Lp E p μ`.
   - *Filter production proof in Mathlib (`UniformSpace/Cauchy.lean:838–914`):* `completeSpace_lp_of_cauchy_complete_eLpNorm` invokes `Metric.complete_of_convergent_controlled_sequences` with $B_n = (1/2)^n$, which delegates to `UniformSpace.complete_of_convergent_controlled_sequences`. Given an arbitrary Cauchy filter $\mathcal F$ on $\mathrm{Lp}\,E\,p\,\mu$, let $U_n := \{(x, y) : \operatorname{dist}(x, y) < 2^{-n}\} \in \mathcal U_{\mathrm{Lp}}$ and let $(U'_n)$ be an antitone countable basis of $\mathcal U_{\mathrm{Lp}}$ (`exists_antitone_seq`). For each $m$, the Cauchy property of $\mathcal F$ selects $s'_m \in \mathcal F$ with $s'_m \times s'_m \subseteq U_m \cap U'_m$ (`setSeqAux`, `:839–840`). Setting $s_n := \bigcap_{m \le n} s'_m \in \mathcal F$ (`setSeq`, `:844–848`) produces an antitone chain in $\mathcal F$ satisfying $s_m \times s_n \subseteq s_N \times s_N \subseteq U_N \cap U'_N$ for all $N \le m, n$ (`setSeq_prod_subset`, `:856–860`). Since $\mathcal F$ is `NeBot`, every $s_n$ is nonempty; choosing $u_n \in s_n$ (`SequentiallyComplete.seq`, `:865–869`) yields a $2^{-N}$-controlled sequence $(u_n)$ (`seq_pair_mem`, `:871–873`), which converges to $[f_{\lim}]$ by part (1). Finally, `le_nhds_of_seq_tendsto_nhds` (`:879–890`) returns from $u_n \to [f_{\lim}]$ to $\mathcal F \le \mathcal N([f_{\lim}])$: for any entourage $W \in \mathcal U_{\mathrm{Lp}}$, choose $m$ with $U_m \cap U'_m \subseteq W$ and $n$ such that $\operatorname{dist}([f_{\lim}], u_k) < 2^{-m}$ for all $k \ge n$; at $K := \max(m, n)$, the set $s_K \in \mathcal F$ contains the pivot $u_K$, satisfies $s_K \times s_K \subseteq U_m \cap U'_m \subseteq W$, and has $([f_{\lim}], u_K) \in U_m$, which forces $\mathcal F \le \mathcal N([f_{\lim}])$ (`le_nhds_of_cauchy_adhp_aux`). Plugging `cauchy_complete_eLpNorm` into `completeSpace_lp_of_cauchy_complete_eLpNorm` yields `Lp.instCompleteSpace` (`Complete.lean:378–380`). $\square$

*Proof of Proposition 2.3.2.7i.*

1. *Necessity of `[CompleteSpace E]` (`CompleteOfCompleteLp.lean:23–121`) and concrete $c_{00}$ failure:* First, if `Nontrivial (Lp E p μ)` holds with $1 \le p \le \infty$, pick $f_0 \in \mathrm{Lp}\,E\,p\,\mu$ with $f_0 \neq 0$, i.e. $\neg(\Uparrow f_0 =^{\mathrm{ae}}_\mu 0)$. If $p = \infty$, this forces $\mu \neq 0$, so $[1] \neq 0$ in `Lp ℝ ∞ μ` (`:51–56`). If $1 \le p < \infty$, `MemLp.aefinStronglyMeasurable` expresses $f_0$ as an a.e. limit of simple functions $(s_n)$ of finite-measure support; if every measurable set of positive measure had $\mu$-measure $\infty$, every $\operatorname{supp}(s_n)$ would have measure $0$ and countable intersection `ae_all_iff` would give $f_0 =^{\mathrm{ae}}_\mu 0$, a contradiction (`exists_measurableSet_measure_pos_lt_top`, `:23–42`). Hence there exists $s \in \mathcal M$ with $0 < \mu(s) < \infty$, and `indicatorConstLp p s_meas s_top.ne (1 : ℝ)` has norm $\mu.\mathrm{real}(s)^{1/p.\mathrm{toReal}} > 0$, proving `Nontrivial (Lp ℝ p μ)` (`:64–75`). Now choose $f \in \mathrm{Lp}\,\mathbb R\,p\,\mu$ with $f \neq 0$ (`:87–89`), and consider the continuous $\mathbb R$-linear embedding $m : E \to_L \mathrm{Lp}\,E\,p\,\mu$, $u \mapsto f \cdot u$ (`((lsmul ℝ ℝ).flip.compLpL₂ p μ).flip f`, `:90`, with $\lVert m(u)\rVert_{L^p} = \lVert f\rVert_{L^p}\,\lVert u\rVert_E$). Given any Cauchy sequence $(u_n)$ in $E$, $(m(u_n))$ is Cauchy in `Lp E p μ`, hence converges by `[CompleteSpace (Lp E p μ)]` to some $g \in \mathrm{Lp}\,E\,p\,\mu$ (`:92–93`). Because $L^p$ convergence implies convergence in measure, `(tendstoInMeasure_of_tendsto_Lp hg).exists_seq_tendsto_ae` (`:95–97`) extracts $n_0 < n_1 < \cdots$ with $(m(u_{n_i}))(x) \to g(x)$ $\mu$-a.e. Since $f \neq 0$ in $L^p$, $\mu(\operatorname{supp}(f)) > 0$, so `ae (μ.restrict (support f))` is `NeBot` (`:98–104`). Intersecting a.e. convergence, membership in $\operatorname{supp}(f)$, and the countable family $\forall n,\ (m(u_n))(x) = f(x) u_n$ (`ae_all_iff`, `:109–114`) yields a point $x_0$ with $f(x_0) \neq 0$ and $f(x_0) u_{n_i} \to g(x_0)$ in $E$. Scaling by $f(x_0)^{-1} \in \mathbb R$ gives $u_{n_i} \to f(x_0)^{-1} g(x_0)$, and returning from the subsequence $(u_{n_i})$ to the Cauchy sequence $(u_n)$ (`tendsto_nhds_of_cauchySeq_of_subseq`, `:117`) proves `CompleteSpace E` (`:80–120`).

   For the explicit witness $E = c_{00}(\mathbb N_{\ge 1})$ (with $\lVert\cdot\rVert_\infty$ or $\lVert\cdot\rVert_1$) on $(\mathbb R_{\ge 0}, \mathrm{vol})$ without invoking convergence in measure: for $M > N \ge 1$, $F_M - F_N = \bigl(\sum_{m=N+1}^M 2^{-m} e_m\bigr) 1_{[0, 1]}$ has $\lVert F_M - F_N\rVert_{L^p(\mathbb R_{\ge 0}; c_{00})} = \lVert \sum_{m=N+1}^M 2^{-m} e_m\rVert \cdot \mathrm{vol}([0, 1])^{1/p} \le 2^{-N}$ (with $1/\infty := 0$), so $(F_N)$ is Cauchy in `Lp c₀₀ p vol`. Suppose $F_N \to G$ in `Lp c₀₀ p vol`. Fix any coordinate $k \ge 1$, with $1$-Lipschitz projection $\pi_k : c_{00} \to \mathbb R$. For every $N \ge k$ and $x \in [0, 1]$, $\lvert 2^{-k} - \pi_k(G(x))\rvert = \lvert \pi_k(F_N(x) - G(x))\rvert \le \lVert F_N(x) - G(x)\rVert$, so monotonicity of `eLpNorm` gives $\operatorname{eLpNorm}((2^{-k} - \pi_k \circ G) 1_{[0, 1]}, p, \mathrm{vol}) \le \operatorname{eLpNorm}(F_N - G, p, \mathrm{vol}) \to 0$. Hence $\operatorname{eLpNorm}((2^{-k} - \pi_k \circ G) 1_{[0, 1]}, p, \mathrm{vol}) = 0$, so $\pi_k(G(x)) = 2^{-k} \neq 0$ on a conull subset $A_k \subseteq [0, 1]$. Since $\mathrm{vol}([0, 1]) = 1 > 0$, the countable intersection $\bigcap_{k=1}^\infty A_k$ has $\mathrm{vol}$-measure $1$ and is nonempty; picking $x_0 \in \bigcap_{k=1}^\infty A_k$ gives $(G(x_0))_k = 2^{-k} \neq 0$ for **all** $k \ge 1$, contradicting $G(x_0) \in c_{00}(\mathbb N_{\ge 1})$.

2. *Boundary at $0 < p < 1$ (`MeanInequalitiesPow.lean:315–330`, `TriangleInequality.lean:37–75`) and $p = \frac{1}{2}$ counterexample:* For $0<q\le1$, subadditivity of $t^q$ bounds the power integral of the sum by the sum of the two power integrals. Convexity of $t^{1/q}$ then bounds the rooted size by the sum of the individual sizes times $2^{1/q-1}$, strictly greater than one if $q<1$. These are the two steps in [§2.3.2.7.3](#integral-minkowski-supply). On disjoint measurable sets $A, B$ with $\mu(A) = \mu(B) = m \in (0, \infty)$, $\operatorname{eLpNorm}'(1_A + 1_B, q, \mu) = (2m)^{1/q} = 2^{1/q - 1}(m^{1/q} + m^{1/q}) = 2^{1/q - 1}\bigl(\operatorname{eLpNorm}'(1_A, q, \mu) + \operatorname{eLpNorm}'(1_B, q, \mu)\bigr)$, proving sharpness and falsifying subadditivity at constant $1$.

   At $q = \frac{1}{2}$ on $([0, 1), \mathrm{vol})$, each $E_k = I_{r, j}$ ($k = 2^r + j$, $0 \le j < 2^r$) has $\mathrm{vol}(E_k) = 2^{-r}$, so $\lvert h_k - h_{k-1}\rvert = 1_{E_k}$ has $\operatorname{eLpNorm}'(h_k - h_{k-1}, \tfrac{1}{2}, \mathrm{vol}) = \bigl(\int_0^1 1_{E_k}^{1/2}\,dx\bigr)^2 = (2^{-r})^2 = 4^{-r}$. Summing over $2^r$ intervals in sweep $r$ gives $\sum_{k=1}^\infty \operatorname{eLpNorm}'(h_k - h_{k-1}, \tfrac{1}{2}, \mathrm{vol}) = \sum_{r=0}^\infty 2^r \cdot 4^{-r} = \sum_{r=0}^\infty 2^{-r} = 2 < \infty$. Yet for every $x \in [0, 1)$ and every sweep $s \in \{0, \dots, R\}$, $x$ belongs to **exactly one** dyadic interval $I_{s, j}$ of sweep $s$:
   - thus $\sum_{k=1}^{2^{R+1}-1} \lvert h_k(x) - h_{k-1}(x)\rvert = R + 1$ identically on $[0, 1)$, so $\operatorname{eLpNorm}'\bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{2^{R+1}-1} \lvert h_k - h_{k-1}\rvert, \tfrac{1}{2}, \mathrm{vol}\bigr) = \bigl(\int_0^1 (R + 1)^{1/2}\,dx\bigr)^2 = R + 1 \to +\infty$ (already $3 > 2$ at $R = 2$, $K_2 = 7$), refuting `Complete.lean:196` for $q = \frac{1}{2}$;
   - at every $x \in [0, 1)$, $\lvert h_k(x) - h_{k-1}(x)\rvert = 1$ holds once in every sweep $r \ge 0$ (infinitely often), so $(h_k(x))_{k \ge 0}$ diverges at **every** $x \in [0, 1)$;
   - across each complete $r$-th sweep, $h_{2^{r+1}-1}(x) - h_{2^r - 1}(x) = \sum_{j=0}^{2^r - 1} (-1)^{2^r + j} 1_{I_{r, j}}(x)$ has modulus $1$ everywhere on $[0, 1)$ by disjointness of $\{I_{r, j}\}_{j=0}^{2^r - 1}$, so $\operatorname{eLpNorm}'(h_{2^{r+1}-1} - h_{2^r - 1}, \tfrac{1}{2}, \mathrm{vol}) = 1 \not\to 0$ even though $\sum_{k=2^r}^{2^{r+1}-1} \operatorname{eLpNorm}'(h_k - h_{k-1}, \tfrac{1}{2}, \mathrm{vol}) = 2^{-r} \to 0$.

   Finally, for any $0 < q < 1$ and $a, b \ge 0$, $(a + b)^q \le a^q + b^q$ (`ENNReal.rpow_add_le_add_rpow` since $a/(a+b), b/(a+b) \in [0, 1]$ satisfy $t^q \ge t$ on $[0, 1]$), so $d_q(f, g) := \int \lVert f - g\rVert^q\,d\mu = \operatorname{eLpNorm}'(f - g, q, \mu)^q$ satisfies the genuine triangle inequality $d_q(f, h) \le d_q(f, g) + d_q(g, h)$ (and defines the same topology on $L^q$, since $t \mapsto t^q$ is a homeomorphism of $[0, \infty)$). If $(F_k)$ is $d_q$-Cauchy, extracting $k_n$ with $d_q(F_m, F_r) < 2^{-n}$ ($m, r \ge k_n$) makes $\sum_{n=0}^\infty d_q(F_{k_{n+1}}, F_{k_n}) < 2$ (whereas for $h_k$ above, $\sum_{k=1}^{2^{R+1}-1} d_{1/2}(h_k, h_{k-1}) = \sum_{r=0}^R 2^r \cdot 2^{-r} = R + 1 \to +\infty$); subadditivity $(a + b)^q \le a^q + b^q$ plus Fatou's lemma then bounds $\int \bigl(\sum_{n=0}^\infty \lVert F_{k_{n+1}} - F_{k_n}\rVert\bigr)^q d\mu \le \sum_{n=0}^\infty d_q(F_{k_{n+1}}, F_{k_n}) < \infty$ without Minkowski's inequality. On the resulting conull set, finiteness of the series of norms gives a pointwise limit by completeness of $E$. The measurable patch in Lemma 2.3.2.7g supplies an a.e. strongly measurable limit. Lemma 2.3.2.7f(1) passes the $d_q$ tail bounds to that limit. The $d_q$ triangle inequality gives finite distance from zero and returns from the subsequence to the full Cauchy sequence. $\square$

**Consumer Contract Table.** The table below maps the direct Mathlib and archive A2 consumers of `Lp.instCompleteSpace` and the companion exports of `Complete.lean`, recording the exponent $p$, the measure and codomain assumptions, and the exact mechanism of consumption.

| Consumer declaration & pinned location | Exponent $p$ & measure | Codomain $E$ & completeness supply | Consumed `Complete.lean` exports | Downstream mathematical role | Tag |

**Remarks (why pointwise convergence never replaces $L^p$ convergence, why no global measure/separability assumptions appear, and classical origins).**

1. *Why pointwise $\mu$-a.e. convergence never substitutes for $L^p$ convergence, and why `Complete.lean` avoids DCT at the final step.* [Expository] Neither implication between $\mu$-a.e. pointwise convergence and $L^p$ convergence holds unconditionally:
   - *Pointwise everywhere convergence without $L^p$ convergence:* On $(\mathbb R_{\ge 0}, \mathrm{vol})$, the sliding blocks $u_n := 1_{[n, n+1]}$ satisfy $u_n(x) \to 0$ at **every** $x \in \mathbb R_{\ge 0}$, yet $\lVert [u_n] - 0\rVert_{L^p} = 1$ for every $1 \le p \le \infty$; on $([0, 1], \mathrm{vol})$, the concentration spikes $v_n := n^{1/p}\,1_{(0, 1/n)}$ ($1 \le p < \infty$) satisfy $v_n(x) \to 0$ at **every** $x \in [0, 1]$, yet $\lVert [v_n] - 0\rVert_{L^p} = 1$. Thus Lemma 2.3.2.7g ($f_n \to f_{\lim}$ $\mu$-a.e.) does *not* by itself prove $F_n \to [f_{\lim}]$ in $L^p$.
   - *Why `cauchy_tendsto_of_tendsto` uses `eLpNorm_lim_le_liminf_eLpNorm` rather than DCT:* For $1 \le p < \infty$, one *could* deduce $\operatorname{eLpNorm}'(f_n - f_{\lim}, p.\mathrm{toReal}, \mu) \to 0$ on the controlled subsequence from Lebesgue's dominated convergence theorem (§2.3.2.7) using the $L^p$-integrable envelope $M(x) := \lVert f_0(x)\rVert + \sum_{i=0}^\infty \lVert f_{i+1}(x) - f_i(x)\rVert$ (since $\lVert f_n(x) - f_{\lim}(x)\rVert^p \le (2 M(x))^p$). At $p = \infty$, however, dominated convergence has **no** counterpart: $v_n(x) := x^n$ on $([0, 1), \mathrm{vol})$ is uniformly bounded by the $L^\infty$-majorant $1$ and converges everywhere on $[0, 1)$ to $0$, yet $\lVert [v_n] - 0\rVert_{L^\infty([0,1))} = 1 \not\to 0$! The production lemma `cauchy_tendsto_of_tendsto` (`Complete.lean:321–343`) avoids DCT altogether: passing $m \to \infty$ inside $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_n - f_m, p, \mu) < B_N$ via Lemma 2.3.2.7f transfers the **two-index Cauchy tail** $B_N$ directly to $\operatorname{eLpNorm}(f_n - f_{\lim}, p, \mu) \le B_N \to 0$, handling $1 \le p < \infty$ and $p = \infty$ by a single ten-line proof. Conversely, $L^p$ convergence does not imply full-sequence a.e. convergence either (taking $w_k := 1_{E_k}$ along the dyadic sweeps $E_k = I_{r, j}$ of Proposition 2.3.2.7i(2) gives $\lVert [w_k]\rVert_{L^p([0,1))} = 2^{-r/p} \to 0$ for all $1 \le p < \infty$ while $(w_k(x))$ oscillates between $0$ and $1$ infinitely often at every $x \in [0, 1)$ — as Riesz himself already noted in 1910, §6, p. 464); only the *thinned subsequence* $(F_{k_n})$ converges $\mu$-a.e., whereas the full Cauchy sequence converges in $L^p$ norm.

2. *Where global finiteness/$\sigma$-finiteness of $\mu$ and global separability of $E$ are avoided.* [Expository] Three potential traps would force extraneous global hypotheses if handled carelessly:
   - *No Hölder reduction $L^p \hookrightarrow L^1$:* On an infinite measure space such as $((0, \infty), \mathrm{vol})$ in Hand calculation 1, $L^p(\mu)$ does not embed in $L^1(\mu)$ (e.g. $(x + 1)^{-1} \in L^2((0, \infty)) \setminus L^1((0, \infty))$). `Complete.lean:212–267` works directly with the $q$-th power $\bigl(\sum_{i=0}^\infty \lVert f_{i+1}(a) - f_i(a)\rVert_e\bigr)^q$ and `ae_lt_top'`, which needs only $\int^- H\,d\mu \neq \infty$ (`Markov.lean:148–152`) without $\sigma$-finiteness. Notice also that in `CondexpL1.lean:321`, `[SigmaFinite (μ.trim hm)]` is required by Radon–Nikodym/projection uniqueness of conditional expectation on the *sub-$\sigma$-algebra* $m$, not by `Lp.instCompleteSpace`.
   - *No completeness of $\mu$:* Even when $\mu$ is incomplete (so subsets of null sets need not be measurable), `exists_stronglyMeasurable_limit_of_tendsto_ae` (`AEStronglyMeasurable.lean:711–724`) patches on the *measurable* common conull set `aeSeqSet` (§2.3.2.8) to produce a genuinely `StronglyMeasurable` representative $f_{\lim}$ everywhere on $\alpha$.
   - *No global `[SeparableSpace E]`:* As Hand calculation 2 illustrates with $E = \ell^\infty(\mathbb N_{\ge 1})$, Bochner `AEStronglyMeasurable` already bakes *per-function* a.e.-separable range into `MemLp (f n) p μ`. Countable union and closure $\overline{\bigcup_{n \in \mathbb N} t_n}$ (`AEStronglyMeasurable.lean:704`) manufacture a separable closed subspace tailored to the single Cauchy sequence $(f_n)$, leaving arbitrary non-separable flanks of $E$ untouched.

3. *Classical origins.* [Expository] On p. 450 of his 1910 memoir, Riesz cites his March 1907 note on orthogonal systems, Fischer’s May 1907 note on convergence in quadratic mean, and his own May 1909 note on sequences of measurable functions. The 1907 notes concern the square-integrable case. The original pages of these three notes remain [Open] in this reading. In §6, p. 464 of the memoir Riesz defines strong convergence. In §7, p. 468, (18), he extends Fischer’s completeness theorem to $p>1$ on an interval, using weak subsequence compactness and lower semicontinuity. The footnote on p. 468 points to the 1909 method for $0<p\le1$, and p. 497 discusses the passage to general Euclidean measurable sets. The integral triangle inequality of §2 is discussed in [§2.3.2.7.3](#integral-minkowski-supply). Rudin’s Theorem 3.11 gives the scalar geometric-subsequence proof for general positive measures, with a separate essential-supremum argument at infinity. [Riesz 1910](https://real.mtak.hu/214052/1/Riesz_14_Math_Annalen_1910_449-497.pdf) · [Bibliography](../sources/SOURCES.md#ch02-lp-completeness-sources)

<a id="integral-minkowski-supply"></a>

##### 2.3.2.7.3 The integral Minkowski inequality used by Riesz–Fischer

**Motivation.** A geometric Cauchy subsequence bounds each successive difference in $L^p$. To control the partial sums of their pointwise magnitudes, [Riesz–Fischer](#lp-riesz-fischer-completeness) needs a triangle inequality whose constant remains $1$ at each addition. The production proof uses a coarse power bound to establish finiteness and then uses Hölder to obtain the exact constant. At infinity the argument uses almost-everywhere upper bounds. [Expository]

**Examples.** Use either Lebesgue measure on $\mathbb R$ with $A=[0,1)$ and $B=[1,2)$ or counting measure on $\mathbb N$ with $A=\{0\}$ and $B=\{1\}$. Both cells have measure one.

1. For disjoint supports take $u=a1_A,v=b1_B$ with $a,b>0$. The individual $p$th power integrals are $a^p,b^p$, and the sum has integral $a^p+b^p$. The three rooted sizes are $a,b,(a^p+b^p)^{1/p}$ and the essential suprema are $a,b,\max(a,b)$. With $a=b=1$, the proposed ordinary triangle bound becomes $2^{1/p}\le2$. This is strict for $p>1$, an equality at $p=1$, and false for every $0<p<1$ in both measure spaces. At $p=1/2$ the three sizes are $1,1,4$, and the powered bound reads $2=1+1$. The same pair attains the sharp factor $2^{1/p-1}$.
2. For overlapping supports put $u=1_A+t1_B,v=t1_A+1_B$ with $0<t\le1$. Each individual $p$th power integral is $1+t^p$. The sum is $1+t$ on both cells, giving integral $2(1+t)^p$ and rooted size $2^{1/p}(1+t)$. The individual rooted sizes are both $(1+t^p)^{1/p}$ and the essential suprema are $1,1,1+t$. At $p=2,t=1/4$ the squares of the sum size and the sum of the individual sizes are $25/8$ and $17/4$. At $p=1/2,t=1/4$ the ordinary comparison fails by $5>9/2$, while the powered comparison is $\sqrt5<3$. For $0<t<1$ strict convexity of $x^p$ at $1,t$ gives the ordinary strict inequality for $p>1$, and strict concavity reverses it for $0<p<1$. At $t=1$ the two functions coincide and the ordinary comparison is an equality at every positive exponent including infinity. At $p=1$ it is an equality for every $t$. [Expository]

**Definitions.** Let $\mu$ be any measure on a measurable space $X$. For $u\colon X\to[0,\infty]$ and real $p>0$, put $I_p(u)=\int_X u^p\,d\mu$ and $N_p(u)=I_p(u)^{1/p}$. These are extended nonnegative values, and the integral is the lower integral, defined even for arbitrary functions by measurable simple minorants. Put $N_\infty(u)=\operatorname{ess\,sup}_\mu u$, the least almost-everywhere upper bound in $[0,\infty]$. Almost-everywhere measurability means agreement almost everywhere with a measurable representative. For normed additive group values set $\|f\|_{p,\mu}=N_p(\|f(\cdot)\|)$. Almost-everywhere strong measurability implies almost-everywhere measurability of the norm. We use $0\cdot\infty=0$. A positive power fixes zero and infinity and is an order isomorphism with inverse the reciprocal power. [Mathlib, LpSeminorm/Defs.lean:69–147](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSeminorm/Defs.lean#L69-L147)

**Lemma (scalar powers).** For $a,b\in[0,\infty]$,
$$
(a+b)^r\le 2^{r-1}(a^r+b^r)\quad(r\ge1),
\qquad (a+b)^r\le a^r+b^r\quad(0<r\le1).
$$
[Lean, read]

*Proof.* In the first inequality use convexity of $x^r$ with weights $1/2,1/2$ and arguments $2a,2b$. The source starts with finite weighted Jensen on nonnegative reals. In the extended version an infinite weighted sum forces an infinite weighted sum of powers, since a positive weight preserves an infinite entry and a zero weight kills either entry. When the sums are finite every weighted term can be transported to nonnegative reals. For the two positive weights this gives, in the finite case, $((2a+2b)/2)^r\le((2a)^r+(2b)^r)/2$. If either argument is infinite both sides are infinite.

For the second inequality first prove $x^s+y^s\le(x+y)^s$ for $s\ge1$. For finite $x,y\ge0$ with $x+y>0$, normalize by $x+y$. On $[0,1]$ one has $z^s\le z$, so the two normalized powers sum to at most one. Multiply back by $(x+y)^s$. A zero sum gives equality, and an infinite sum gives an infinite right side. Thus $(x^s+y^s)^{1/s}\le x+y$. For $q\ge r>0$ apply this to $x=a^r,y=b^r,s=q/r$ and take the increasing power $1/r$. This is the intermediate comparison
$$
(a^q+b^q)^{1/q}\le(a^r+b^r)^{1/r}.
$$
Put $q=1$ and raise to $r$. The scalar Lean statement also includes $r=0$, when all zeroth powers equal $1$, even at zero and infinity. $\square$ [Mathlib, MeanInequalitiesPow.lean:73–76,103–107,137–187,221–319](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/MeanInequalitiesPow.lean#L221-L319)

**Theorem (two terms).** For almost-everywhere measurable $u,v\colon X\to[0,\infty]$ and $1\le p<\infty$,
$$
N_p(u+v)\le N_p(u)+N_p(v).
$$
For $0<p\le1$ it suffices that $u$ be almost-everywhere measurable, with $v$ arbitrary, to have
$$
I_p(u+v)\le I_p(u)+I_p(v),\qquad
N_p(u+v)\le 2^{1/p-1}\bigl(N_p(u)+N_p(v)\bigr).
$$
At infinity arbitrary $u,v$ satisfy $N_\infty(u+v)\le N_\infty(u)+N_\infty(v)$. The corresponding norm inequalities hold for almost-everywhere strongly measurable $f,g$ when $1<p<\infty$, with one almost-everywhere strongly measurable summand when $0<p\le1$, and for arbitrary $f,g$ at infinity. The powered estimate for the norm reads $\|f+g\|_{p,\mu}^p\le\|f\|_{p,\mu}^p+\|g\|_{p,\mu}^p$. [Lean, read]

*Proof for $1\le p<\infty$.* If either individual power integral is infinite, the right side is infinite. For $p=1$ use lower-integral additivity for nonnegative functions. One almost-everywhere measurable summand suffices for additivity, so the scalar equality also holds for arbitrary $v$ when $u$ meets that hypothesis.

For $p>1$ set $h=u+v$, $J=I_p(h)$, $D=N_p(u)+N_p(v)$ and $q=p/(p-1)$. If $J=0$ the inequality holds immediately. With both individual integrals finite, the first scalar bound gives
$$
J\le 2^{p-1}\bigl(I_p(u)+I_p(v)\bigr)<\infty.
$$
This uses lower-integral additivity with a measurable representative of the first summand. The coefficient is finite, so it comes outside both lower integrals. The finiteness helper itself only requires almost-everywhere measurability of $u$.

The pinned [Hölder contract](#integral-holder-normalization) takes real conjugates $p,q>1$ with $1/p+1/q=1$ and two almost-everywhere measurable nonnegative extended functions $U,V$. It gives $\int_X UV\,d\mu\le N_p(U)N_q(V)$. When one size is zero its factor vanishes almost everywhere, so the product integral is zero even if the other size is infinite. If one size is infinite and the other positive the bound is infinite. Both exponents are finite reals. At $p=1$ the preceding additivity branch applies. [Lean] [Mathlib, Integral/MeanInequalities.lean:128–166](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/MeanInequalities.lean#L128-L166)

Apply Hölder to $u$ and $h^{p-1}$, then to $v$ and $h^{p-1}$. The latter power is almost-everywhere measurable and $(h^{p-1})^q=h^p$ since $(p-1)q=p$. Both powers are positive, so the identity includes zero and infinity. The factorization $h^p=h\,h^{p-1}$ follows from adding exponents for finite positive $h$. Both sides vanish at zero and both are infinite at infinity. Distribute the product, then split the lower integral using almost-everywhere measurability of the first product. The result is
$$
J=\int_X u h^{p-1}\,d\mu+\int_X v h^{p-1}\,d\mu\le D J^{1/q}.
$$
The production helper rewrites $J^{1/q}=J J^{-1/p}$, cancels $J$, and multiplies by $J^{1/p}$. Both cancellations use $0<J<\infty$, giving $J^{1/p}\le D$. [Mathlib, Integral/MeanInequalities.lean:263–275,310–402](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/MeanInequalities.lean#L310-L402)

*Proof for $0<p\le1$.* Take lower integrals of the pointwise subadditivity bound and split the right side using almost-everywhere measurability of $u^p$. This gives the powered estimate. Take the increasing power $1/p$ and apply the first scalar bound with exponent $1/p$ to the two individual power integrals. This gives the factor $2^{1/p-1}$ with no division by an integral. The raw Lean theorem also handles $p=0$: $1/0=0$, each rooted expression is $1$, and the right side is $2^{-1}(1+1)=1$. The public extended seminorm has a separate zero-exponent definition, identically zero. [Mathlib, Integral/MeanInequalities.lean:407–422](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/MeanInequalities.lean#L407-L422)

*Proof at infinity and passage to norms.* Each function is bounded by its essential supremum almost everywhere. Intersect the two conull conditions, add the bounds and take the least almost-everywhere upper bound. A zero bound means vanishing almost everywhere, and an infinite right-hand bound gives the inequality immediately. The pinned essential-supremum lemma invokes addition of upper limits on the almost-everywhere filter. For normed-group functions first use $\|f(x)+g(x)\|\le\|f(x)\|+\|g(x)\|$. Monotonicity of positive powers and lower integrals gives the finite cases. Almost-everywhere monotonicity gives the infinite case. The public two-term lemma takes both almost-everywhere strong measurability hypotheses and dispatches at infinity to a lemma needing neither. Its finite positive branch invokes the raw finite-power theorem, and the separate low-exponent wrapper requires only one almost-everywhere strongly measurable function. The wrappers also support extended seminorms. $\square$ [Mathlib, EssSup.lean:348–362](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/EssSup.lean#L348-L362) · [TriangleInequality.lean:25–75](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSeminorm/TriangleInequality.lean#L25-L75)

**Remarks on equality and endpoints.** The scalar finite-size criteria below assume both inputs are almost-everywhere measurable. [Expository]

| Range or case | Equality and applicability |
|---|---|
| $1<p<\infty$, positive finite scalar sizes | Equality holds exactly when $u/N_p(u)=v/N_p(v)$ almost everywhere. |
| $p=1$ | Scalar additivity includes infinite integrals. For normed-group inputs with finite sizes and measurable norms of both inputs and their sum up to null sets, equality holds exactly when the pointwise norm triangle is an equality almost everywhere. |
| $0<p<1$, finite scalar integrals | The powered estimate is an equality exactly when almost everywhere at least one entry is zero. Equality in the sharp rooted estimate additionally requires equal individual integrals. |
| $p=\infty$, finite scalar bounds $S,T$ and nonzero measure | Equality holds exactly when for every $\varepsilon>0$ the intersection of $u>S-\varepsilon$ and $v>T-\varepsilon$ is non-null. For arbitrary functions, non-null means there is no measurable null cover. Negative thresholds impose no restriction. For the zero measure every size is zero. |
| Degenerate scalar sizes | A zero size for an almost-everywhere measurable input gives equality in each applicable ordinary or powered estimate. In the sharp rooted estimate for $0<p<1$ it gives strictness if the other size is positive and finite. An infinite scalar size gives equality in the extended order by monotonicity, including at infinity. Normed-group inputs can cancel even with infinite sizes. |
| $p=0$ and real conjugacy | The raw zero-exponent formula is a totalized identity. The public extended seminorm at zero is identically zero. The real conjugate exponents of the Hölder input must both exceed one. The endpoint product bound follows from an essential-supremum bound on one factor. |

For $1<p<\infty$ let $a=N_p(u)$ and $b=N_p(v)$ be positive and finite. The normalized sum $(u+v)/(a+b)$ is the weighted average of $u/a,v/b$ with positive weights $a/(a+b),b/(a+b)$. Its $p$th power is bounded by the weighted average of the normalized powers, whose integral is one. By strict convexity the gap vanishes exactly where the normalized inputs agree on the conull set of finite values. The integral of that nonnegative gap is zero exactly when it vanishes almost everywhere. The same zero-gap argument at $p=1$ gives the normed-group test in the table. At $0<p<1$ the scalar powered inequality is strict when both finite entries are positive, since normalization by their sum gives $z^p>z$ for $0<z<1$. The rooted step is equality exactly when the two power integrals agree, by strict convexity at exponent $1/p$. At infinity, intersect the two individual upper bounds. If the essential supremum of the sum is $S+T$, the sum is within $\varepsilon$ of $S+T$ on a non-null set, so both of the nonnegative deficits $S-u,T-v$ are less than $\varepsilon$ there. Conversely simultaneous deficits below $\varepsilon$ put the sum within $2\varepsilon$ of $S+T$. For vector-valued functions the pointwise norm triangle brings in the geometry of the target norm. The classification here concerns the scalar inequalities and the stated $p=1$ pointwise criterion.

**Why the hypotheses matter.** The disjoint-support example drops $p\ge1$ with strict failure even for simple functions. To test measurability give $\{0,1\}$ the indiscrete sigma algebra and total mass one. The two singleton indicators have only the zero measurable nonnegative simple minorant. Each lower integral of a positive power is zero, whereas the sum is the constant one with integral one. Dropping measurability of both summands breaks even the powered estimate. [Expository]

**Consumer contract.** The controlled-sequence reduction sets $B_n=2^{-n}$ and assumes $\|f_n-f_m\|_{p,\mu}<B_N$ for $n,m\ge N$. A suitable geometric Cauchy subsequence has these tail bounds. For finite $p\ge1$, take $d_i(x)=\|f_{i+1}(x)-f_i(x)\|$. The source invokes the finite-sum lemma `eLpNorm'_sum_le`, which iterates the two-term `eLpNorm'_add_le` on the almost-everywhere strongly measurable norm functions. With $(n,m)=(i+1,i)$ at $N=i$ it gives
$$
\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{n}d_i\right\|_{p,\mu}
\le \sum_{i=0}^{n}\|f_{i+1}-f_i\|_{p,\mu}
\le \sum_{i=0}^{\infty} B_i=2.
$$
The next helper raises the bound to $p$ before the infinite-series step. At infinity the pointwise construction instead uses the controlled tail bounds on a countable intersection. In the later membership step for every $1\le p\le\infty$, the direct two-term call `eLpNorm_add_le` bounds $\|f_{\rm lim}\|_{p,\mu}$ by the sizes of $f_{\rm lim}-f_N$ and $f_N$. The first is finite by norm convergence for sufficiently large $N$, and the second is finite by membership. [Lean, read] [Mathlib, Complete.lean:157–219](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSpace/Complete.lean#L157-L219) · [Infinity branch, lines 291–320](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSpace/Complete.lean#L291-L320) · [Membership, lines 343–359](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSpace/Complete.lean#L343-L359)

**Source note.** Local R3 covers the integral Minkowski proof and the direct scalar power supply. Hölder enters by contract. Its underlying Young input for the same conjugates and $a,b\in[0,\infty]$ is $ab\le a^p/p+b^q/q$. [Mathlib, Analysis/MeanInequalities.lean:550–565](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/MeanInequalities.lean#L550-L565)

On p. 455 of his 1910 memoir, Riesz attributes the finite-sum triangle inequality (2) to Minkowski and cites printed p. 95 of the 1907 *Diophantische Approximationen*. That page in §15 treats a three-dimensional lattice argument and does not contain (2). The original location of the finite-sum inequality remains [Open]. On p. 456, Riesz derives the integral inequality (6) from the discrete inequality when the right-hand integrals exist. See the historical note to [§2.3.2.7.4](#integral-holder-normalization). The proof and its direct scalar supply here are author-level local R3 [Lean, read]. The same-source preliminary review affirmed the mathematical dependencies and requested this correction to the historical note. <a id="integral-holder-normalization"></a>

##### 2.3.2.7.4 Integral Hölder: normalize, integrate Young's inequality, and restore scale

**Motivation.** In the [finite-exponent Minkowski argument](#integral-minkowski-supply) the term to estimate is a product of a summand with a dual power of the sum. In [Chapter 1, §1.2.2.5](01-statement-and-fidelity.md#1225-log-convexity-the-bohrmollerup-theorem-and-the-duplication-formula), a product instead comes from splitting the exponential and power in Euler's integral. In each setting the two factors are measured at different exponents. Young's inequality controls their product additively. Normalizing each power integral to one makes the two Young coefficients add up to one, and restoring the two scales gives the multiplicative bound. [Expository]

**Example A (an atom and an interval).** On the disjoint union of an atom of mass $2$ and $[0,1)$ with Lebesgue measure, use the three cells given by the atom and the two half intervals. Their masses are $2,1/2,1/2$. Take $p=3/2$, $q=3$, and take the values of $f$ and $g$ to be $(1,4,9)$ and $(2,4,6)$ on those cells. Both functions are nonconstant, with positive values on the atom and the nonatomic part. The values of $f^p$ are $(1,8,27)$, and those of $g^q$ are $(8,64,216)=8(1,8,27)$. Thus
$$
\int f^p=2+4+27/2=39/2,\qquad
\int g^q=16+32+108=156,\qquad
\int fg=4+8+27=39.
$$
Writing $H=39/2$, the product of the rooted integrals is $H^{2/3}(8H)^{1/3}=2H=39$. Both normalized power densities are $(1,8,27)/H$. In particular the Young bound is attained in each cell. [Expository]

**Example B (strictness on two atoms).** With counting measure on two points take $p=q=2$, $f=(1,2)$, $g=(2,1)$. The power integrals are $1+4=5$ and $4+1=5$, and the product integral is $2+2=4<5$. The normalized power densities are $(1/5,4/5)$ and $(4/5,1/5)$, which differ at both atoms. The normalized products are $2/5$ at each atom and the two Young upper bounds are $1/2$ each. The integrated gap is $1/5$, giving an unnormalized gap of $1$. [Expository]

**Definitions.** Use the lower-integral rules in [§2.3.2.6](#2326-monotone-convergence-on-truncation-domains-the-lower-integral-theorem-behind-lemma-c-and-the-signed-limits-at-both-ends-of-eulers-integral). Fix any measurable space $X$ and measure $\mu$. For $h:X\to[0,\infty]$, write $\int h$ for the lower integral with respect to $\mu$, the supremum of the integrals of nonnegative measurable simple minorants. It is defined for arbitrary $h$ and is invariant under equality almost everywhere. A function is a.e. measurable if it agrees a.e. with a measurable representative. For $0<r<\infty$, set $I_r(h)=\int h^r$ and $N_r(h)=I_r(h)^{1/r}$. Positive powers preserve zero and infinity and have reciprocal powers as inverses. Extended arithmetic uses $0\cdot\infty=0$. For $0<N_r(h)<\infty$, write $\widehat h_r=h N_r(h)^{-1}$. The Lean definition funMulInvSnorm is total and uses this same formula everywhere. The reconstruction and unit moment below require finite, positive scales.

Mathlib's real predicate HolderConjugate is HolderTriple at third exponent $1$. It has $p,q>0$ and $1/p+1/q=1$. Each reciprocal is strictly below one because the other is strictly positive. Consequently $p,q>1$, and multiplying the reciprocal identity by $pq$ yields $(p-1)q=p$. Conversely any real $p>1$ gives a unique such $q=p/(p-1)>1$. The embedded coefficients $1/p$ and $1/q$ in $[0,\infty]$ are positive finite and sum to one. [Mathlib, ConjExponents.lean:48–62,163–189](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Data/Real/ConjExponents.lean#L48-L62)

**Lemma (scalar Young).** For real conjugates $p,q$ and $a,b\in[0,\infty]$,
$$
ab\le \frac{a^p}{p}+\frac{b^q}{q}.
$$
For finite $a,b$ equality holds exactly when $a^p=b^q$. [Lean, read]

*Proof.* If either input is infinite, its positive power is infinite and its positive finite coefficient preserves infinity. The right side is then infinite, including when the other input is zero. For finite inputs the extended proof transports operations to nonnegative real numbers. Both denominators are strictly positive, so division commutes with the embedding. The scalar real input is the two-weight arithmetic–geometric mean inequality, with weights $\alpha=1/p$, $\beta=1/q$ and arguments $u=a^p$, $v=b^q$. Its geometric mean $u^\alpha v^\beta$ is $ab$, and its arithmetic mean is the right side. For positive $u,v$ the equality criterion is the strict concavity of the logarithm with both weights positive. When one of $u,v$ is zero, the geometric mean is zero and the arithmetic mean is zero exactly when both are zero. This is the finite equality input used below. $\square$ [Mathlib, Analysis/MeanInequalities.lean:499–504,515–520,532–545,550–565](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/MeanInequalities.lean#L499-L565)

**Lemma (unit moments).** For arbitrary $F,G:X\to[0,\infty]$ with $F$ a.e. measurable and $\int F^p=\int G^q=1$, one has $\int FG\le1$. [Lean, read]

*Proof.* Integrate scalar Young by monotonicity of the lower integral. The summand $F^p/p$ is a.e. measurable, so the lower integral of the sum splits. A finite constant pulls out of the lower integral even for an arbitrary nonnegative function. Pull out $1/p$ and $1/q$ and use the unit moments. The resulting sum is $1/p+1/q=1$. These two integration rules explain why the lemma needs only one a.e. measurable input. $\square$ [Mathlib, Integral/MeanInequalities.lean:66–79](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/MeanInequalities.lean#L66-L79)

**Theorem (integral Hölder).** For every measure $\mu$, every real conjugate pair $p,q$ and every pair of a.e. measurable $f,g:X\to[0,\infty]$,
$$
\int fg\le N_p(f)N_q(g).
$$
If both power integrals are finite and positive, equality holds if and only if
$$
\frac{f^p}{I_p(f)}=\frac{g^q}{I_q(g)}
\quad\text{almost everywhere}.
$$
Equivalently, $f^p=cg^q$ a.e. for some real $0<c<\infty$, in which case $c=I_p(f)/I_q(g)$. [Lean, read] for the inequality. The integral equality proof is expository.

*Proof in the finite positive case.* For $r>0$ and $0<I_r(h)<\infty$, $N_r(h)$ and its inverse are finite and positive. Thus $h=\widehat h_r N_r(h)$ pointwise by associativity and the inverse law at the scalar $N_r(h)$. This remains true at points where $h=\infty$. The positive-power product law gives $(\widehat h_r)^r=h^r I_r(h)^{-1}$. It applies at $h=0$ and $h=\infty$ because the scaling factor is positive and finite. The coefficient rule gives $\int(\widehat h_r)^r=I_r(h)I_r(h)^{-1}=1$, with no measurability needed for this identity.

Set $A=N_p(f)$, $B=N_q(g)$, $F=\widehat f_p$ and $G=\widehat g_q$. The first normalized function is a.e. measurable, since multiplication by a fixed finite scalar preserves measurability of a representative. Reconstruct $fg=FG(AB)$ and use the finite coefficient rule once more. Then $\int fg=AB\int FG\le AB$ by the unit-moment lemma. The finite positive inequality branch needs only the a.e. measurability of $f$. [Mathlib, Integral/MeanInequalities.lean:81–125](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/MeanInequalities.lean#L81-L125)

*Zero and infinite moments.* If $I_p(f)=0$, the a.e. measurable nonnegative function $f^p$ has lower integral zero and hence vanishes a.e. Positivity of the exponent then gives $f=0$ a.e. Consequently $fg=0$ a.e., even at points with $g=\infty$. The product of the sizes is also zero by the same extended convention. If $I_p(f)>0$ and $I_q(g)=0$, use this argument with the roles interchanged. These two branches take precedence. With both moments positive, if either is infinite the corresponding size is infinite and the other size is positive. Their product is infinity, so the inequality follows from the order. This exhausts the extended cases. On the zero measure both moments vanish. [Mathlib, Integral/MeanInequalities.lean:127–166](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/MeanInequalities.lean#L127-L166)

*Equality in the finite positive case.* Finiteness of each power integral implies finiteness of the corresponding function a.e. Indeed an infinite value on a positive-measure set for a measurable representative would allow arbitrarily large constant simple minorants there. Use finite measurable representatives for $F,G$, setting any infinite values to zero on their measurable null sets. All the integrals and a.e. statements are preserved. The finite nonnegative measurable difference
$$
D=F^p/p+G^q/q-FG
$$
is integrable, being bounded by the Young upper bound, whose integral is one. Additivity applied to $FG+D$ gives $\int FG+\int D=1$. Since $AB$ is finite and strictly positive, equality in the original inequality is equivalent to $\int FG=1$, hence to $\int D=0$. This holds exactly when $D=0$ a.e. Pointwise finite Young equality identifies this condition with $F^p=G^q$ a.e., which is the displayed condition. Integrating any relation $f^p=cg^q$ with $0<c<\infty$ fixes $c$ by the finite positive moments, and that value in turn gives the normalized identity. $\square$ [Expository]

**Endpoint and measurability remarks.** The real conjugacy predicate has an empty slice at either real exponent $1$, since that would force the other positive reciprocal to vanish. The endpoint pair $(1,\infty)$ has a separate proof. Put $S=\operatorname{ess\,sup}_\mu g$, the least a.e. upper bound, so $g\le S$ a.e. If $S<\infty$, then $fg\le Sf$ a.e. and $\int fg\le S\int f$ by lower-integral monotonicity and the finite coefficient rule. This covers $S=0$ even when $\int f=\infty$. If $S=\infty$ and $\int f>0$, the bound is infinity. If $S=\infty$ and $\int f=0$, a.e. measurability of $f$ gives $f=0$ a.e., and both sides vanish. The symmetric $(\infty,1)$ case interchanges $f,g$. With $0<S<\infty$ and $0<\int f<\infty$, equality holds exactly when $g=S$ a.e. on $\{f>0\}$: on the conull set of finite $f$ and $g\le S$ the integrable gap is $f(S-g)$, and its integral vanishes exactly when the gap vanishes a.e. This uses the inherited essential-supremum upper bound.

For a failure of the public theorem with just one a.e.-measurability hypothesis deleted, give $X=\{1,2,\ldots\}$ the indiscrete sigma algebra and total mass one. Take $p=q=2$, $f(n)=1/n$ and $g(n)=\infty$. Measurable simple functions are constant, and the only nonnegative constant minorant of $f^2$ is zero. Therefore $I_2(f)=0$ and $I_2(g)=\infty$. Yet $fg$ is identically infinity since $f$ is everywhere positive, and the lower integral is infinity. The only measurable null set is empty, so $f$ cannot agree a.e. with a measurable function. Interchanging the factors tests the other hypothesis. This example also shows why a zero *lower integral* alone cannot replace the implication to a.e. vanishing. [Expository]

Riesz gives the integral triangle inequality separately as equation (6). His footnote on p. 455 attributes the finite-sum triangle inequality, equation (2), to Minkowski's 1907 book and cites p. 95. The cited printed page in the digitized edition treats three-dimensional lattices. The precise original location of the inequality remains open. <a id="finite-weighted-holder"></a>

##### 2.3.2.7.5 Weighted Hölder and products of a finite family

A bound on the moment of each adjacent ratio can bound the integral of their product. The ratios may be strongly dependent, so the useful estimate keeps the original measure and assigns each factor a share of the reciprocal-exponent budget. In the archive's `holder_lintegral_prod_le`, a family of $n$ factors uses $n$-th moments with shares $1/n$. Weighted Hölder also lets one factor carry no share, a distinction that matters when the factor has value zero or infinity.

**Example 1 (an interval and an atom).** Use Lebesgue measure on $(0,1)$ plus mass $3$ at $2$. Write a pair for the constant value on the interval and the value at the atom. For $g_1=(1,2)$, $g_2=(2,4)$, $g_3=(3,6)$, the cubic moments are $25,200,675$. The product integral is $6+3\cdot48=150$, and $25\cdot200\cdot675=150^3$. If we replace $g_3$ by $(3,0)$, the product integral is $6$ and the product of the new moments is $25\cdot200\cdot27>6^3$. Here the normalized cubic moments agree pointwise as densities in the first calculation and differ at the atom in the second.

**Definitions.** Let $(X,\mathcal A,\mu)$ be any measure space. Write $L(f)$ for the nonnegative lower integral of a function $f:X\to[0,\infty]$. Use extended multiplication with $0\cdot\infty=0$, and $x^0=1$ for every $x\in[0,\infty]$. A function is a.e. measurable if it agrees with a measurable representative outside a null set. For finitely many such functions all representative replacements can be made on a single conull set.

**Lemma (weighted product).** [Lean, read] For every index type $I$, finite subset $S\subset I$, family $f_i:X\to[0,\infty]$ a.e. measurable for each $i\in S$, and real weights $w_i\ge0$ for $i\in S$ with $\sum_{i\in S}w_i=1$, one has

$$ L\left(\prod_{i\in S} f_i^{w_i}\right)\le\prod_{i\in S} L(f_i)^{w_i}. $$

There is no finiteness or sigma-finiteness requirement on $\mu$. The active set $A=\{i\in S:w_i>0\}$ is nonempty since its weights sum to one. Terms outside $A$ are units on both sides, even at zero and infinity. Their a.e. measurability is part of the pinned declaration, and the mathematical bound under the weaker active-only hypothesis follows by replacing each inactive function by the constant one.

**Proof from the production bodies.** The two-weight declaration first handles weights $(0,1)$ and $(1,0)$ by $x^0=1$. For $u,v>0$ with $u+v=1$, the real exponents $1/u,1/v$ are conjugate and exceed one. Apply [§2.3.2.7.4](#integral-holder-normalization) to $f^u,g^v$, and compose the positive powers to recover the moments $L(f),L(g)$. This supplies the two-weight step with the full extended range.

In `lintegral_prod_norm_pow_le` the induction is on $S$ with the weights generalized. The empty case is impossible because $0\ne1$. Write the inserted index as $j$ and the remaining set as $T$. If $w_j=1$, nonnegativity forces every weight on $T$ to be zero. Both sides reduce to $L(f_j)$. Otherwise $t=1-w_j>0$, since the sum condition and nonnegativity give $w_j\le1$. Put $v_i=w_i/t$ on $T$. These weights are nonnegative and sum to one. The a.e. measurable function $H=\prod_{i\in T}f_i^{v_i}$ satisfies $H^t=\prod_{i\in T}f_i^{w_i}$. The power identity distributes over the entire finite product at a nonnegative exponent and composes with $v_i$, including $v_i=0$. With $M_i=L(f_i)$ the two-weight bound and the induction hypothesis give

$$ L(f_j^{w_j}H^t)\le M_j^{w_j}L(H)^t\le M_j^{w_j}\left(\prod_{i\in T} M_i^{v_i}\right)^t=M_j^{w_j}\prod_{i\in T} M_i^{w_i}. $$

The middle comparison uses monotonicity of nonnegative power and multiplication in the extended order. It remains valid when a multiplier is zero or infinity. This is the induction used in the pinned proof, including when $w_j=0$ and $t=1$. The distinguished-factor declaration with a function $g$ of weight $q\ge0$ and $q+\sum_{i\in S}w_i=1$ embeds $S$ as the `some` indices of `Option I` and adds `none` for $g$. The sum expands to the specified total and the product splits into $g^q$ and the old factors. Here $S$ itself may be empty, forcing $q=1$. [Pinned integral proofs, lines 168–261](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/MeanInequalities.lean#L168-L261)

**Equality and extended moments.** If any active $M_j=0$, a.e. measurability gives $f_j=0$ a.e., so both sides vanish, including when another moment is infinite. If all active moments are positive and some is infinite, the right side is infinite. Equality then holds exactly when the lower integral on the left is infinite, equivalently when its integrals of the increasing truncations at positive integers are unbounded. On two counting atoms with weights $1/2,1/2$, $(\infty,0)$ paired with $(1,0)$ gives equality, while pairing it with $(0,1)$ gives left side zero. Zero measure belongs to the first branch.

Assume now that every active $M_i$ is finite and positive. Then each active $f_i$ is finite a.e., since an infinite value on a measurable set of positive measure would force infinite integral. Take measurable representatives and set them to zero on their measurable infinite-value null sets. Define $h_i=f_i/M_i$ for $i\in A$. Each $L(h_i)=1$, and finite positive scalar extraction gives the original left side as $\prod_{i\in A}M_i^{w_i}$ times $L(\prod_{i\in A}h_i^{w_i})$. The real-valued deficit

$$ D=\sum_{i\in A}w_i h_i-\prod_{i\in A}h_i^{w_i} $$

is measurable and nonnegative by finite weighted AM–GM. The arithmetic mean has integral one by finite additivity, so $D$ is integrable and the normalized product integral plus the deficit integral equals one. Equality holds exactly when $D=0$ a.e. The strict weighted AM–GM criterion gives precisely

$$ \text{for a.e. }x,\qquad f_i(x)/M_i=f_j(x)/M_j\quad\text{for every }i,j\in A. $$

This includes a singleton active set, where equality is automatic. All subtraction here takes place in the finite reals. [Pinned scalar AM–GM and equality](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/MeanInequalities.lean#L264-L287)

**Corollary (finite family of norms, including infinite exponents).** For every finite $S$ and a.e. measurable $g_i:X\to[0,\infty]$ for $i\in S$, let $p_i\in[1,\infty]$ satisfy $\sum_{i\in S}1/p_i=1$, with $1/\infty=0$. Set $N_i=L(g_i^{p_i})^{1/p_i}$ for finite $p_i$ and $N_i=\operatorname{ess\,sup}_\mu g_i$ otherwise. Then $L(\prod_{i\in S}g_i)\le\prod_{i\in S}N_i$.

Indeed, the finite-exponent set $F$ is nonempty and its weights $1/p_i$ sum to one. If any $N_j=0$, a finite exponent gives a.e. vanishing by the zero-integral criterion and an infinite exponent gives a.e. vanishing by the essential upper bound. Hence both sides are zero even in the presence of an infinite norm. If the norms are positive and any is infinite, the right side is infinite, and equality has the same unbounded-truncation criterion as above for this product. Infinite norms can yield equality or strictness even when the infinite norm belongs to an infinite exponent. Take counting measure on two points with $p_1=1,p_2=\infty$, and $g_2=(\infty,0)$. The choices $g_1=(1,0)$ and $g_1=(0,1)$ give respectively infinity and zero on the left. With an infinite norm at a finite exponent the same two outcomes follow by interchanging the function roles while keeping the exponents.

For the remaining branch all norms are finite and positive. Apply the lemma to $f_i=g_i^{p_i}$ for $i\in F$ and bound every other factor by $N_i$ a.e. Put $P=\prod_{i\in F}g_i$, $Q=\prod_{i\in S\setminus F}g_i$, and $C=\prod_{i\in S\setminus F}N_i$, with empty products equal to one. The finiteness of the moments ensures that $P$ is finite a.e. The two inequalities are $L(PQ)\le C L(P)\le\prod_{i\in S}N_i$. Equality in the second is exactly the common normalized moment density on $F$, since $C>0$. Equality in the first is equivalent to $P(C-Q)=0$ a.e. because this nonnegative loss is bounded by the integrable function $CP$. On $\{P>0\}$, the product $Q$ attains $C$ exactly when every infinite-exponent factor attains $N_i$, since all caps are positive and the factors lie below them a.e. Thus equality requires both the common density on $F$ and saturation of each essential bound a.e. on the positive support of $P$. For a singleton $F$, the finite exponent is $1$ and the density condition is automatic. If $|F|>1$, no finite exponent can equal $1$, since the other reciprocals are positive. An empty or all-infinite family cannot satisfy the reciprocal budget.

**Example 2 (zero weights and infinite exponents).** On the measure of Example 1 let $f=(1,4)$, $h=(4,16)$ and $k=(0,\infty)$. With weights $(1/2,1/2,0)$, the product integrand is $(2,8)$, its integral is $26$, and the bound is $\sqrt{13\cdot52}=26$. The third moment is infinite, and its zeroth power is one. For a norm example use $g_1=(1,2)$, $g_2=(2,4)$ with exponents $2,2$ and $z=(5,1)$ with exponent infinity. The finite moments are $13$ and $52$, the cap is $5$, and the bound is $130$. The integral is $10+3\cdot8=34$ because the cap is missed at the atom. Replacing $z$ by $(5,5)$ attains $130$. Replacing it by zero gives the zero norm branch. In the weighted lemma the inactive function disappears, whereas in the norm inequality the infinite-exponent function stays in the integrand and is paid for by its essential bound.

**Failure boundaries.** [Expository] On a single atom of mass $2$ with all functions equal to one, discarding the total weight condition and using three weights $1/6$ would require $2\le\sqrt 2$. Allowing negative weights with total one fails on two counting atoms: use weights $(2,-1)$, $f=(1,0)$ and $h=(1,1)$. The left side is $1$ and the right side is $1/2$. For an active a.e.-measurability deletion, use the indiscrete probability space of §2.3.2.7.4 with $f(n)=1/n$, pair it with the constant infinity and a constant one, and use three weights $1/3$. The first lower moment is zero since constant measurable simple minorants have supremum zero, yet the integrand is everywhere infinite. The bound would read $\infty\le0$. Inactive measurability can be dropped as explained in the lemma.

**Return to the archive.** In `holder_lintegral_prod_le` take $n=|S|>0$, $f_i=g_i^n$ and $w_i=1/n$. Measurability of $g_i$ supplies a.e. measurability of the powers. Composition cancels the two positive exponents, and the input $L(g_i^n)\le B_i^n$ then bounds the product integral by $\prod_{i\in S}B_i$. The consumer has a separate empty case, where probability normalization supplies $L(1)=1$. In `moment_ratio_telescoping` the integrand factors are the nonnegative powers $(M_{i+1}/M_i)^{2q}$ embedded into the extended reals. Positivity of each $M_i$ pays for cancellation, and measurability of the ratios pays for the product call. The $n$-th moment of each such factor is the assumed moment of exponent $2nq$. In `conv_sq_le` the two weighted inputs at fixed $\xi$ are $a(\eta)=k(\xi-\eta)$ and $a(\eta)g(\eta)^2$. Both have weight $1/2$. Their weighted product is $a(\eta)g(\eta)$ by extended power algebra. Squaring the bound and using invariance of Lebesgue measure under $\eta\mapsto\xi-\eta$ yields the claimed convolution estimate. [Archive provenance](../sources/SOURCES.md#1-the-lean-archive-primary-source)

**Source note.** Riesz's 1910 §2 gives the finite-sum Cauchy–Hölder bound in equation (1) on p. 455 and the two-factor integral bound in equation (5) on p. 456, with the right-hand integrals assumed to exist. The inherited independent review covers the weighted production, its equality and endpoint analysis, and the named archive calls. The finite-family induction and distinguished-factor construction follow the pinned Mathlib community proof in the integral file credited to Rémy Degenne. The finite positive equality argument, the mixed-exponent corollary and the examples are the book's exposition. [Riesz, original scan](https://real.mtak.hu/214052/1/Riesz_14_Math_Annalen_1910_449-497.pdf#page=7)

<a id="holder-lp-product"></a>

##### 2.3.2.7.6 Pointwise products and the Hölder map

**Motivation.** A pairing such as $\int \overline{u}v\,d\mu$ begins with two functions in $L^2$. Before using linearity of the Bochner integral, we need the actual product to be integrable. This requires an almost everywhere strongly measurable representative and a finite integral of its modulus. For a target exponent other than one, the same problem is to turn control of two moments into control of a third and then pass to classes modulo null sets.

**Example 1 (a bounded multiplier on a nonprobability space).** Give the two points $a,b$ masses $2,3$, respectively. Take $f=(1,2)$ and $g=(3,1)$. With $(p,q,r)=(2,2,\infty)$, the reciprocal identity is $1/2=1/2+0$. The input sizes are $\sqrt{14}$ and $3$. The product $(3,2)$ has size $\sqrt{30}$ in $L^2$, and $30\le 9\cdot14$. The multiplier is controlled by its essential bound and the finite moment still uses the original masses. With all three exponents infinite on the same space, the size comparison is $3\le 2\cdot3$.

**Definition.** Let $(X,\mathcal A,\mu)$ be any measure space. A function into a normed space is strongly measurable if it is a pointwise limit of measurable finite-valued simple functions, and almost everywhere strongly measurable if it agrees almost everywhere with a strongly measurable function. For either a positive finite real $s$ or $s=\infty$, write

$$N_s(u)= \begin{cases} \left(\int_X \lVert u\rVert^s\,d\mu\right)^{1/s},&0<s<\infty,\\ \operatorname{ess\,sup}_{X}\lVert u\rVert,&s=\infty. \end{cases}$$

These sizes take values in $[0,\infty]$. The space $L^s(\mu;E)$ consists of classes of almost everywhere strongly measurable $E$-valued functions of finite size. For $s<1$ the inherited expression is a quasi-norm in general. In a reciprocal equation the symbol $1/\infty$ means zero. In a product of **sizes** we use the extended nonnegative convention $0\cdot\infty=\infty\cdot0=0$.

**Lemma (finite real exponents).** [Lean, read] Let $p,q,r$ be real, $0<p<q$, and $1/p=1/q+1/r$. For two almost everywhere measurable $F,G:X\to[0,\infty]$ write $I(H)=\int_X H\,d\mu$ for the lower integral. Then

$$I((FG)^p)^{1/p}\le I(F^q)^{1/q}I(G^r)^{1/r}.$$

The hypotheses force $r>p$, so all three powers on the left and right use genuinely positive finite exponents. The Lean statement is `ENNReal.lintegral_Lp_mul_le_Lq_mul_Lr`. Its codomain and inequality use extended nonnegative values, while its three exponents are real. [MeanInequalities.lean:277–308](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/MeanInequalities.lean#L277-L308)

**Proof.** Because $q>p>0$, the difference $1/p-1/q$ is strictly positive and strictly smaller than $1/p$. Thus $1/r$ has those properties, which excludes $r=0$ even in Lean's totalized real division and gives $r>p$. Put $a=q/p$ and $b=a/(a-1)=q/(q-p)=r/p$. Both are greater than one and $1/a+1/b=1$. Apply the [ordinary two-factor lower-integral Hölder inequality](#integral-holder-normalization) to $F^p,G^p$. Constant positive powers preserve almost everywhere measurability. Since $(FG)^p=F^pG^p$, we obtain

$$I((FG)^p)\le I(F^{pa})^{1/a}I(G^{pb})^{1/b}.$$

Raise to $1/p$ using monotonicity, distribute the power over the product, and use $pa=q$, $pb=r$. The power and product laws hold for zero and infinity as well, by positivity and the convention for $0\cdot\infty$. This is the proof at lines 287–308, where $a$ is named `p2` and $b$ is its `conjExponent`. The ordinary conjugate-exponent inequality is the input at line 298. [MeanInequalities.lean:150–166](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/MeanInequalities.lean#L150-L166)

**Theorem (product mapping).** [Expository] Let $E,F,H$ be normed spaces over $\mathbb R$ or $\mathbb C$, and let $B:E\times F\to H$ be bilinear with $\lVert B(v,w)\rVert\le C\lVert v\rVert\lVert w\rVert$ for some finite $C\ge0$ and all $v,w$. Let $p,q,r\in(0,\infty]$ obey $1/p=1/q+1/r$. For all almost everywhere strongly measurable $f:X\to E$, $g:X\to F$, the function $h(x)=B(f(x),g(x))$ is almost everywhere strongly measurable and satisfies

$$N_p(h)\le C N_q(f)N_r(g).$$

Consequently $B$ gives a well-defined bilinear map $L^q(\mu;E)\times L^r(\mu;F)\to L^p(\mu;H)$ with the indicated size bound. For pointwise multiplication in a normed algebra with a submultiplicative norm take $C=1$. For $p=1$ the membership condition is precisely Bochner integrability when $H$ is complete. In that case $\lVert\int_X h\,d\mu\rVert\le N_1(h)$.

**Proof.** For strongly measurable representatives, approximate the two inputs pointwise by sequences of simple functions. On the common finite refinement at each index, the image under $B$ is simple. The bilinear bound implies joint continuity by expanding $B(v',w')-B(v,w)$ as $B(v'-v,w')+B(v,w'-w)$. The simple images therefore converge pointwise to the image of the representatives. On the intersection of the two sets of full measure where the representatives agree with the originals, the images agree. This supplies the almost everywhere strongly measurable representative. If both inputs are strongly measurable, the argument applies to the inputs themselves.

For a measurable nonnegative function $u$, zero lower integral implies $u=0$ almost everywhere, since each set $\{u\ge 1/n\}$ must be null. The argument applies to an almost everywhere measurable function by choosing a measurable representative. An essential supremum in $[0,\infty]$ is an almost everywhere upper bound: for a finite supremum $M$, intersect the countable family of bounds $M+1/n$ and take the limit. Hence for every allowed $s$, $N_s(v)=0$ is equivalent to $v=0$ almost everywhere for the functions under consideration.

If $\mu=0$, all sizes vanish, and even a constant zero function serves as an almost everywhere representative of any function. For $\mu\ne0$, if $C=0$ then $h=0$ pointwise. If $C>0$ and either input has size zero, then $h=0$ almost everywhere and the right-hand side is zero, even if the other size is infinite. If $C>0$ and both sizes are positive with at least one infinite, the right-hand side is infinite. The inequality then holds in the extended order, and membership can depend on the overlap of supports.

It remains to take $C>0$ and finite positive input sizes. For finite $q,r$, the reciprocal identity forces $0<p<\min(q,r)$. The lemma applied to the two moduli and monotonicity of the lower integral give the bound, with $C$ factored out by homogeneity. If $q=\infty$ and $r<\infty$, then $p=r$ and $\lVert h\rVert\le C N_\infty(f)\lVert g\rVert$ almost everywhere. Integrate the $r$-th power and take its $r$-th root. If $r=\infty$ and $q<\infty$, use $p=q$ and $\lVert h\rVert\le C N_\infty(g)\lVert f\rVert$. If both $q,r$ are infinite, so is $p$, and $C N_\infty(f)N_\infty(g)$ is itself an almost everywhere bound for $\lVert h\rVert$. These four exponent cases exhaust the reciprocal equation. In particular an infinite target requires two infinite inputs.

For finite input sizes the bound yields a finite output size. Changing either representative changes the product only on the union of the two exceptional null sets. Pointwise bilinearity passes to the classes. When $p=1$, $N_1(h)$ is the integral of the modulus. The product therefore defines an integrable class, to which the [Bochner construction and norm inequality](#banach-dense-extension) apply. [Bochner/Basic.lean:934–950](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/Bochner/Basic.lean#L934-L950)

**The interfaces in Mathlib.** [Lean, read] The auxiliary `eLpNorm'` takes a real exponent. The public `eLpNorm` takes an exponent of type `ℝ≥0∞`, with an explicit zero branch giving zero, a top branch taking the essential supremum, and a positive finite branch passing `toReal` to `eLpNorm'`. `ENNReal.HolderTriple P Q R` encodes $P^{-1}+Q^{-1}=R^{-1}$ with the **input exponents first**. Thus the textbook triple $(p,q,r)$ is supplied in the order $(q,r,p)$. Extended inversion has $\infty^{-1}=0$ and $0^{-1}=\infty$. Real inversion instead has Lean's totalized $0^{-1}=0$. The zero-exponent branches in the library are outside the positive-exponent theorem above.
