[LpSeminorm/Defs.lean:69–119](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSeminorm/Defs.lean#L69-L119) · [Data/ENNReal/Holder.lean:42–81](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Data/ENNReal/Holder.lean#L42-L81)

The finite-real norm estimate compares the norm of the output with the product of input norms under an almost everywhere domination with constant $C$. It extracts $C$ and calls the lower-integral lemma. The extended-exponent wrapper first separates zero and top inputs. For finite positive inputs it transports the reciprocal equation through `toReal` with finite reciprocals, proves that the output exponent in real coordinates is positive and strictly smaller than the first input, and then calls the real estimate. For a top input the proof uses the essential bound and either the finite moment or a second essential bound. These estimates accept an arbitrary two-argument operation meeting the stated size bound. The membership lemma `MemLp.of_bilin` asks separately for almost everywhere strong measurability of its output. The scalar-action specialization `MemLp.smul` proves that premise by composing the measurable pair with continuous scalar multiplication and proves finiteness by multiplying finite extended norms. `MemLp.mul` uses the action of the normed ring on itself. Its method receiver corresponds to the **right** factor. `MemLp.integrable_mul` arranges that order and uses `memLp_one_iff_integrable`. [LpSeminorm/CompareExp.lean:166–272](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSeminorm/CompareExp.lean#L166-L272) · [LpSeminorm/CompareExp.lean:292–327](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSeminorm/CompareExp.lean#L292-L327) · [L1Space/Integrable.lean:59–67](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/L1Space/Integrable.lean#L59-L67) · [L1Space/Integrable.lean:1085–1088](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/L1Space/Integrable.lean#L1085-L1088)

**Two calls on the quotient.** [Lean, read] In A2, let $\nu$ denote `quotMeasure Γ μ` on the quotient by the left action of the discrete subgroup $\Gamma$. The archive fixes a second-countable Borel topological group and works here with a left- and right-invariant measure, finite on compacts, on a locally compact group. The quotient measure is the pushforward of the measure restricted to a [fundamental domain](#234-measurable-fundamental-domains-for-discrete-quotients). `MemL2Quotient Γ μ ψ` requires left invariance under $\Gamma$ and an $L^2(\nu)$ descended function. Right translation preserves $\nu$ in these hypotheses. Both calls below occur in `src/Development/AutomorphicSpectrum/Proofs/BlockDecomposition/L2InnerDerivativeTranslate.lean` in the Alpöge–Claude A2 archive, SHA-256 `9631b4f020199e580ba86178cb56c6e9b09f690c2369a80dc4334aa93f49c7fc`. [A2 source record](../sources/SOURCES.md#a2)

| Actual call | Parameters supplied | How the result is used |
|---|---|---|
| `integrable_pair`, lines 68–81, `eLpNorm_le_eLpNorm_mul_eLpNorm_of_nnnorm` | $\nu$, the triple $(2,2,1)$ in Lean order, $u\in L^2(\nu)$, the descent of $x\mapsto\psi(xc(s))$ for each real $s$, $B(a,b)=\overline a b$, $C=1$. Both input measurability witnesses are supplied and the pointwise bound follows from multiplicativity of the complex modulus. This operation is real bilinear and conjugate-linear in its first complex variable. | The inner $L^1$ moment is bounded by $N_2(u)N_2(\operatorname{descend}\psi)$ uniformly for all $s$, since right translation preserves $\nu$. Joint measurability comes from the measurable section, continuity and multiplication at lines 56–63. Integrating the uniform bound over the finite interval measure on $\operatorname{uIoc}(0,t)$ gives finiteness on the product measure. |
| `R_sub_eq_integral`, lines 99–105, `(Lp.memLp u).star.integrable_mul hf.2` | $\nu$, $(2,2,1)$, the conjugate of $u$, and the descent of any $f$ with `hf : MemL2Quotient Γ μ f`. The section identifies the descended value with $f(\operatorname{sec}(q))$. | The local helper `hi` pays both hypotheses of `integral_sub`, once for $f(x)=\varphi(xc(t))$ and once for $f=\varphi$. The separate `integrable_pair` supplies the premise of the integral swap. |

For a second direct Mathlib use of the finite real lemma, `eLpNorm'_le_eLpNorm'_mul_rpow_measure_univ` uses the constant function one. For real exponents $0<p<q$, its contribution is the size factor $\mu(X)^{1/p-1/q}$, with $r=pq/(q-p)$. The case $p=q$ is settled by the zeroth-power identity. [CompareExp.lean:32–51](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/LpSeminorm/CompareExp.lean#L32-L51)

**Example 2 (a complex product in the first call).** [Expository] Use $G=\{2^n:n\in\mathbb Z\}$ under multiplication with the discrete topology, counting measure and $\Gamma=\{1\}$. The quotient is identified with $G$, the section is the inverse of this identification and $\nu$ is counting measure. Both translation invariances hold, compact sets are finite, and continuity of the functions on $G$ is automatic. Take $c(s)=1$, $t=2$, and functions $u,\psi$ that vanish off $\{1,2\}$ with values $(i,1)$ and $(1,2i)$ there. The squares of their $L^2$ norms are $2$ and $5$. The product $\overline u\psi$ has values $(-i,2i)$, so its $L^1$ norm is $3$ and its Bochner integral over the quotient is $i$. The inner estimate reads $3\le\sqrt{10}$. On the product of the interval and the quotient the $L^1$ norm is $6\le2\sqrt{10}$ and the integral is $2i$. This instantiates `integrable_pair` with its original hypotheses. Taking $f=\psi$ also instantiates the local integrability statement `hi`. The derivative identity enclosing that statement has further hypotheses on $\varphi,\psi,c$.

**Example 3 (a real exponent below one).** [Expository] At a single atom of mass $4$, take $f=2$ and $g=3$. With $(p,q,r)=(2/3,1,2)$, the reciprocal equation reads $3/2=1+1/2$. Here $N_{2/3}(fg)=6\cdot4^{3/2}=48$, while $N_1(f)N_2(g)=8\cdot6=48$. In the lower-integral lemma the powered inputs use the ordinary conjugate exponents $a=3/2$ and $b=3$.

**Failure tests.** [Expository] On $(0,1)$ with Lebesgue measure take $f(x)=g(x)=x^{-1/3}$. Both squared modulus integrals equal $3$. The product is in $L^1$, with modulus integral $3$, but its squared modulus integral diverges, since the integral from $\varepsilon$ to $1$ is $3(\varepsilon^{-1/3}-1)$. Thus choosing $p=2$ with $q=r=2$ can lose membership. For a measurability deletion, give a two-point set the trivial $\sigma$-algebra and total mass $2$. Take $f=(1,-1)$ and $g\equiv1$ with $(p,q,r)=(1,2,2)$. Both moduli are measurable and both would-be $L^2$ sizes are $\sqrt2$. Every measurable simple function is constant, and the only null set is empty. Hence $fg=f$ has no almost everywhere strongly measurable representative and fails the integrability predicate.

**Remark on infinite sizes.** [Expository] On $\mathbb N$ with counting measure use $(p,q,r)=(1,1,\infty)$. The choices $f=1$, $g=1$ give an infinite output size, while $f=1$, $g=1_{\{0\}}$ give output size one with the same infinite right-hand bound. Replacing $g$ by zero gives size zero even though $N_1(f)=\infty$. This illustrates why the zero-size test precedes the positive-infinite-size test.

**Source note.** [Paper] Riesz's 1910 §2, printed p. 456, equation (5), gives the conjugate two-factor integral inequality on his domain of integration and explicitly derives existence of the left integral from existence of the right integrals. It is the historical target-exponent-one comparison. The reading here uses Mathlib at `0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474`. The lower-integral file credits Rémy Degenne, the norm-estimate file credits Rémy Degenne and Eric Wieser, and the integrability file credits Zhouhang Zhou. The theorem on classes, boundary arguments and hand examples are textbook deductions. [Riesz, original journal scan](https://real.mtak.hu/214052/1/Riesz_14_Math_Annalen_1910_449-497.pdf#page=8)

<a id="metric-valued-ae-limits"></a>

#### 2.3.2.8 Almost-everywhere limits of metric-valued functions

In Lemma P of [§2.3.2.7](#2327-dominated-convergence-through-the-l1-extension-the-filter-to-sequence-reduction-the-decreasing-tail-supremum-step-and-local-uniform-domination-of-the-complex-gamma-kernel), before putting the pointwise limit in $L^1$, we need a measurable representative. A limit stated almost everywhere leaves the values on a null set unspecified. On an incomplete measure space those values can destroy measurability. We will replace the inputs and the limit on the same measurable exceptional set, then read measurability of the patched limit from distances to closed sets.

**Example 1 (real values).** [Expository] Give $X=\{a,b,u,v\}$ the sigma-algebra of unions of the atoms $\{a\},\{b\},\{u,v\}$, with masses $1/3,2/3,0$. For $n\ge1$ let $F_n$ take the values $1+1/n,-2/n,n,-n$ in that order. Set both null-atom values to zero to obtain a measurable representative $H_n$. On the measurable conull set $A=\{a,b\}$ the limit takes values $1,0$. Prescribe $f(u)=7,f(v)=-7$. Its preimage of $(6,8)$ is the nonmeasurable set $\{u\}$. Define $h=f$ on $A$ and zero on its complement. The preimage under $h$ of $(6,8)$ is empty. The patched sequence converges to $h$ everywhere. Its distances to the closed set $\{0\}$ are $1+1/n,2/n,0,0$, with limit $1,0,0,0$. The zero set $\{b,u,v\}$ equals $h^{-1}(\{0\})$.

The majorant $2\,1_A$ bounds $|F_n|$ on $A$ and has integral $2$. The integral of $F_n$ is $(1+1/n)/3-4/(3n)=1/3-1/n$, which tends to the integral $1/3$ of $f$. The absolute error has integral $1/(3n)+4/(3n)=5/(3n)$. All these integrals use representatives, so the arbitrary point values on the null atom contribute zero.

**Example 2 (two-dimensional values).** [Expository] On the same space let $F_n$ take the values $(1,1/n),(0,2-1/n),(n,0),(0,n)$ in the Euclidean plane. Prescribe $f$ by the values $(1,0),(0,2),(4,0),(0,4)$. Again patch inputs and limit to $(0,0)$ outside $A$. The ball of radius $1/2$ about $(4,0)$ pulls back to $\{u\}$ under $f$ and to the empty set under the patched limit $h$. For the horizontal axis $C=\mathbb R\times\{0\}$, the patched distance functions have values $1/n,2-1/n,0,0$. Their limit has zero set $\{a,u,v\}=h^{-1}(C)$. The union of all patched input ranges is countable. Its closure contains both of the limit values on $A$ and the fallback value, so it provides a separable effective range.

The two positive-mass norms $\sqrt{1+n^{-2}}$ and $2-1/n$ are at most $2$, so $2\,1_A$ again gives an integrable majorant. The weighted sum of input vectors is $(1/3,4/3-1/(3n))$, with limit $(1/3,4/3)$, the weighted sum for $f$. Both pointwise norm errors on $A$ are $1/n$. Their weighted sum is $1/n$, whereas the norm of the difference of integrals is $1/(3n)$ because the vertical errors have opposite signs.

**Definitions and countable bases.** A map $F$ on $(X,\Sigma,\mu)$ is almost-everywhere measurable if it has a measurable representative $G$ that equals $F$ off a measurable null set. A measurable set is conull if its complement has measure zero. The Borel sigma-algebra of a topological space is generated by the open sets, equivalently by the closed sets. A pseudometric defines the topology by balls and permits distinct points at distance zero.

A base for a filter $U$ on $I$ is a family of members of $U$ such that every $S\in U$ contains a member of the family. For a countable base, take successive finite intersections to obtain a decreasing base $B_0\supset B_1\supset\cdots$. If $U$ is proper, each $B_n$ is nonempty. Choose $v(n)\in B_n$. Every $S\in U$ contains some $B_N$, so it contains $v(n)$ for every $n\ge N$. This proves that $v$ tends to $U$ along the natural-number tail filter. Mathlib's `Filter.exists_seq_tendsto` implements these three steps using `exists_antitone_basis`, nonemptiness of base members, and the basis convergence criterion. The natural numbers themselves have the decreasing base of tails. [Mathlib, Order/Filter/AtTopBot/CountablyGenerated.lean:32–34,58–72](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Order/Filter/AtTopBot/CountablyGenerated.lean#L58-L72)

Separately, each point $y$ of a pseudometric space has a countable neighborhood base $B(y,1/(k+1))$. The source uses the Archimedean property to refine each positive distance threshold by some $1/(k+1)$ in the uniformity, then takes the section through $y$. For convergence of a sequence to $y$, each such ball must contain a whole tail. This local countability is independent of separability. [Mathlib, Topology/MetricSpace/Pseudo/Defs.lean:661–690,812–814](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/MetricSpace/Pseudo/Defs.lean#L661-L690)

[Expository] If a metric space is separable, balls centered on a countable dense set with rational radii give a countable base for the entire topology. Indeed, given an open ball about $y$, choose the center less than half its radius away from $y$ and a rational radius strictly between the distance to $y$ and the old radius minus that distance. The triangle inequality makes the new ball a neighborhood of $y$ contained in the old one.

**Lemma U (everywhere limits). [Lean, read]** An everywhere pointwise limit of Borel-measurable maps into a pseudometrizable space is Borel measurable when the index is a sequence or a proper countably generated filter.

*Proof.* In the filter case compose with $v$ as above. Choose a compatible pseudometric. For a nonempty closed set $C$ put $d_C(y)=\inf_{z\in C}d(y,z)$. The triangle inequality gives $|d_C(y)-d_C(w)|\le d(y,w)$, so $d_C$ is continuous and Borel measurable. If $H_n\to h$ pointwise, then $r_n=d_C\circ H_n$ are measurable scalar functions with limit $d_C\circ h$. For each real $t$,
$$
\{x:d_C(h(x))<t\}
=\bigcup_{q\in\mathbb Q,\ q<t}\ \bigcup_{N\ge0}\ \bigcap_{n\ge N}\{x:r_n(x)\le q\}.
$$
A limit strictly below $t$ admits a rational number strictly between itself and $t$. Conversely an eventual upper bound by $q$ bounds the limit by $q$. The right side is measurable by countability. Thus $d_C\circ h$ is measurable. Its zero set is $h^{-1}(C)$ because distance zero means every ball meets $C$, and $C$ is closed. The empty closed set has empty preimage. Closed preimages suffice to prove Borel measurability.

*Source proof.* `measurable_of_tendsto_metrizable'` chooses the compatible pseudometric and uses `measurable_of_isClosed'`. It rewrites product-topology convergence as convergence at each $x$, then composes with the continuous `infNndist`. The scalar theorem `NNReal.measurable_of_tendsto'` embeds in the extended nonnegative reals, where the source selects a sequence and uses the measurable liminf, the supremum of the infima over the tails. Under convergence this equals the scalar limit. The final equality in the source identifies the closed preimage with the preimage of $\{0\}$. [Mathlib, Metrizable.lean:25–53](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Constructions/BorelSpace/Metrizable.lean#L25-L53) · [Mathlib, Real.lean:272–283](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Constructions/BorelSpace/Real.lean#L272-L283) · [Mathlib, Real.lean:459–465](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Constructions/BorelSpace/Real.lean#L459-L465)

**Theorem V (almost-everywhere limits). [Lean, read]** Let $(X,\Sigma,\mu)$ be a measure space, $Y$ pseudometrizable with its Borel sigma-algebra, and $U$ a proper countably generated filter on $I$. Suppose every $F_i:X\to Y$ is almost-everywhere measurable and $F_i(x)\to f(x)$ along $U$ for almost every $x$. Then $f$ has a measurable representative. In particular the result holds for a sequence with an almost-everywhere limit.

*Proof.* Choose $v:\mathbb N\to I$ as above and compose the convergence on the given conull set with $v\to U$. Choose measurable representatives $G_n$ of $F_{v(n)}$ and measurable null sets $N_n$ containing their disagreement sets. Also take a measurable null set $N_{\mathrm{lim}}$ containing the failure set for convergence. By countable subadditivity,
$$
A=X\setminus\left(N_{\mathrm{lim}}\cup\bigcup_{n\ge0}N_n\right)
$$
is measurable and conull. Fix a value $y_0$ in $Y$, supplied by the maps if $X$ is nonempty. For empty $X$ measurability is immediate. Patch $G_n$ and $f$ to $y_0$ outside $A$ and call the results $H_n$ and $h$. For a Borel set $D$, the preimage under $H_n$ is $A\cap G_n^{-1}(D)$ with $X\setminus A$ added exactly when $y_0\in D$. This proves that $H_n$ is measurable. On $A$, $H_n=F_{v(n)}$ and the limit is $h=f$. Off $A$ all terms and the limit equal $y_0$. Lemma U gives measurability of $h$ everywhere, and $h=f$ almost everywhere.

*Source proof.* Lines 59–63 of `aemeasurable_of_tendsto_metrizable_ae` select $v$, restrict the inputs and set the predicate $p$ to express convergence to $f(x)$. `aeSeqSet` takes the complement of a measurable hull of the failure set for the conjunction of all representative equalities and $p$. The proof of `measure_compl_aeSeqSet_eq_zero` uses `ae_all_iff` to put all equalities on one conull set and combines it with the conull convergence hypothesis. Lines 64–74 use `aeSeq.measurable` and split the convergence proof on membership in `aeSeqSet`. The fallback in both definitions is a shared classical choice from `Nonempty Y`. Proof irrelevance makes that choice independent of the particular witness used to prove nonemptiness. Lines 75–77 show the patched limit equals the prescribed limit almost everywhere. The wrapper at lines 79–82 specializes to natural-number tails. [Mathlib, Metrizable.lean:55–82](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Constructions/BorelSpace/Metrizable.lean#L55-L82) · [Mathlib, AEMeasurableSequence.lean:32–96](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/AEMeasurableSequence.lean#L32-L96)

**Lemma W (the effective range). [Lean, read]** If the inputs of Theorem V are almost-everywhere strongly measurable, so is the limit. Strong measurability means pointwise approximation by measurable finite-valued functions. Its almost-everywhere version asks for a strongly measurable representative.

*Proof.* Equip the target with the Borel sigma-algebra. Use the characterization from §2.3.2.7 that almost-everywhere strong measurability is equivalent to Borel almost-everywhere measurability together with an almost-everywhere separable range. This supplies the inputs to Theorem V and a separable set $T_n$ containing $F_{v(n)}(x)$ almost everywhere for each $n$. Choose countable $D_n$ whose closures contain $T_n$. Then $D=\bigcup_n D_n$ is countable and its closure contains every $T_n$. The closed set $T=\overline{\bigcup_n T_n}$ is therefore contained in $\overline D$ and is separable. These are the proofs of `IsSeparable.iUnion` and `IsSeparable.closure` in substance. Intersect the countably many conull range sets and the conull convergence set. On the intersection the selected sequence stays in $T$, so the limit belongs to $T$ by closedness. Theorem V gives the measurable representative, and the reverse implication of the characterization yields almost-everywhere strong measurability. The source makes the two branches explicit after `borelize β`. [Mathlib, AEStronglyMeasurable.lean:693–708](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/StronglyMeasurable/AEStronglyMeasurable.lean#L693-L708) · [Mathlib, Topology/Bases.lean:485–515](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/Bases.lean#L485-L515)

**Return to the Bochner limit.** In the branch `CompleteSpace F` of `tendsto_setToFun_of_dominated_convergence`, where $F$ is the codomain of the set operator $T$, the line proving `f_measurable` calls Lemma W directly. The sequence version supplies a proper countably generated filter through the natural-number tails. The norm supplies a compatible metric, and `borelize` sets the Borel structure for the auxiliary argument. The strong-measurability hypotheses supply representatives and separable effective ranges, and the convergence hypothesis already gives the limit. The integrable majorant is used later to bound the norm integrals and to prove convergence of the $L^1$ error. For the general filter version, §2.3.2.7 explains the reduction to test sequences and discarding a finite prefix to secure the eventual hypotheses. Target completeness belongs to the integral construction. The measurability argument works with incomplete measures and nonseparable ambient spaces. The [$L^1$ extension](#banach-dense-extension) uses simple-function density from Chapter 14, [§14.4.2.3.1](14-masullos-trace-formula-route.md#144231-finite-measure-simple-functions-in-lp). [Mathlib, SetToL1.lean:1252–1293](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/SetToL1.lean#L1252-L1293) · [Mathlib, DominatedConvergence.lean:57–74](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/DominatedConvergence.lean#L57-L74)

**Remarks on the hypotheses. [Expository]**

1. *Index countability.* Without a countable filter base the sequence extraction can fail. For an actual counterexample, take Borel Lebesgue measure on $[0,1]$, choose a non-Lebesgue-measurable $V$, and direct its finite subsets $s$ by inclusion. The Borel maps $1_s$ converge everywhere along the tail filter to $1_V$: each $x\in V$ is in all $s$ beyond $\{x\}$, and each $x\notin V$ is in none. A Borel representative of $1_V$ would give a Borel preimage of $(1/2,\infty)$ differing from $V$ only inside a measurable null set, which would make $V$ Lebesgue measurable. Any sequence of finite indices misses some $x\in V$ in its countable union, so it never enters the tail requiring $x\in s$. Also each $1_s$ equals zero almost everywhere, while there is no common conull set on which all do.
2. *Target countability.* An uncountable discrete metric space has no global countable base, yet Lemma U applies. Its scalar test works separately for each closed set. If the target is merely topological, the construction of a compatible distance and its ball base may be unavailable. That obstruction to this proof makes no claim about the theorem in any particular class of nonmetrizable spaces.
3. *Borel compatibility.* The proof needs the closed-set distances to be measurable and the closed sets to generate the target sigma-algebra. On the usual real line equip the target with the coarser sigma-algebra generated by $\{0\}$. On a two-point domain with the trivial sigma-algebra and total mass one let $F_n(a)=1/n$ and $F_n(b)=1$. Each input avoids zero and is measurable for the coarser target. The limit pulls back $\{0\}$ to $\{a\}$, which is nonmeasurable and cannot be corrected on a null set. The distance to $\{0\}$ composed with $F_n$ is nonmeasurable for $n>1$, so the scalar step fails. In the other direction, give the target $[0,1]$ its usual topology and the power-set sigma-algebra, and give the domain Borel Lebesgue measure. The dyadic round-down functions $2^{-n}\lfloor 2^n x\rfloor$ have finitely many values and Borel fibers. They converge everywhere to the identity, whose closed preimages are measurable. A representative measurable for the power-set target would have a Borel preimage of $V$ equal to $V$ off a measurable null set, again impossible. Here the closed tests succeed but fail to generate the required sigma-algebra.

*Reading scope.* R3 covers the cited limit bodies, the conull patch, the sequence and ball bases, the separable union and closure, and the direct consumer. The characterization of strong measurability and deeper topology, measure-hull and $L^1$ inputs are consumed at their stated interfaces or inherited from §2.3.2.7. The manifest at `lean/lake-manifest.json:10` pins Mathlib to `0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474`. The joined Lean archive SHA-256 is `9631b4f020199e580ba86178cb56c6e9b09f690c2369a80dc4334aa93f49c7fc`. See the [source register](../sources/SOURCES.md#ch02-metric-limit-sources) for authorship and hashes. Source reading and book rendering are distinct from Lean kernel verification.

### 2.3.3 The half-plane and critical-strip propositions

We record in mathematical notation the exact propositions linked in this chapter.

**Definition 2.3 (Target and intermediate propositions).**

1. **Primitive right half-plane (`Formalism.HC.ZeroFreeAll`).** For every bundled primitive
   character $\psi = (N, \chi, \text{prim})$ and every $s \in \mathbb C$ with $\Re s > 1/2$, we
   have $L(s, \psi.\chi) \neq 0$ [Lean, read]
   (`src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/ExplicitFormulaErrorOfHostConditions.lean:11`, `:14`, `:18`):
   ```lean
   def PChar.ZeroFreeAbove (ψ : PChar) (θ : ℝ) : Prop :=
     ∀ s : ℂ, θ < s.re → DirichletCharacter.LFunction ψ.χ s ≠ 0

   def ZeroFreeOn (Q : PChar → Prop) : Prop := ∀ ψ : PChar, Q ψ → ψ.ZeroFreeAbove (1 / 2)

   def ZeroFreeAll : Prop := ZeroFreeOn fun _ => True
   ```
   Notice that `PChar.ZeroFreeAbove ψ (1 / 2)` requires $L(s, \psi.\chi) \neq 0$ for *every*
   $s$ with $\Re s > 1/2$, **without** excluding $s = 1$ even when $\psi = \mathrm{PChar.one}$!
   Why is that true rather than false at $(\psi, s) = (\mathrm{PChar.one}, 1)$? Because, as we saw
   in Chapter 1 (Section 1.2.2), for $N = 1$ we have `LFunction 1 1 = riemannZeta 1 = (γ - log 4π)/2 ≠ 0`
   (`riemannZeta_ne_zero_of_one_le_re`, `Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/Nonvanishing.lean:411`) [Lean, read]!
   Mathlib's nonzero junk value at the pole $s = 1$ of $\zeta$ allows the internal predicate
   `ZeroFreeAll` to omit the exception $(\psi, s) = (1, 1)$, whereas the public statement
   `EveryCharacter` prudently restores the hypothesis $(\chi \neq 1 \lor s \neq 1)$ so that
   nothing in the final theorem statements depends on any junk value at $s = 1$ [Expository].
2. **All-character right half-plane (`UpperTheory.EveryCharacter` / `Claim.HalfPlane` / `DirichletLNonvanishingRightOfHalf`).**
   For every $N \ge 1$, every $\chi \bmod N$, and every $s \in \mathbb C$ with $\Re s > 1/2$, if
   $\chi \neq 1$ or $s \neq 1$, then $L(s,\chi) \neq 0$ [Lean, read]
   (`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/EveryCharacter.lean:13`;
   `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/HalfPlane.lean:3`; `src/Statements.lean:6`).
3. **Critical-strip targets (`PrimitiveDirichletLZerosOnLine`, `DirichletLZerosOnLine` / `Claim.Strip`)
   and global RH (`RiemannHypothesis` / `RiemannZetaZerosOnLine` / `Claim.Zeta`).** As stated in
   Chapter 1, Sections 1.2.1 and 1.3.1 [Lean, read] (`src/Statements.lean:3,10,14`;
   `Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/RiemannZeta.lean:185`).

### 2.3.4 Measurable fundamental domains for discrete quotients

An automorphic function on an adelic group is often invariant under left multiplication by its rational subgroup. Its square norm is taken on the space of orbits. To define that norm using a measure on the group, we need a measurable region that counts each orbit once. Discreteness gives small open sets in which each orbit has at most one point. Second countability lets us order countably many such sets and choose the first one that meets a given orbit.

**Example 1. Integer translations of the real line.** [Expository] Give $G=\mathbb R$ its usual topology, let $\Gamma=\mathbb Z$ act by addition, and use Lebesgue measure. The interval $(-1/2,1/2)$ isolates $0$ in the subgroup. For each $x\in\mathbb R$ subtract $m=\lfloor x\rfloor$. The result $r=x-m$ lies in $[0,1)$. Any other representative in $[0,1)$ differs from $r$ by an integer in $(-1,1)$, hence is $r$. For $x=-7/3$, the integer subtracted is $-3$ and the representative is $2/3$. Every integer maps to $0$. Including $1$ would count the integer orbit twice, while dropping $0$ from $[0,1)$ would miss it. The half-open interval is Borel and has length $1$. The floor and fractional-part functions are Borel, since the floor is constant on each half-open unit interval. With the measure constructed below, the quotient function with value $r$ at the orbit of $x$ has squared $L^2$ norm $\int_0^1 r^2\,dr=1/3$.

**Example 2. A skew lattice in the plane.** [Expository] Let $\Lambda=\mathbb Z(2,0)+\mathbb Z(1,3)$ act on $\mathbb R^2$ by addition, with Lebesgue area. Let $A$ be the linear map with columns $(2,0)$ and $(1,3)$. It is invertible and maps $\mathbb Z^2$ onto $\Lambda$. The open set $A((-1/2,1/2)^2)$ isolates $0$ in $\Lambda$. For $(x,y)=A(a,b)$ the coordinates are $b=y/3$ and $a=(x-b)/2$. Subtract $\lfloor a\rfloor(2,0)+\lfloor b\rfloor(1,3)$. The remaining point has both coordinates in $[0,1)$ in this basis. Two such points in the same orbit have integer differences in both coordinates, each strictly between $-1$ and $1$, so they coincide. The region $A[0,1)^2$ includes the edges with $a=0$ or $b=0$ subject to the half-open coordinate ranges. The edges with $a=1$ or $b=1$ reduce to the corresponding included edges. For instance, $(1,3)$ reduces to $(0,0)$. At $(-1/2,7/2)$ the coordinates are $(-5/6,7/6)$, so we subtract $-(2,0)+(1,3)=(-1,3)$ and obtain $(1/2,1/2)=A(1/6,1/6)$. The region is Borel because $A$ is a homeomorphism. Its coordinate reductions are Borel by the same floor construction. The determinant is $2\cdot3-0\cdot1=6$, so the area is $6$. The quotient function with value $a-\lfloor a\rfloor$ has squared $L^2$ norm $6\int_0^1\int_0^1 a^2\,da\,db=2$. Both examples satisfy the abstract group and measure hypotheses, including Hausdorffness, local compactness and two-sided Haar invariance. They illustrate the quotient construction in additive notation.

**Definitions.** Let $G$ be a topological group, $\Gamma$ a subgroup with the induced topology, and $Y$ the space of left orbits $\Gamma x$. Write $\pi(x)=\Gamma x$. A *Borel fundamental domain* here means a Borel set $F\subseteq G$ meeting every orbit in exactly one point. Equivalently, for each $x\in G$ there is exactly one $\gamma\in\Gamma$ with $\gamma x\in F$. The equivalence uses cancellation in $G$. In the Borel setting we equip $Y$ with the quotient $\sigma$-algebra. A set $B\subseteq Y$ is measurable precisely when $\pi^{-1}(B)$ is Borel in $G$.

**Local slice lemma.** [Lean, read] Suppose $\Gamma$ is discrete. There is an open identity neighbourhood $V$ such that $\pi$ is injective on $Vg$ for every $g\in G$.

**Proof.** The singleton $\{1\}$ is open in $\Gamma$, so choose an open $W$ in $G$ with $W\cap\Gamma=\{1\}$. The map $(v,w)\mapsto vw^{-1}$ is continuous at $(1,1)$. Its inverse image of $W$ contains a product of two open identity neighbourhoods. Intersect them to obtain $V$ with $VV^{-1}\subseteq W$. Set $U_g=Vg$. If $x,\gamma x\in U_g$, then $xg^{-1},\gamma xg^{-1}\in V$ and $(\gamma xg^{-1})(xg^{-1})^{-1}=\gamma$. Hence $\gamma\in W\cap\Gamma$ and $\gamma=1$. The right translate $Vg$ makes the difference cancel $g$ on the right even in a noncommutative group. In particular the translates $\gamma U_g$ for fixed $g$ are pairwise disjoint. [Pinned neighbourhood lemma](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/Algebra/Group/Basic.lean#L785-L791)

**Theorem.** [Lean, read] Suppose $G$ is second countable and $\Gamma$ is discrete. There is a Borel fundamental domain $F$. In any measurable structure on $G$ containing the open sets, the same $F$ is measurable and, for every measure $\mu$ on that structure, satisfies Mathlib's `IsFundamentalDomain` predicate for the left action of $\Gamma$.

**Proof.** The sets $U_g$ are open and contain their centres. From a countable basis, take every basic open set contained in at least one $U_g$ and select one such $U_g$ for each. Each $x$ lies in a basic open set contained in $U_x$, so the selected sets cover $G$. Enumerate the selected family as $U_0,U_1,\ldots$, repeating sets if the family is finite. The group identity guarantees a nonempty family.

For each $n$ let $S_n=\{x:\gamma x\in U_n\text{ for some }\gamma\in\Gamma\}$. This is the open union of the inverse translates of $U_n$ and is invariant under $\Gamma$. Multiplying $x$ by $\delta\in\Gamma$ replaces the candidate $\gamma$ by $\gamma\delta$, a bijective reindexing. Openness at this step holds for any index cardinality. Define

$$
F_n=U_n\setminus\bigcup_{k<n}S_k,\qquad F=\bigcup_{n\ge 0}F_n .
$$

Each $F_n$ is a difference of open sets and the countable union is Borel. Fix $x$ and take the least $n$ with $x\in S_n$, which exists by the cover. Some $\gamma$ has $\gamma x\in U_n$. For $k<n$ the orbit misses $S_k$, so $\gamma x\in F_n$. Conversely, if $\delta x\in F_j$, then $x\in S_j$ and $x\notin S_k$ for every $k<j$, by invariance. Thus $j=n$ and both $\gamma x$ and $\delta x$ lie in $U_n$. Apply the slice lemma at $\gamma x$ to $\delta\gamma^{-1}$ to obtain $\delta=\gamma$. This yields the exact disjoint union $G=\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\gamma F$. Every $x$ belongs to $\gamma^{-1}F$ for its unique multiplier $\gamma$. If two translates meet, the inverse multipliers of the common point have to agree.

For Mathlib's predicate, measurability gives null measurability, the pointwise cover implies an almost-everywhere cover, and actual disjointness implies almost-everywhere disjointness. This is the constructor `IsFundamentalDomain.mk'`. The abstract conclusion requires second countability and discreteness in a topological group. The adelic application also supplies local compactness and Hausdorffness. [Pinned countable cover](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/Bases.lean#L911-L944) · [Pinned fundamental-domain constructor](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Group/FundamentalDomain.lean#L65-L92)

**A measurable section and the quotient measure.** [Lean, read] In the Borel setting, let $F$ be the specific domain chosen by `stdDom`. Define $s(q)$ as the unique point of $F$ over $q$. The archive's `sec` starts with `q.out`, any representative, and multiplies it on the left by the unique subgroup element that sends it into $F$. Every choice of the starting point produces the same result by uniqueness in the fibre.

For a Borel set $C\subseteq G$, the pullback in $G$ of $s^{-1}(C)$ is the union, over $\gamma\in\Gamma$, of the sets $\{x:\gamma x\in C\cap F\}$. Indeed $s(\pi(x))\in C$ means that the unique representative of the orbit in $F$ belongs to $C$. The subgroup is countable because a discrete subspace of a second-countable space has countably many singletons in a countable subspace basis. The union is therefore Borel, since left translations are homeomorphisms. The quotient $\sigma$-algebra now makes $s$ measurable. Also $\pi\circ s$ is the identity, and for every measurable $B\subseteq Y$ one has $s(B)=F\cap\pi^{-1}(B)$, where $s(B)$ denotes the image. Thus $s$ is injective and sends measurable sets to Borel sets, as required by `measurableEmbedding_sec`. The proof uses only the algebraic property $\pi(q.\mathrm{out})=q$ of `Quotient.out`. [Pinned quotient measurable structure](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/MeasurableSpace/Constructions.lean#L130-L157)

For the quotient topology these measurable sets are exactly the Borel sets. The continuous projection pulls Borel sets back to Borel sets. In the other direction, the open sets $\pi(U_n)$ cover $Y$, and $\pi$ on $U_n$ is a homeomorphism onto its image. Indeed $\pi$ is open because the saturation of an open subset of $G$ is open. If $\pi^{-1}(B)$ is Borel, then $B\cap\pi(U_n)$ is Borel in $\pi(U_n)$ by that homeomorphism, hence Borel in $Y$ because $\pi(U_n)$ is open. The countable union gives $B$.

The same set $F$ enters `quotMeasure` by restriction followed by pushforward. Writing $\nu$ for this measure, we have

$$
\nu=\pi_*(\mu|_F),\qquad \nu(B)=\mu\bigl(F\cap\pi^{-1}(B)\bigr).
$$

In particular $\nu(Y)=\mu(F)$. For a left-invariant $\mu$, the inherited `quotMeasure_eq` permits replacement of $F$ by any `IsFundamentalDomain` for that measure. For an infinite $\Gamma$, unrestricted pushforward adds a copy of the same mass for every translate. In fact $\pi^{-1}(B)$ is the disjoint union of the translates of $F\cap\pi^{-1}(B)$, so $(\pi_*\mu)(B)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\nu(B)$ by left invariance. This is zero when $\nu(B)=0$ and infinite when $\nu(B)>0$. For finite $\Gamma$ the same sum is $|\Gamma|\nu(B)$. In the two examples, the unrestricted pushforward gives infinite mass to the whole quotient, whose restricted-pushforward mass is respectively $1$ and $6$.

**Remarks.**

- **Pointwise and almost-everywhere domains.** The Mathlib predicate asks for null measurability (agreement modulo null sets with a measurable set), an a.e. cover, and pairwise a.e. disjoint translates. With integer translations of the line, $[0,1]$ counts the integer orbit twice and $(0,1)$ misses it. Both satisfy the a.e. predicate for Lebesgue measure. For the closed interval the overlaps consist of endpoints, and for the open interval the uncovered set is $\mathbb Z$, a countable null set. The pointwise construction provides a function on every quotient class, including those whose orbits meet discarded boundaries.
- **What fails without discreteness.** Put the dense subgroup $\Gamma=\mathbb Q$ in $G=\mathbb R$, retaining the topology and Lebesgue measure of Example 1. For any nonempty open $U$ choose an interval $(a-r,a+r)\subseteq U$, with $r>0$. Choose a positive integer $m$ with $1/m<r$. Then $a$ and $a+1/m$ both lie in $U$ and are distinct members of the same orbit. Hence $U\cap(U+1/m)$ contains $a+1/m$, and every nonempty open set fails the slice condition. This proves failure of the local construction. Taking $\Gamma=\mathbb R$ in the same ambient group also fails the local test and has the singleton Borel domain $\{0\}$.
- **What integration needs.** For a nonnegative measurable $h$ on $Y$, the pushforward identity gives $\int h\,d\nu=\int_F h\circ\pi\,d\mu$ by checking indicators, simple functions and monotone approximation. The Bochner identity uses integrability, including a.e. strong measurability and a finite norm integral. A function on $G$ descends pointwise if it is constant on left orbits. Membership in `MemL2Quotient` additionally requires an a.e. strongly measurable descent with finite integral of its squared norm. The Hölder product and Bochner integrability estimates supply the pairing of two such classes. Right translations on $Y$ are well defined. The inherited measure-preserving theorem used for translated $L^2$ functions assumes both left and right invariance upstairs. Finite quotient mass, integrable majorants and interchanges of integrals require their own hypotheses.
- **The actual adelic parameters.** [Lean, read] In A2, take $G=\mathrm{GA}(D,V)$ and $\Gamma=\mathrm{GQ}(V)$ for $D$ of type `D7` and $V$ of type `HSp D`. The wrapper `exists_fundDom` accepts a specified Haar measure on this group. It receives second countability from the adelic topology theorem, discreteness from the rational subgroup theorem, and uses the Borel structure and the topological group instance on the unitary group over the finite-dimensional tensor algebra. Local compactness and Hausdorffness come with the adelic topology theorem. The consumer `exists_meas` obtains Haar measure using local compactness and calls the wrapper with `Measure.haar`. In the generic $L^2$ path, `stdDom` chooses directly from the pointwise theorem, giving one set before $\mu$ is selected. The existing section identities express `descend Γ f q` as $f(s(q))$ for left-invariant $f$ and identify $f(s(q)g)$ with right translation of the descent.
- **Sources and reading scope.** The production proofs are in A2, `src/Development/AbelianVarietyTheory/Proofs/IwasawaDecomposition/ExistsIsFundamentalDomain.lean:19–114`. The adelic wrappers are `LocallyCompactSecondCountableT2.lean:16–17` and `DiscreteCountableGQ.lean:18–21` in the same directory. The section and measure interface is `src/Development/AbelianVarietyTheory/Definitions/Foundations/AestronglyMeasurableQuotMeasureIffLift.lean:265–318`, with subgroup countability at :258–261. A2's joined archive SHA-256 is `9631b4f020199e580ba86178cb56c6e9b09f690c2369a80dc4334aa93f49c7fc`. The direct Mathlib arguments use the pin `0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474`. The comparison with the Borel structure of the quotient topology and the unrestricted-pushforward formula are textbook arguments. The deeper adelic topology and arithmetic supplies retain their named-input status [Lean]. Bekka, de la Harpe and Valette prove the classical strict Borel assertion for right orbits with a countable cover from $\sigma$-compactness. Inversion gives the left-orbit version on locally compact second-countable groups. The two computations and the failed slice example are textbook material. [Classical comparison, Proposition B.2.4(i), p. 353](https://ncatlab.org/nlab/files/BekkaHarpeValetteOnKashdanPropertyT.pdf) · [A2 source record](../sources/SOURCES.md#1-the-lean-archive-primary-source)

### 2.3.5 Why the adelic unitary group is locally compact and second countable

**Motivation.** The [Borel fundamental-domain construction](#234-measurable-fundamental-domains-for-discrete-quotients) for a discrete subgroup needs countably many open slices on the ambient group. The adelic application also needs local compactness to select a Haar measure on that same group. The restricted product puts all but finitely many coordinates in prescribed compact open rings. At each point only finitely many local neighbourhoods need to be chosen, and the possible finite lists of exceptions form a countable family.

**Example 1. A compact open neighbourhood over $\mathbb Q$.** [Expository] Let $a$ be the finite adèle with every coordinate equal to $1/6$. Its exceptional primes are precisely $2$ and $3$. The normalized valuations $v_2(1/6)$ and $v_3(1/6)$ equal $-1$, and $v_p(1/6)=0$ at every other prime. A compact open neighbourhood of $a$ is

$$
U=(1/6+4\mathbb Z_2)\times(1/6+3\mathbb Z_3)\times\prod_{p\ne2,3}\mathbb Z_p.
$$

Each coset is compact and open by translation and nonzero scalar multiplication from $\mathbb Z_p$. The product is compact in the chart for the exceptions $\{2,3\}$, and the chart is open. At $2$ and $3$ the perturbations have valuation at least $2$ and $1$, respectively. Both bounds are strictly higher than the valuation of the centre, so every point in $U$ has exceptional set exactly $\{2,3\}$. Inside $U$ we can also prescribe the coordinate at $5$ by replacing $\mathbb Z_5$ with $1/6+25\mathbb Z_5$. The new list of prescribed coordinates is $\{2,3,5\}$. The actual exceptional set stays $\{2,3\}$, since the fifth-prime centre has valuation $0$ and its perturbations have valuation at least $2$. Changing only that coordinate to $1/6+25=151/6$ meets the new condition. Changing it to $1/6+5=31/6$ leaves the point in $U$, while the perturbation has valuation $1$ and fails the new condition.

**Example 2. An adelic Hermitian line.** [Expository] Take $F=\mathbb Q$, $E=\mathbb Q(i)$, conjugation $\sigma$ and Gram matrix $(1)$. Write $R=\mathbb A_{\mathbb Q}$. In the basis $1,i$, the coefficient ring $R\otimes_{\mathbb Q}E$ consists of pairs $(r,s)$ with multiplication $(r,s)(u,v)=(ru-sv,rv+su)$ and conjugation $(r,s)\mapsto(r,-s)$. The group in matrix and inverse coordinates is

$$
\{((r,s),(r,-s)):r^2+s^2=1\}.
$$

Indeed the Hermitian equation says $(r,-s)(r,s)=(r^2+s^2,0)=(1,0)$, which also determines the two-sided inverse. With the rational numbers embedded diagonally, $(r,s)=(3/5,4/5)$ gives $9/25+16/25=1$. The inverse coordinates are $(3/5,-4/5)$, and the product has imaginary coordinate $(-12+12)/25=0$. Both pairs are integral at all finite primes except $5$, where both coordinates have valuation $-1$. At the real place take the closed intervals $[1/2,7/10]$ and $[7/10,9/10]$ for $r,s$. At $5$ use $3/5+5\mathbb Z_5$ and $4/5+5\mathbb Z_5$, with $\mathbb Z_p$ at every other finite prime for each variable. Use this product for the matrix coordinate and its conjugate for the inverse coordinate. These compact sets contain open neighbourhoods of the displayed coordinates, using the interiors of the real intervals. Intersecting their product with the closed equations for the group yields a compact neighbourhood in the group.

**Definitions.** For a family of spaces $X_i$ and open sets $B_i\subseteq X_i$, write $X=\prod_i'(X_i,B_i)$ for the tuples with $x_i\in B_i$ outside a finite set. Its topology is the final topology of the inclusions of the charts

$$
X_S=\prod_{i\in S}X_i\times\prod_{i\notin S}B_i \qquad (S\subseteq I\text{ finite}),
$$

each with the product topology. Here final means that a set is open exactly when its intersection with each chart is open in that chart. The finite adèle ring $\mathbb A_{F,f}$ uses $X_v=F_v$ and $B_v=\mathcal O_v$ at the finite places of a number field $F$. The full ring $\mathbb A_F$ is the product of $\mathbb A_{F,\infty}=\prod_{v\mid\infty}F_v$ and $\mathbb A_{F,f}$.

**Restricted-product lemma.** If $I$ is countable and all $X_i$ are second countable, then $X$ is second countable. If all $X_i$ are Hausdorff, then $X$ is Hausdorff. If all $X_i$ are locally compact Hausdorff and all but finitely many $B_i$ are compact, then $X$ is locally compact. The last assertion allows arbitrary $I$.

**Proof.** The charts cover by the definition of a restricted tuple. For finite $S,T$, the intersection $X_S\cap X_T$ inside $X_T$ imposes $x_i\in B_i$ at the finitely many indices $T\setminus S$. It is open there. In either chart the intersection has the product topology of $X_{S\cap T}$, since each coordinate is either unchanged or given its subspace topology. Thus any open subset of $X_S$ intersects every other chart in an open set there. Each chart is therefore an open subspace of $X$. Coordinate projections from $X$ are continuous by restriction to the charts. Two distinct points differ in some coordinate, where disjoint open neighbourhoods pull back to separate them in $X$.

In Lean, the principal filter of the complement of $S$ gives this chart: its members lie in $B_i$ at every index outside $S$. These are exactly the principal filters above the cofinite filter. The use of open embeddings is the same in the following countability argument.

For the basis, choose a countable basis in each $X_i$ and restrict it to $B_i$ for the tail factors. In each chart choose finitely many coordinates and one basic open set for each of them. Allow all values in the other factors of that chart. These cylinders form a basis of the chart. When $I$ is countable its finite subsets form a countable set because each is the image of a finite tuple in $I$. There are countably many cylinders in each chart and countably many charts. The union of their bases is a countable basis of $X$ by openness of the charts.

For compact neighbourhoods fix $x\in X$ and an open neighbourhood $W$ of $x$. Begin with a chart containing $x$ and enlarge its finite exceptional set to include the indices at which $B_i$ is noncompact. Choose a product cylinder about $x$ inside the intersection of that chart with $W$. Enlarge its exceptional set again to include the finitely many coordinates restricted by the cylinder. Write $S$ for the final finite set. For each $i\in S$ choose a compact neighbourhood $C_i$ in $X_i$ inside the restriction imposed by the cylinder and the earlier chart. At a newly included tail coordinate the old requirement $x_i\in B_i$ is still imposed. It permits this choice because $B_i$ is open in $X_i$. At unrestricted coordinates of $S$ choose any compact neighbourhood. Then

$$
C=\prod_{i\in S}C_i\times\prod_{i\notin S}B_i
$$

is compact by the product theorem. It contains an open neighbourhood of $x$ in $X_S$, hence in $X$, and is contained in $W$. The pinned group proof constructs a compact neighbourhood in a principal chart and transports it along an open embedding. Its group instance upgrades compact neighbourhood existence to the locally compact space instance. [Sutherland, Definition 25.1 and Propositions 25.5–25.6](https://math.mit.edu/classes/18.785/2021fa/LectureNotes25.pdf) · [Pinned principal-chart compactness](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/Algebra/RestrictedProduct/TopologicalSpace.lean#L248-L277)

**Theorem.** [Lean, read] For number fields $F,E$ with $E$ an $F$-algebra, let $\sigma:E\to E$ be an $F$-algebra endomorphism, $M\ge0$, and $J\in\operatorname{Mat}_M(E)$. The archive's $\mathrm{HA}(\mathbb A_F,\sigma,J)$ is locally compact, second countable and Hausdorff ($T_2$) in its specified topology. The matrix argument works over any locally compact Hausdorff second-countable commutative topological $F$-algebra $R$ when $E/F$ is finite-dimensional, with the scalar multiplication and topology on $R$ as supplied. In the number-field case finite dimension over $F$ follows because a finite $\mathbb Q$-spanning set of $E$ also spans over $F$. The Hermitian application takes $\sigma$ to be the CM conjugation and $J$ the chosen Gram matrix.

**Proof.** The number field $F$ embeds as a set in $\mathbb Q^{[F:\mathbb Q]}$ via a basis, so $F$ and its subring $\mathcal O_F$ are countable. The ring $\mathcal O_F$ is Noetherian. The finite subsets of its underlying set map onto its ideals by taking their spans. The height-one spectrum injects into the set of ideals by sending a prime to its underlying ideal. Thus the actual finite-place index set is countable. This is the proof of `countable_heightOneSpectrum`.

For each finite place use the local arithmetic supply: the completion is a complete discretely valued field with an open discrete valuation ring and finite residue field. The archive uses the complete-DVR and finite-residue criterion to get a proper metric space and then compactness of its valuation ring. The criterion uses [total boundedness of the integer ring from finite residue field](#2351-finite-residue-fields-and-compact-unit-balls) together with completeness. The integer ring is the closed unit ball, and openness follows from the valuation topology. A proper metric space is locally compact and second countable. Its compact closed balls about a fixed point of integer radii exhaust it, and finite $1/m$-nets on these balls, for positive integers $m$, give a countable dense set. Balls of positive rational radii about that set form a countable basis. The metric gives Hausdorffness. The restricted-product lemma now applies to $\mathbb A_{F,f}$. For continuity of the ring operations, put any two inputs in one common chart by taking the union of their exception sets. In this open chart, coordinatewise addition and multiplication are continuous and take values in the same chart because the $\mathcal O_v$ are subrings. Negation works on every chart. At the finitely many infinite places the completions are $\mathbb R$ or $\mathbb C$. The source obtains second countability by isometric embedding into $\mathbb C$, and local compactness by the finite-product instance. Products give the three properties on $\mathbb A_F$. Here $F$ acts through its diagonal map: an element of $F$ is a quotient of algebraic integers with nonzero denominator, and such a denominator belongs to only finitely many prime ideals of $\mathcal O_F$.

Now put $A=R\otimes_F E$ with the archive's module topology. If $e_1,\ldots,e_d$ is an $F$-basis of $E$, then $1\otimes e_j$ is an $R$-basis of $A$. On a pure tensor $r\otimes e$, where $e=\sum_j a_j e_j$, the coordinates are $(r\,a_j)_j$ using the scalar map $F\to R$. This formula is $F$-balanced and extends additively to the tensor product. The inverse sends $(r_j)_j$ to $\sum_j r_j\otimes e_j$. The coordinate map and its inverse are $R$-linear. The module topology is the finest topology making the module operations continuous. Finite products of $R$ have that topology. For any topology making the module operations continuous, the sum of the coordinate scalar multiples of the fixed basis vectors is continuous from the ordinary product $R^d$. The product itself has continuous module operations. Transport by the basis therefore gives exactly the homeomorphism to $R^d$ in `nonempty_homeomorph`. In these coordinates multiplication uses the finitely many structure constants of $E$, so $A$ is a topological ring. The map $\sigma_R=1\otimes\sigma$ is given by a fixed matrix of scalars and is continuous. The root proof identifies its `sigR` with the base change of the linear map $\sigma$ by checking pure tensors and extending by additivity. Thus $A$ inherits the three topological properties of $R$.

Let $P=\operatorname{Mat}_M(A)$ with its finite product topology. The matrix unit group has the topology induced by the injection $g\mapsto(g,g^{-1})$ into $P\times P$, with the second factor carrying opposite multiplication in Lean and the same underlying topology. The image consists exactly of $(g,h)$ with $gh=hg=1$. These are two closed equations since matrix multiplication is continuous and the singleton identity is closed. Inversion in the image swaps the two coordinates and multiplication sends $((g,h),(g',h'))$ to $(gg',h'h)$. Both operations are continuous. This realizes `lcsc_GL` using `Units.isClosedEmbedding_embedProduct`.

Let $J_R$ be $J$ with each entry mapped by $e\mapsto1\otimes e$. Write $c_\sigma(g)=(\sigma_R(g_{ij}))^{\mathsf T}$. By definition $\mathrm{HA}(R,\sigma,J)$ is the subgroup of matrix units where $c_\sigma(g)J_R g=J_R$, with the subspace topology from the units. Entrywise application of a ring homomorphism followed by transpose reverses multiplication and preserves the identity. Hence the equation holds at the identity and is preserved by products. Multiplying the equation on the left by $c_\sigma(g^{-1})$ and on the right by $g^{-1}$ gives the same equation for the inverse. The map $g\mapsto c_\sigma(g)J_Rg$ is continuous because each entry is a finite sum of products of continuous functions. Its equality to $J_R$ is a closed condition. The group is therefore a closed subspace of the matrix-and-inverse model, itself closed in $P\times P$. A closed subspace of a locally compact Hausdorff space has compact neighbourhoods by intersection with compact neighbourhoods in the ambient space. Intersecting a countable ambient basis with a subspace gives a countable basis, and Hausdorffness also passes to subspaces. For $M=0$ both matrix factors and the group are singletons. The argument includes singular $J$, since the inverse coordinate supplies a two-sided inverse independently of the Gram equation.

This is the production chain `tens_props` → `lcsc_GL` → `closed_UJ` → `lcsc_sub` in `UpperTheory.Reformulated.B.Yzd4.lcsc_HA`. The wrapper `lcsc_adelicUnitary` installs the three instances on the adèle ring and calls `lcsc_HA` on that ring. [A2 source record and exact locations](../sources/SOURCES.md#adelic-topology-for-chapter-2) · [Pinned units range and closedness](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/Algebra/Group/Basic.lean#L1409-L1428)

**Remarks.**

- **Where the properties enter.** Fix the original $D:\mathrm{D7}$ and $V:\mathrm{HSp}(D)$. The call inserts $D.\mathrm{host}.F$, $D.\mathrm{host}.E$, $D.\mathrm{sp}.\mathrm{cm}.\mathrm{sig}$, $V.\mathrm{rank}$ and $V.\mathrm{gram}$ into the theorem. This gives the triple on the very group $G=\mathrm{GA}(D,V)$ used for integration. `exists_fundDom` uses second countability and separately receives the discreteness of $\mathrm{GQ}(V)$, the Borel structure and the topological group structure. It accepts any $\mu$ with $\mu.\mathrm{IsHaarMeasure}$ and returns a domain with the almost-everywhere predicate. `exists_meas` uses local compactness, the Borel structure and the topological group instance to select `Measure.haar` and calls that wrapper. Hausdorffness is part of the triple and is used in the closed-matrix argument. In the inherited Borel quotient construction, `stdDom` chooses an exact domain before a measure is selected, `sec` picks its unique representative in each orbit, and `quotMeasure` pushes forward the restriction to that domain. The predecessor received same-source review for these statements. The archive's `record_nonempty` and `Model.line D` give an instance on the original objects, whose group contains the identity. [A2 call sites](../sources/SOURCES.md#adelic-topology-for-chapter-2)
- **Dropping countability.** [Expository] On an uncountable set $I$ take the discrete groups $X_i=\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$ and $B_i=\{0\}$. Every chart is finite discrete and every singleton is open in the restricted product. The point supported at each single index is distinct, so a basis needs uncountably many singletons. The space still has compact open singleton neighbourhoods and is Hausdorff. An uncountable index can also yield all three properties, for instance when every factor is a singleton.
- **Dropping compactness or openness.** [Expository] Take countably infinitely many copies of $\mathbb R$ with $B_i=\mathbb R$. The ordinary product is second countable and Hausdorff. Any neighbourhood of zero contains a cylinder with an unrestricted coordinate, so a compact neighbourhood would have a continuous projection equal to all of $\mathbb R$, which is impossible. For a separate test of openness take $X_i=\mathbb R$ and $B_i=\{0\}$ for positive integers $i$, retaining compactness and the principal-chart final topology. The zero chart ceases to be open. Given any sequence of neighbourhoods $N_i$ of zero, the $i$-th axis meets $N_i$ in a neighbourhood of zero. Choose a nonzero point $t_i$ there. Any subset of the chosen points is closed in the final topology because its intersection with each finite chart is finite and closed. The complement of all the $t_i$ is an open neighbourhood of zero that contains none of the $N_i$. Hence there is no countable local basis. If $K$ were a compact neighbourhood, repeating the selection with $N_i=K$ would give an infinite closed discrete subspace of $K$, which is impossible. Coordinate projections still separate points. Modifying finitely many $B_i$ leaves the restricted product and its final topology unchanged, since one may restrict to charts whose finite lists include all the modified indices.
- **Classical scope.** [Paper] Sutherland gives the restricted charts and product-compactness argument, the locally compact Hausdorff adèle ring, and the finite-basis tensor topology preceding Proposition 25.10. Conrad constructs the closed affine-point topology over Hausdorff rings, explains the inverse coordinate for units, and obtains a countable base and local compactness for separated finite-type adelic spaces. In the present affine group, expand $gh=hg=1$ and $c_\sigma(g)J_Rg=J_R$ into polynomial equations in $2dM^2$ coordinates over $F$ using the structure constants of $E/F$. The affine-point topology is exactly the finite-coordinate topology just proved. The classical construction thus covers the adelic theorem. [Sutherland, Lecture 25](https://math.mit.edu/classes/18.785/2021fa/LectureNotes25.pdf) · [Conrad, Proposition 2.1, §3 and §4](https://math.stanford.edu/~conrad/papers/adelictop.pdf)
