#### 2.3.5.1 Finite residue fields and compact unit balls

**Motivation.** The [finite adèle construction](#235-why-the-adelic-unitary-group-is-locally-compact-and-second-countable) uses the integer ring of each completion as a compact open set. A finite residue field divides the integer ring into finitely many first-level balls. Each deeper layer has the same number of choices per ball, and completeness supplies limits for Cauchy sequences that pass successively into these smaller balls.

**Example 1 ($\mathbb Q_3$).** [Expository] In the completion of $\mathbb Q$ at $(3)$, set $|3|_3=1/3$. The integer ball is $\mathbb Z_3$, with maximal ideal $3\mathbb Z_3$ and residue representatives $0,1,2$. At depth two the representatives $a_0+3a_1$ are $0,1,\ldots,8$. Their nine disjoint cosets are closed balls of radius $1/9$. At any depth $n$ the representatives are $0,\ldots,3^n-1$ and the radius is $3^{-n}$. For instance $5/2$ lies in the ball centred at $7$ at depth two, since $5/2-7=-9/2$. At depth three it lies in the ball centred at $16=1+2\cdot3+3^2$, since $5/2-16=-27/2$. Thus at open radius $1/8$ the nine depth-two centres suffice. They are necessary because distinct centres have distance at least $1/3$, and an open ultrametric ball of radius $1/8$ cannot contain two such points.

**Example 2 (a totally ramified quadratic extension).** [Expository] Set $L=\mathbb Q_3(\pi)$, where $\pi^2=3$. The element $3$ has odd $3$-adic order and therefore is no square in $\mathbb Q_3$, so the extension has degree two. Use the absolute value extending $|\cdot|_3$. On the basis $(1,\pi)$ the determinant of multiplication by $a+b\pi$ is $a^2-3b^2$. The two terms have distinct $3$-adic orders whenever both are nonzero, one even and one odd. Using $v_3(0)=+\infty$, the formula for the absolute value in terms of the field norm gives

$$
v_L(a+b\pi)=\min\{2v_3(a),1+2v_3(b)\},
\qquad |a+b\pi|_L=3^{-v_L(a+b\pi)/2}.
$$

Completeness and the formula for the absolute value follow from the extension theorem for a complete DVR. Approximating both coefficients by rationals gives a dense copy of $\mathbb Q(\sqrt3)$ with the restricted metric, so $L$ is also its completion at the place defined by this absolute value. [Sutherland, Lecture 10, Theorem 10.4, pp. 1–2](https://math.mit.edu/classes/18.785/2021fa/LectureNotes10.pdf)

The integer ring is $\mathbb Z_3+\mathbb Z_3\pi$. Multiplication by $\pi$ sends $(a,b)$ to $(3b,a)$, so the maximal ideal is $3\mathbb Z_3+\mathbb Z_3\pi$. Reduction sends $a+b\pi$ to $a\bmod 3$. The representatives are $0,1,2$ and the residue field is $\mathbb F_3$. The equation $3=\pi^2$ gives ramification index two, and the residue degree is one. At depth $n$, the ideal $\pi^n\mathcal O_L$ consists of coefficients

$$
a\in3^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\mathbb Z_3,\qquad
b\in3^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\mathbb Z_3.
$$

Indeed $\pi^{2j}=3^j$ and $\pi^{2j+1}=3^j\pi$. The ranges $0\le a<3^{\lceil n/2\rceil}$ and $0\le b<3^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}$ give exactly $3^n$ cosets, each of closed radius $3^{-n/2}$. At depth two the nine centres are $a_0+a_1\pi$ with $a_i\in\{0,1,2\}$ and radius $1/3$. At depth three the 27 centres have the form $a_0+a_1\pi+3a_2$ and radius $3^{-3/2}$. At depth four there are 81 centres with radius $1/9$. For $x=5/2+(7/2)\pi$, the depth-three centre is $7+2\pi$. The difference $-9/2+(3/2)\pi$ has order $\min(4,3)=3$. The depth-four centre is $7+8\pi$, giving $x-(7+8\pi)=-(9/2)(1+\pi)$ of order $4$. At open radius $1/8$ the depth-four centres suffice since $1/9<1/8$. Distinct centres have distance at least $3^{-3/2}>1/8$, as follows by squaring, so all 81 are needed. The same residue size gives different covering numbers at this radius because $|\pi|_L=3^{-1/2}$. The prime defining this completion has absolute ideal norm $3$. Indeed global algebraic integers map into $\mathcal O_L$: if an element had absolute value greater than one, the leading term in its monic equation over $\mathbb Z$ would strictly dominate all the others. Reduction lands in $\mathbb F_3$, and $0,1,2$ already map onto it. The kernel is the prime of the indicated place. The pinned completion normalization therefore takes $|\pi|=1/3$ in the quadratic example, giving radii $3^{-n}$ with the same partitions and valuation topology. The count of 81 at radius $1/8$ uses the absolute value extending $|\cdot|_3$. [Pinned completion norm](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/NumberTheory/NumberField/Completion/FinitePlace.lean#L253-L273)

**Definitions.** Let $K$ carry a nontrivial rank-one multiplicative valuation. Use the real absolute value $|\cdot|$ given by the order-preserving embedding of its value group and the metric $d(x,y)=|x-y|$. The induced uniformity is the original valuation uniformity. In the discrete case write $\operatorname{ord}:K^\times\to\mathbb Z$ for the normalized additive order and extend it by $\operatorname{ord}(0)=+\infty$. Put $\mathcal O=\{x:|x|\le1\}$, $\mathfrak m=\{x:|x|<1\}$ and $k=\mathcal O/\mathfrak m$. A uniformizer $\pi$ generates $\mathfrak m$ and has order one. Write $c=|\pi|\in(0,1)$, so $|x|=c^{\operatorname{ord}(x)}$ for $x\ne0$. Total boundedness of the integer ball means it has a finite cover by open balls centred there at every real radius $\varepsilon>0$. The metric on $K$ is proper if every closed ball of finite radius is compact. [Pinned rank-one metric](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/Algebra/Valued/NormedValued.lean#L144-L199)

**Lemma (finite quotients at every depth).** [Lean, read for finiteness] For a discrete valuation ring $\mathcal O$ with residue field of size $q$, every quotient $\mathcal O/\mathfrak m^n$, $n\ge0$, has $q^n$ elements. A set $D$ of residue representatives containing $0$ gives representatives

$$
S_n=\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a_j\pi^j:a_j\in D\right\},
\qquad S_0=\{0\}.
$$

**Proof.** Choose the unique $a_0\in D$ reducing to $x\bmod\mathfrak m$ and write $x=a_0+\pi x_1$ with $x_1\in\mathcal O$. Iterating $n$ times gives $x-s=\pi^n x_n$ for some $s\in S_n$. Two strings first differing at index $j<n$ have difference $\pi^j$ times a unit, because after division by $\pi^j$ the residue is $a_j-a'_j\bmod\mathfrak m$, which is nonzero. Their difference has order exactly $j$, so they represent distinct classes. At $n=0$ the quotient by $\mathfrak m^0=\mathcal O$ is a singleton.

Mathlib proves finiteness by induction on $n$. The reduction $\mathcal O/\mathfrak m^{n+1}\to\mathcal O/\mathfrak m^n$ is onto and its kernel is the image of $\mathfrak m^n$ in the source quotient. Multiplication by $\pi^n$ identifies $k$ additively with $\mathfrak m^n/\mathfrak m^{n+1}$. It is onto, and cancelling $\pi^n$ tests membership in $\mathfrak m$. This last quotient identifies with the kernel, and each fibre is a translate of the kernel. In Lean, <code>DoubleQuot.quotQuotEquivQuotOfLE</code> supplies the lower quotient, <code>Ideal.quotEquivPowQuotPowSuccEquiv</code> and <code>Ideal.powQuotPowSuccEquivMapMkPowSuccPow</code> supply the kernel, and <code>Finite.of_ideal_quotient</code> combines their finiteness. The digit formula gives a specific section of the residue map at each depth. Mathlib's cover uses <code>Quotient.out</code> for its choice of one representative per class. [Pinned finite-quotient induction](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/Algebra/Valued/LocallyCompact.lean#L103-L126) · [Pinned principal-ideal equivalence](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/RingTheory/Ideal/IsPrincipalPowQuotient.lean#L40-L86)

**Theorem (finite nets and compactness).** [Lean, read] For the original nontrivial rank-one valuation with discrete valuation ring $\mathcal O$, the integer ball is totally bounded exactly when $k$ is finite. The integer ball is compact exactly when $k$ is finite and $K$ is complete. In this setting compactness of the integer ball, local compactness of $K$ and properness of its valuation metric are equivalent.

**Proof.** For $x\in K$, the inequality $|x|\le c^n$ is equivalent to $x/\pi^n\in\mathcal O$. Thus for $n\ge0$, $\mathfrak m^n=\overline B(0,c^n)$ as sets in the field. Its cosets partition $\mathcal O$ into the closed balls $\overline B(s,c^n)$ for $s\in S_n$. For every real $\varepsilon>0$, choose $n\ge0$ with $c^n<\varepsilon$. Each of these closed balls is contained in the open $\varepsilon$-ball about its centre. This proves total boundedness. With the least such $n$, the number $q^n$ is the minimum cover size. For $n>0$, distinct centres have distance at least $c^{n-1}\ge\varepsilon$, and an open ultrametric $\varepsilon$-ball can contain at most one of them. At $n=0$ one ball is necessary because $\mathcal O$ is nonempty. Conversely, a finite open cover at radius $c$ gives finitely many residues, since two elements at distance less than $c$ from a common centre differ by an element of $\mathfrak m$. Neither direction of the total boundedness criterion uses completeness.

The integer ball is closed in $K$, so field completeness makes the subspace complete. To see compactness from its finite nets, start with any sequence and successively retain infinitely many terms in one coset at each depth, inside the coset chosen at the preceding depth. A diagonal subsequence with increasing indices is Cauchy, since its tail at depth $n$ has mutual distances at most $c^n$. It converges in $\mathcal O$. Sequential compactness implies compactness in a metric space. Conversely, compactness makes $\mathcal O$ complete and totally bounded. Any Cauchy sequence in $K$ has a tail in a translate of $\mathcal O$, so $K$ is complete as well.

The integer ball is open since for each $x\in\mathcal O$ the open unit ball about $x$ lies in $\mathcal O$. When $\mathcal O$ is compact, the translates $x+\pi^N\mathcal O$ give compact neighbourhoods inside every neighbourhood of $x$. Conversely, any compact neighbourhood of zero contains a closed ball $\pi^N\mathcal O$ at some sufficiently small radius. This closed subset is compact, and multiplication by $\pi^{-N}$ makes $\mathcal O$ compact. To prove properness, for every finite $R\ge0$ choose $m\ge0$ with $R\le c^{-m}$. The closed ball of radius $R$ about zero is a closed subset of the compact set $\pi^{-m}\mathcal O$. Translation gives compactness at every centre. Properness in turn gives compactness of $\overline B(0,1)$. The field itself is unbounded, since $|\pi^{-m}|=c^{-m}$, and hence is noncompact. [Pinned proof of total boundedness](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/Algebra/Valued/LocallyCompact.lean#L130-L176) · [Pinned compactness and properness criteria](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Topology/Algebra/Valued/LocallyCompact.lean#L327-L358)

**Remarks.**

- **The actual Mathlib steps.** The finite-residue direction of <code>totallyBounded_iff_finite_residueField</code> chooses an irreducible of the DVR as uniformizer. It chooses $n$ with $|\pi|^n<\varepsilon$, takes the finite range of <code>Quotient.out</code> on $\mathcal O/\mathfrak m^n$, and uses <code>Ideal.univ_eq_iUnion_image_add</code> to cover by translates of the $n$-th ideal. <code>Irreducible.maximalIdeal_pow_eq_setOfPred_le_v_coe_pow</code> identifies the ideal with the valuation cutoff for the closed ball. The proof includes that closed ball in the open ball at the requested radius. Lines 327–338 combine the cover with completeness of the integer subtype. Lines 340–358 pass between the integer subtype, the radius-one closed ball and the proper metric using scaling in the nontrivially normed field. In the reverse direction the library criterion under rank one also infers the DVR from compactness. Our finite-net proof starts with the DVR supplied by the caller. [Pinned source ledger](../sources/SOURCES.md#finite-residue-fields-and-compact-unit-balls)
- **The same finite place in the archive.** [Lean, read] In <code>LcscAdelicUnitaryGroup.lean:40–45</code> let $F$ be the caller's number field and $v\in\operatorname{HeightOneSpectrum}(\mathcal O_F)$. The field is exactly <code>v.adicCompletion F</code> and the integer ring is <code>v.adicCompletionIntegers F</code>. The completion supplies <code>CompleteSpace</code>, the original adic valuation supplies rank one, and the DVR instance and <code>Formalism.UG.P.glint_finite_residueField v</code> give the other inputs on that ring. These arithmetic supplies enter as named [Lean] inputs. The call to the complete-DVR and finite-residue criterion produces <code>ProperSpace</code> there. Lines 47–49 apply <code>properSpace_iff_compactSpace_integer</code> to that very instance, giving compactness of the integer subtype. Lines 63–70 use its compactness as a subset of the same completion, with openness from <code>Valued.isOpen_valuationSubring</code>. Lines 72–77 use the same proper metric for local second countability. The parent section gives the [restricted-product, closed matrix model and original Haar and fundamental-domain consumers](#235-why-the-adelic-unitary-group-is-locally-compact-and-second-countable). [A2 and Mathlib call sites](../sources/SOURCES.md#finite-residue-fields-and-compact-unit-balls)
- **Without completeness.** [Expository] Give $\mathbb Q$ the $3$-adic metric. Its integer ball $\mathbb Z_{(3)}$ has uniformizer $3$ and residue field $\mathbb F_3$, so all the finite nets still exist. Start with $u_1=1$ and choose $u_{n+1}=u_n+3^n t_n$ with $t_n\in\{0,1,2\}$ so that $u_{n+1}^2\equiv7\pmod{3^{n+1}}$. The digit is unique because $(u_n^2-7)/3^n+2u_n t_n\equiv0\pmod3$ and $u_n\equiv1\pmod3$. The quadratic term $3^{2n}t_n^2$ is divisible by $3^{n+1}$. The $u_n$ are Cauchy in the ball. A rational limit would square to $7$, impossible by the odd exponent of $7$ in the rational square-root equation. The ball is noncompact. Translation and scaling then rule out local compactness and properness.
- **With infinite residue field.** [Expository] On $\mathbb Q((t))$ use the order of the first nonzero coefficient and $|t|=1/2$. It is complete: a Cauchy sequence has a tail with a common lower bound on the exponents, and each coefficient eventually stabilizes. These eventual coefficients give a Laurent series to which the sequence converges at every depth. Its integer ring $\mathbb Q[[t]]$ has residue field $\mathbb Q$. Distinct constant series have distance one. Each open ball of radius one contains at most one of them, so the integer ball fails total boundedness and compactness. A compact neighbourhood would scale to a compact unit ball, so the field fails local compactness and its metric fails properness.
- **Classical scope.** [Paper] Sutherland's Proposition 9.6 assumes the absolute value of a discrete valuation and gives the equivalence with completeness and finite residue field. It uses completeness to identify the integer ring with the inverse limit of its finite quotients. Lemma 9.4 gives the equivalence with compactness of all closed balls under a nontrivial absolute value by field scaling. The present section spells out the finite nets in the original metric and identifies the Lean implementations and their callers. [Sutherland, Lecture 8, Propositions 8.10–8.11](https://math.mit.edu/classes/18.785/2021fa/LectureNotes8.pdf) · [Sutherland, Lecture 9, Lemma 9.4, Corollary 9.5 and Proposition 9.6, pp. 1–2](https://math.mit.edu/classes/18.785/2021fa/LectureNotes9.pdf)

## 2.4 Lemmas and proofs

We now prove every implication in the chain from `ZeroFreeAll` to the five theorems of
`src/Main.lean`. Each proof is self-contained and follows the exact mathematical reasoning checked by
Lean.

### 2.4.1 Bad Euler factors and passage from primitive to all characters

**Lemma 2.1 (Non-vanishing of single Euler factors on $\Re s > 0$).** *Let $M \ge 1$, let
$\psi$ be a Dirichlet character modulo $M$, let $p$ be a prime number, and let $s \in \mathbb C$
satisfy $\Re s > 0$. Then*
$$1 - \psi(p)\,p^{-s} \neq 0.$$
*(Lean: `Claim.factor_ne_zero`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ClaimStrip.lean:7`,
and inline inside `UpperTheory.Top.imprimitive`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/Imprimitive.lean:21`.)* [Lean, read]

*Proof.* Suppose for a contradiction that $1 - \psi(p) p^{-s} = 0$, so $\psi(p) p^{-s} = 1$. Since
$p$ is prime, $p > 1$; since $-\Re s < 0$, we have
$$\lvert p^{-s} \rvert = p^{-\Re s} < 1.$$
Every value of a Dirichlet character lies in $\{0\} \cup \{z \in \mathbb C : \lvert z \rvert = 1\}$,
so $\lvert \psi(p) \rvert \le 1$. Multiplying gives
$$1 = \lvert \psi(p)\,p^{-s} \rvert = \lvert \psi(p) \rvert \cdot \lvert p^{-s} \rvert
\le 1 \cdot p^{-\Re s} < 1,$$
a contradiction. $\square$

To relate $L(s,\chi)$ to $L(s,\chi^*)$ across the whole complex plane (away from the pole at
$s = 1$), Mathlib provides the level-change identity (`LFunction_changeLevel`,
`Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/DirichletContinuation.lean:150`) [Lean]: if $d \mid N$ ($d, N \ge 1$)
and $\psi \bmod d$, then for every $s \in \mathbb C$ satisfying $\psi \neq 1 \lor s \neq 1$,
$$L\bigl(s, \operatorname{changeLevel}_{d \mid N}(\psi)\bigr)
= L(s,\psi) \prod_{p \mid N} \bigl(1 - \psi(p)\,p^{-s}\bigr),$$
where the product runs over the distinct prime divisors of $N$. (Notice that if a prime $p \mid N$
also divides $d$, then $\psi(p) = 0$ when $d > 1$, so the factor $1 - \psi(p) p^{-s}$ is simply
$1$; only primes dividing $N$ and coprime to $d$ contribute nontrivial factors. On $\Re s > 1$ the
identity is immediate by comparing Euler products, and Mathlib extends it to $\mathbb C \setminus \{1\}$
— and to $s = 1$ when $\psi \neq 1$ — by continuity on the dense open set $\mathbb C \setminus \{1\}$.)

**Proposition 2.2 (Primitive characters imply all characters on $\Re s > 1/2$).** *Assume
`ZeroFreeAll`: for every primitive Dirichlet character $\psi$ and every $s \in \mathbb C$ with
$\Re s > 1/2$, we have $L(s,\psi) \neq 0$. Then `EveryCharacter` holds: for every $N \ge 1$, every
Dirichlet character $\chi \bmod N$, and every $s \in \mathbb C$ with $\Re s > 1/2$ and
$(\chi \neq 1 \lor s \neq 1)$, we have $L(s,\chi) \neq 0$.*
*(Lean: `UpperTheory.Top.imprimitive`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/Imprimitive.lean:5`.)* [Lean, read]

*Proof.* Fix $N \ge 1$, $\chi \bmod N$, and $s \in \mathbb C$ with $\Re s > 1/2$ and
$(\chi \neq 1 \lor s \neq 1)$. Let $d = \operatorname{cond}(\chi) \ge 1$, so $d \mid N$, and let
$\chi^* \bmod d$ be the primitive character inducing $\chi$, so that
$\chi = \operatorname{changeLevel}_{d \mid N}(\chi^*)$.

First, applying `ZeroFreeAll` to the bundled primitive character $\mathrm{PChar.of}(\chi) = (d, \chi^*)$
gives $L(s, \chi^*) \neq 0$.

Second, we verify the hypothesis $(\chi^* \neq 1 \lor s \neq 1)$ needed to invoke the level-change
identity. If $s \neq 1$, the second disjunct holds immediately. If $\chi \neq 1$, then we must
have $\chi^* \neq 1$: indeed, if $\chi^* = 1 \bmod d$, then inducing the trivial character from
level $d$ to level $N$ yields the trivial character $1 \bmod N$ (`changeLevel_one`,
`Mathlib/NumberTheory/DirichletCharacter/Basic.lean:220`), contradicting $\chi \neq 1$.

Therefore the level-change formula applies at $s$ and gives
$$L(s,\chi) = L(s,\chi^*) \prod_{p \mid N} \bigl(1 - \chi^*(p)\,p^{-s}\bigr).$$
Here $L(s,\chi^*) \neq 0$, and since $\Re s > 1/2 > 0$, Lemma 2.1 shows that every factor
$1 - \chi^*(p) p^{-s}$ in the finite product is nonzero. A product of nonzero complex numbers is
nonzero, so $L(s,\chi) \neq 0$. $\square$

*(Remark on the proof.)* Notice that even if one weakened `ZeroFreeAll` to exclude the pole
$(\psi, s) = (\mathrm{PChar.one}, 1)$, the proof above would still go through word for word: when
$(\chi \neq 1 \lor s \neq 1)$ holds, we just checked that $(\chi^* \neq 1 \lor s \neq 1)$ holds,
and if $\chi^* = 1$ with $d = 1$ that forces $s \neq 1$ [Expository].

### 2.4.2 Functional-equation reflection in the critical strip

We turn to the deduction of the critical-line theorems from `EveryCharacter`. First we check that
the archimedean Gamma factors never vanish in the open right half-plane $\Re s > 0$.

**Lemma 2.3 (Non-vanishing of Gamma factors on $\Re s > 0$).** *For any modulus $M \ge 1$, any
Dirichlet character $\varphi \bmod M$, and any $t \in \mathbb C$ with $\Re t > 0$, we have
$\gamma_\varphi(t) \neq 0$.*
*(Lean: `Formalism.VW.gammaFactor_ne_zero`, `src/Formalism/GammaFactorNonvanishing/CritLine.lean:6`,
and `Claim.gamma_ne_zero`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ClaimStrip.lean:35`.)* [Lean, read]

*Proof.* For $\Re w > 0$, both $\pi^{-w/2} = \exp(-\frac{w}{2}\log\pi)$ and $\Gamma(w/2)$ (since
$\Re(w/2) > 0$) are nonzero, so $\Gamma_{\mathbb R}(w) \neq 0$ (`Gammaℝ_ne_zero_of_re_pos`). Now
$\varphi$ is either even or odd. If $\varphi$ is even, $\gamma_\varphi(t) = \Gamma_{\mathbb R}(t)$,
which is nonzero since $\Re t > 0$. If $\varphi$ is odd, $\gamma_\varphi(t) = \Gamma_{\mathbb R}(t + 1)$,
which is nonzero since $\Re(t + 1) = \Re t + 1 > 1 > 0$. $\square$

Next we prove that zeros of a primitive $L$-function in the open strip $0 < \Re s < 1$ reflect
cleanly under $(s, \psi) \mapsto (1 - s, \psi^{-1})$. For a primitive character $\chi \bmod M$,
Mathlib proves the global functional equation (`IsPrimitive.completedLFunction_one_sub`,
`Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/DirichletContinuation.lean:284`) [Lean]: for every $s \in \mathbb C$,
$$\Lambda(1 - s, \chi) = M^{s - 1/2}\,\varepsilon(\chi)\,\Lambda(s, \chi^{-1}),$$
where $\varepsilon(\chi) = \tau(\chi) / (i^{\mathfrak a} M^{1/2})$ is the root number (`rootNumber`,
`:272`, with $\mathfrak a = 0$ for $\chi$ even and $\mathfrak a = 1$ for $\chi$ odd). Watch how
applying this equation to $\chi = \psi^{-1}$ rather than to $\psi$ spares us from proving
anything at all about the Gauss sum $\tau(\psi)$ or the root number $\varepsilon(\psi)$!

**Lemma 2.4 (Reflection of zeros for primitive characters).** *Let $M \ge 1$, let $\psi \bmod M$
be a primitive Dirichlet character, and let $s \in \mathbb C$ satisfy $0 < \Re s < 1$. If
$L(s,\psi) = 0$, then $L(1 - s, \psi^{-1}) = 0$.*
*(Lean: `Claim.prim_reflect`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ClaimStrip.lean:42`,
and inline inside `Formalism.VW.critLine`, `src/Formalism/GammaFactorNonvanishing/CritLine.lean:17–26`.)* [Lean, read]

*Proof.* Since $0 < \Re s < 1$, we have $\Re(1 - s) = 1 - \Re s > 0$; in particular $s \neq 0$ and
$1 - s \neq 0$.

1. Because $s \neq 0$, Definition 2.2(2) gives $L(s,\psi) = \Lambda(s,\psi) / \gamma_\psi(s)$.
   By Lemma 2.3 applied at $t = s$ ($\Re s > 0$), the denominator $\gamma_\psi(s)$ is nonzero.
   Since $L(s,\psi) = 0$, it follows that $\Lambda(s,\psi) = 0$.
2. Because $\psi \bmod M$ is primitive, we have $\operatorname{cond}(\psi^{-1}) = \operatorname{cond}(\psi) = M$,
   so $\psi^{-1} \bmod M$ is also primitive. Applying the functional equation of $\psi^{-1}$ at
   $s$, and using $(\psi^{-1})^{-1} = \psi$, we obtain
   $$\Lambda(1 - s, \psi^{-1}) = M^{s - 1/2}\,\varepsilon(\psi^{-1})\,\Lambda(s, (\psi^{-1})^{-1})
   = M^{s - 1/2}\,\varepsilon(\psi^{-1})\,\Lambda(s,\psi) = 0.$$
3. Because $1 - s \neq 0$, Definition 2.2(2) applied to $\psi^{-1}$ at $1 - s$ gives
   $$L(1 - s, \psi^{-1}) = \frac{\Lambda(1 - s, \psi^{-1})}{\gamma_{\psi^{-1}}(1 - s)}
   = \frac{0}{\gamma_{\psi^{-1}}(1 - s)} = 0.$$
   (Even without checking $\gamma_{\psi^{-1}}(1 - s) \neq 0$, $0 / z = 0$ holds unconditionally; of
   course Lemma 2.3 also shows $\gamma_{\psi^{-1}}(1 - s) \neq 0$ since $\Re(1 - s) > 0$.) $\square$

We also record the converse direction of Proposition 2.2 inside the critical strip: any zero of an
imprimitive $L(s,\chi)$ in $0 < \Re s < 1$ is already a zero of its underlying primitive
$L$-function $L(s,\chi^*)$.

**Lemma 2.5 (Stripping bad Euler factors at a strip zero).** *Let $N \ge 1$, let $\chi \bmod N$ be
any Dirichlet character with primitive character $\chi^* \bmod d$ ($d = \operatorname{cond}(\chi)$),
and let $s \in \mathbb C$ satisfy $0 < \Re s < 1$. If $L(s,\chi) = 0$, then $L(s,\chi^*) = 0$.*
*(Lean: `Claim.prim_zero`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ClaimStrip.lean:23`.)* [Lean, read]

*Proof.* Since $\Re s < 1$, we have $s \neq 1$. Hence the level-change formula applies to $\chi^*$
for $d \mid N$ and gives
$$0 = L(s,\chi) = L(s,\chi^*) \prod_{p \mid N} \bigl(1 - \chi^*(p)\,p^{-s}\bigr).$$
Because $\Re s > 0$, Lemma 2.1 implies that $1 - \chi^*(p) p^{-s} \neq 0$ for every prime $p \mid N$.
Dividing by the nonzero finite product yields $L(s,\chi^*) = 0$. $\square$

Combining Lemmas 2.4 and 2.5 immediately yields both critical-strip theorems of `src/Main.lean`.

**Theorem 2.6 (Critical-line theorems for primitive and general Dirichlet $L$-functions).** *Assume
`EveryCharacter`. Let $N \ge 1$, let $\chi \bmod N$ be a Dirichlet character, and let $s \in \mathbb C$
satisfy $0 < \Re s < 1$ and $L(s,\chi) = 0$. Then $\Re s = 1/2$. In particular, both
`PrimitiveDirichletLZerosOnLine` and `DirichletLZerosOnLine` hold.*
*(Lean: `Formalism.VW.critLine`, `src/Formalism/GammaFactorNonvanishing/CritLine.lean:11`;
`Claim.strip`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ClaimStrip.lean:59`;
`critAll` and `claimAll`, `src/Supplements/Kernels/MainTheorems.lean:11,15`.)* [Lean, read]

*Proof.* Since $0 < \Re s < 1$, we have $s \neq 0$ and $s \neq 1$. Compare $\Re s$ with $1/2$ by
trichotomy:

- **Case $\Re s > 1/2$.** Since $s \neq 1$, the hypothesis $(\chi \neq 1 \lor s \neq 1)$ holds, so
  `EveryCharacter` applied directly to $(N, \chi, s)$ gives $L(s,\chi) \neq 0$, contradicting
  $L(s,\chi) = 0$.
- **Case $\Re s < 1/2$.** Let $d = \operatorname{cond}(\chi) \ge 1$ and let $\chi^* \bmod d$ be
  the primitive character inducing $\chi$ (if $\chi$ is already primitive, $d = N$ and $\chi^* = \chi$).
  By Lemma 2.5, $L(s, \chi^*) = 0$. Since $\chi^*$ is primitive and $0 < \Re s < 1$, Lemma 2.4
  gives $L(1 - s, (\chi^*)^{-1}) = 0$. Now $\Re(1 - s) = 1 - \Re s > 1 - 1/2 = 1/2$, and
  $1 - s \neq 1$ because $s \neq 0$. Applying `EveryCharacter` to $(d, (\chi^*)^{-1}, 1 - s)$
  yields $L(1 - s, (\chi^*)^{-1}) \neq 0$, again a contradiction.
- **Case $\Re s = 1/2$.** This is the only remaining possibility. $\square$

### 2.4.3 Two derivations of the global Riemann Hypothesis

Mathlib's `RiemannHypothesis` (`Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/RiemannZeta.lean:185`) asks for more
than the critical strip $0 < \Re s < 1$: it states that for *every* $s \in \mathbb C$ with
$\zeta(s) = 0$, if $s$ is not of the form $-2(n + 1)$ ($n \in \mathbb N$) and $s \neq 1$, then
$\Re s = 1/2$ [Lean, read]. The archive contains *two* complete proofs of this global statement
from `EveryCharacter`:

1. `Formalism.VW.rh_of_every` (`src/Formalism/GammaFactorNonvanishing/CritLine.lean:29`) + `Formalism.VW.rh_of_right` (`src/Formalism/GammaFactorNonvanishing/RhOfRight.lean:5`),
   which is the proof term `rhOfAll` actually used by the first two theorems of `src/Main.lean:4,6`
   [Lean, read];
2. `Claim.zeta` (`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ClaimStrip.lean:75`), which sits inside `claimAll` (`src/Supplements/Kernels/MainTheorems.lean:15`) and
   uses the symmetric completed zeta function instead of the cosine-Gamma functional equation
   [Lean, read].

Both proofs are short and instructive, so we give both.

**Theorem 2.7 (Global Riemann Hypothesis from `EveryCharacter`).** *Assume `EveryCharacter`. Then
Mathlib's `RiemannHypothesis` (and definitionally `RiemannZetaZerosOnLine` and `Claim.Zeta`) holds:
for every $s \in \mathbb C$, if $\zeta(s) = 0$, $s \notin \{-2(n + 1) : n \in \mathbb N\}$, and
$s \neq 1$, then $\Re s = 1/2$.*
*(Lean: `Formalism.VW.rh_of_every`, `src/Formalism/GammaFactorNonvanishing/CritLine.lean:29`;
`Formalism.VW.rh_of_right`, `src/Formalism/GammaFactorNonvanishing/RhOfRight.lean:5`;
`Claim.zeta`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ClaimStrip.lean:75`.)* [Lean, read]

*First proof (via the asymmetric functional equation, mirroring `rh_of_every` and `rh_of_right`).*
First specialize `EveryCharacter` to $N = 1$ and the unique character $1 \bmod 1$. Since
`LFunction (1 : DirichletCharacter ℂ 1) = riemannZeta` (`LFunction_modOne_eq`,
`Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/DirichletContinuation.lean:67`), we obtain the right half-plane
non-vanishing property for $\zeta$:
$$\forall w \in \mathbb C,\quad \Re w > \tfrac12 \ \text{and}\ w \neq 1 \implies \zeta(w) \neq 0.$$

Now let $s \in \mathbb C$ satisfy $\zeta(s) = 0$, $s \notin \{-2(n + 1) : n \in \mathbb N\}$, and
$s \neq 1$. First note that $s \neq 0$, because $\zeta(0) = -1/2 \neq 0$ (`riemannZeta_zero`,
`Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/RiemannZeta.lean:151`). By trichotomy on $\Re s$ relative to $1/2$:

- If $\Re s > 1/2$, then since $s \neq 1$, the half-plane property immediately gives
  $\zeta(s) \neq 0$, contradicting $\zeta(s) = 0$.
- If $\Re s = 1/2$, we are done.
- Suppose $\Re s < 1/2$, and set $w = 1 - s$. Then $\Re w = 1 - \Re s > 1/2$. Furthermore:
  - $w \neq 1$, because $1 - s = 1$ would give $s = 0$;
  - $w \neq -n$ for every $n \in \mathbb N$, because $\Re w > 1/2 > 0 \ge -n = \Re(-n)$.
  Mathlib's asymmetric functional equation `riemannZeta_one_sub`
  (`Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/RiemannZeta.lean:178`) states that whenever $w \neq 1$ and
  $w \neq -n$ for all $n \in \mathbb N$,
  $$\zeta(1 - w) = 2\,(2\pi)^{-w}\,\Gamma(w)\,\cos\Bigl(\frac{\pi w}{2}\Bigr)\,\zeta(w).$$
  Since $1 - w = s$ and $\zeta(s) = 0$, the left-hand side is $0$. On the right-hand side:
  - $2(2\pi)^{-w} \neq 0$ since $2\pi > 0$;
  - $\Gamma(w) \neq 0$ since $\Re w > 1/2 > 0$ (`Complex.Gamma_ne_zero_of_re_pos`);
  - $\zeta(w) \neq 0$ by the right half-plane property applied to $w$ ($\Re w > 1/2$, $w \neq 1$).
  Hence the remaining factor $\cos(\pi w / 2)$ must vanish. In $\mathbb C$, $\cos z = 0$ if and
  only if $z = (2k + 1)\pi / 2$ for some integer $k \in \mathbb Z$ (`Complex.cos_eq_zero_iff`).
  Cancelling $\pi / 2 \neq 0$ gives $1 - s = w = 2k + 1$, that is, $s = -2k$ with $k \in \mathbb Z$.
  Taking real parts gives $-2k = \Re s < 1/2$, which for an integer $k$ forces $k \ge 0$:
  - if $k \le -1$, then $-2k \ge 2 > 1/2$, impossible;
  - if $k = 0$, then $s = 0$, contradicting $s \neq 0$;
  - if $k \ge 1$, then $m := k - 1 \in \mathbb N$ satisfies $s = -2k = -2(m + 1)$, contradicting
    the hypothesis that $s$ is not a trivial zero!
  Every branch of $\Re s < 1/2$ is impossible, so $\Re s = 1/2$. $\square$

*Second proof (via the completed zeta function, mirroring `Claim.zeta`).*
Let $\zeta(s) = 0$ with $s \notin \{-2(n + 1) : n \in \mathbb N\}$ and $s \neq 1$. Again $s \neq 0$
since $\zeta(0) = -1/2$. Split into three regions according to $\Re s$:

1. **The open strip $0 < \Re s < 1$.** Rewriting $\zeta(s) = L(s, 1 \bmod 1)$ and applying
   Theorem 2.6 (`Claim.strip` at $N = 1$) gives $\Re s = 1/2$.
2. **The closed half-plane $\Re s \ge 1$.** Mathlib's classical non-vanishing theorem
   `riemannZeta_ne_zero_of_one_le_re` (`Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/Nonvanishing.lean:411`)
   gives $\zeta(s) \neq 0$, contradicting $\zeta(s) = 0$. (Notice that `EveryCharacter` itself
   would also rule out $\Re s \ge 1$ since $s \neq 1$; `Claim.zeta` simply invokes Mathlib's
   unconditional lemma here.)
3. **The closed left half-plane $\Re s \le 0$.** First check that $\Gamma_{\mathbb R}(s) \neq 0$
   (under Mathlib's convention where $\Gamma_{\mathbb R}$ vanishes precisely at its classical poles
   $0, -2, -4, \dots$): if $\Gamma_{\mathbb R}(s) = 0$, then $s = -2m$ for some $m \in \mathbb N$
   (`Gammaℝ_eq_zero_iff`); $m = 0$ gives $s = 0$ (impossible), and $m = n + 1$ gives
   $s = -2(n + 1)$ (excluded as a trivial zero). Since $s \neq 0$, the identity
   $\zeta(s) = \Lambda_\zeta(s) / \Gamma_{\mathbb R}(s)$ together with $\zeta(s) = 0$ and
   $\Gamma_{\mathbb R}(s) \neq 0$ forces $\Lambda_\zeta(s) = 0$. By the symmetric functional
   equation $\Lambda_\zeta(1 - s) = \Lambda_\zeta(s)$ (`completedRiemannZeta_one_sub`,
   `Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/RiemannZeta.lean:106`), we get $\Lambda_\zeta(1 - s) = 0$.
   Because $\Re s \le 0$, we have $\Re(1 - s) = 1 - \Re s \ge 1$; in particular $1 - s \neq 0$,
   so $\zeta(1 - s) = \Lambda_\zeta(1 - s) / \Gamma_{\mathbb R}(1 - s) = 0$. That contradicts
   $\zeta \neq 0$ on $\Re(1 - s) \ge 1$ (`riemannZeta_ne_zero_of_one_le_re`). $\square$

## 2.5 Remarks

### 2.5.1 Why two parallel sets of reductions exist in the archive

A reader browsing the archive will notice redundancy in this layer [Lean, read]:

- `CritLine.lean` and `RhOfRight.lean` supply `rhOfAll` (`src/Main.lean:4,6`) and `critAll`
  (`src/Main.lean:12`), while `ClaimStrip.lean` supplies `Claim.strip` (`src/Main.lean:10`) as well as a
  second proof `Claim.zeta` of RH inside `claimAll` (`src/Supplements/Kernels/MainTheorems.lean:15`).
- As noted in `notes/proof-map.md` (P2), there is even a third derivation of RH from
  `EveryCharacter`, `UpperTheory.riemannHypothesis_of_everyCharacter`
  (`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/RiemannHypothesisOfEveryCharacter.lean:215`),
  which `src/Main.lean` never imports [Lean].

In an AI-assisted codebase of 175,187 modules, small glue files were evidently written more than
once as target statements were tidied up into `src/Statements.lean` [Expository]. From a verification
standpoint the duplication is benign — and in fact helpful: `RhOfRight.lean` (`62` lines) and
`ClaimStrip.lean` (`104` lines, via `HalfPlane.lean`) import **only Mathlib**, with zero archive
dependencies, so one can read and type-check the passage from `Claim.HalfPlane` to all five
final theorem shapes against stock Mathlib in seconds [Lean, read].

### 2.5.2 What Dirichlet GRH does and does not imply for Dedekind zeta functions

Once `EveryCharacter` (and hence GRH for all primitive Dirichlet $L$-functions) is established,
what follows automatically for number fields $K / \mathbb Q$ by classical algebraic number theory,
even without formalizing Dedekind zeta functions? The answer splits sharply along abelian versus
non-abelian Galois groups [Expository]:

1. **Abelian number fields: RH for $\zeta_K(s)$ follows.** By the Kronecker–Weber theorem, every
   finite abelian extension $K / \mathbb Q$ lies inside some cyclotomic field $\mathbb Q(\zeta_N)$.
   Viewing $\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb Q(\zeta_N)/\mathbb Q) \cong (\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z)^\times$,
   the character group $X(K)$ of $\operatorname{Gal}(K/\mathbb Q)$ identifies with a subgroup of
   Dirichlet characters modulo $N$, and class field theory (see Washington, *Introduction to
   Cyclotomic Fields*, 2nd ed., Springer, 1997, Theorem 4.3, or Davenport, *Multiplicative Number
   Theory*, 3rd ed., Springer, 2000, Chapter 4) gives the exact factorization
   $$\zeta_K(s) = \prod_{\chi \in X(K)} L(s, \chi^*),$$
   where $\chi^*$ is the primitive Dirichlet character attached to $\chi$. Because this is a
   *honest product with positive integer exponents* (exponent $1$ for each $\chi \in X(K)$), no
   factor is in the denominator: for $\chi = 1$ we get $\zeta(s)$ (whose single pole at $s = 1$
   supplies the simple pole of $\zeta_K(s)$ at $s = 1$), and for $\chi \neq 1$ the factor
   $L(s,\chi^*)$ is entire. Therefore the zeros of $\zeta_K(s)$ in $0 < \Re s < 1$ are simply the
   union of the zeros of $L(s,\chi^*)$ ($\chi \in X(K)$), all of which lie on $\Re s = 1/2$ by
   Theorem 2.6. (In particular, for the abelian CM "host" fields $E \subset \mathbb Q(\zeta_N)$
   that appear in Chapters 4, 5, and 11, $\zeta_E(s)$ factors into primitive Dirichlet
   $L$-functions — a fact that is central to why finite Fourier analysis on the character group of
   $E$ works in Chapter 5, though of course Chapters 4–11 never assume GRH when proving it!)
2. **Non-abelian number fields: ERH does *not* follow from Dirichlet GRH.** For an arbitrary
   Galois extension $K / \mathbb Q$, Artin's decomposition writes $\zeta_K(s)$ as a product of
   Artin $L$-functions of irreducible representations of $\operatorname{Gal}(K/\mathbb Q)$, and
   Brauer's induction theorem expresses each Artin $L$-function as an alternating product
   $\prod_i L(s, \psi_i)^{n_i}$ of **Hecke $L$-functions of intermediate subfields $F_i \subset K$**
   with $n_i \in \mathbb Z$. Two obstructions block any deduction from `EveryCharacter`: first,
   the base fields $F_i$ are in general nontrivial extensions of $\mathbb Q$, so $L(s,\psi_i)$ is
   a Hecke (or principal Dirichlet) $L$-function over $F_i$, not over $\mathbb Q$; second, even if
   one knew non-vanishing on $\Re s > 1/2$ for some factors, negative exponents $n_i < 0$ mean a
   zero of one factor could in principle cancel against another unless Artin's holomorphy
   conjecture or zero-freeness of *all* factors on $\Re s > 1/2$ is known first. Thus the
   Extended Riemann Hypothesis (ERH) genuinely lies outside the scope of the five theorems of
   `src/Main.lean`, matching Alpöge's remark that he expects ERH only "with more time"
   [Paper; remark p. 1].

### 2.5.3 Masullo's counterpart

In Masullo's draft *The Music of the Primes*, the mathematical counterpart of this chapter sits in
**Chapter 3 ("Idelic Zeta Functions", pp. 117–154)** and in the overview of the intended endgame
in **Section 1.8.5.1 (p. 55)**, pointing toward the unfinished **Chapter 13 (pp. 521–522)**
[Paper; Masullo pp. 55, 117–154, 521–522].

- **Tate's thesis instead of Hurwitz zeta functions.** Where Mathlib defines $L(s,\chi)$ over
  $\mathbb Q$ as a linear combination of Hurwitz zeta functions and proves the functional equation
  via finite Fourier transforms on $\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z$ (`ZMod.completedLFunction_one_sub_even` /
  `_odd`), Masullo works over an arbitrary number field $F$ following J. T. Tate's 1950 thesis
  ("Fourier analysis in number fields, and Hecke's zeta-functions", published in J. W. S. Cassels
  and A. Fröhlich, eds., *Algebraic Number Theory*, Academic Press, London, 1967, pp. 305–347) and
  D. Ramakrishnan and R. J. Valenza, *Fourier Analysis on Number Fields* (Springer, 1999) [Paper;
  Masullo p. 11, §1.1.1, and p. 123, Prop. 3.2.1.3]. By working directly with continuous
  characters $\chi$ of the idele class group $C_F = \mathrm{GL}_1(\mathbb A_F)/F^\times$, every
  character is automatically primitive (its ramification at each finite place $v$ determines the
  exact local Euler factor of the completed Hecke $L$-function $L(s,\chi)$, Definition 3.2.1.2,
  p. 123, and of the finite Hecke $L$-function $L_f(s,\chi)$, Definition 3.2.1.4, pp. 123–124),
  and the global idelic Mellin transform of an adelic theta function yields meromorphic
  continuation and the functional equation in one stroke (Theorem 3.2.2.1, p. 124) [Paper; Masullo
  pp. 123–124].
- **Same half-plane-to-strip logic, shifted by $1/2$.** Masullo normalizes his spectral variable so
  that the critical line sits at $\Re s = 0$, writing $L_f(s + 1/2, \chi)$. In the sketch from his
  letter to Sarnak (§1.8.5.1, equation (1.8.5.1), p. 55), the intended output of his trace formula
  is that for every unitary character $\chi$ of $C_F$, the logarithmic derivative
  $$\frac{L_f'(s + 1/2, \chi)}{L_f(s + 1/2, \chi)} - \delta\Bigl(\frac{1}{s + 1/2} + \frac{1}{s - 1/2}\Bigr)$$
  is holomorphic on the open right half-plane $\Re s > 0$ (which is $\Re(s + 1/2) > 1/2$ in
  classical coordinates!), so $L_f(w,\chi)$ has neither zeros nor uncancelled poles on $\Re w > 1/2$
  [Paper; Masullo p. 55]. Passing from $\Re w > 1/2$ to all of $0 < \Re w < 1$ is then the exact
  same classical functional-equation reflection $w \mapsto 1 - w$ (i.e. $s \mapsto -s$ after
  accounting for archimedean Gamma factors). As recorded in Chapter 1 (Section 1.8), Chapter 13 of
  the draft currently stops at restating the trace formula (Theorem 13.3.1.1, p. 522) and has not
  yet written out this final deduction [Paper; Masullo pp. 521–522].

### 2.6.3 The Möbius inverse on the open half-plane

The strict inequality branch of `LFunction_ne_zero_of_one_le_re`, listed in [§2.6.2](#262-mathlib-declarations-used-by-these-proofs), proves nonvanishing by constructing a reciprocal. In a product of Dirichlet series the terms indexed by $a$ and $b$ contribute at $ab$. This suggests finding an inverse at the coefficient level first, then proving that the grouped coefficients really sum to the product of the two series.

**Two examples.** Let $\chi_4$ take values $1,-1$ on $1,3\bmod 4$ and vanish on even integers. Put $b(n)=\mu(n)\chi_4(n)$, with $\mu(1)=1$, $\mu(p)=-1$ and $\mu(p^2)=0$ for primes $p$. In each coefficient calculation the second argument ranges over the divisors in increasing order.

- At $1$ the coefficient is $\chi_4(1)b(1)=1$.
- At $3$ it is $\chi_4(3)b(1)+\chi_4(1)b(3)=-1+1=0$.
- At $9$ it is $\chi_4(9)b(1)+\chi_4(3)b(3)+\chi_4(1)b(9)=1-1+0=0$.
- At $15$ it is $\chi_4(15)b(1)+\chi_4(5)b(3)+\chi_4(3)b(5)+\chi_4(1)b(15)=-1+1+1-1=0$.
- At $6$ it is $\chi_4(6)b(1)+\chi_4(3)b(2)+\chi_4(2)b(3)+\chi_4(1)b(6)=0+0+0+0$.

For an imprimitive example, induce $\chi_4$ to modulus $12$ as in [§2.3.1](#231-conductors-level-change-and-bundled-primitive-characters). The resulting $\psi$ takes values $1,1,-1,-1$ on $1,5,7,11\bmod 12$ and vanishes off the units. Put $c=\mu\psi$. Then $\psi(1)c(1)=1$. At $7$ the coefficient is $\psi(7)c(1)+\psi(1)c(7)=-1+1=0$. At $35$ it is $\psi(35)c(1)+\psi(7)c(5)+\psi(5)c(7)+\psi(1)c(35)=-1+1+1-1=0$. At $15$ it is $\psi(15)c(1)+\psi(5)c(3)+\psi(3)c(5)+\psi(1)c(15)=0+0+0+0$. The modulus change affects the reciprocal coefficient itself. We have $b(3)=1$ and $c(3)=0$.

**Definitions.** For functions on the positive integers, Dirichlet convolution is $(f\ast g)(n)=\sum_{ab=n}f(a)g(b)$. Its identity is $\delta$, with $\delta(1)=1$ and all other values zero. In $(f\ast\delta)(n)$ only $b=1$ contributes. The Möbius function is zero on integers divisible by a prime square and equals $(-1)^r$ on a product of $r$ distinct primes, including the empty product at $1$. Write $D_f(s)=\sum_{n\ge1}f(n)n^{-s}$ in the region of absolute convergence. For $n>0$, $n^{-s}=\exp(-s\log n)$ uses the real logarithm. Mathlib uses sequences on $\mathbb N$ with the zeroth series term set to zero independently of the zeroth coefficient. The zeroth convolution coefficient is also zero. [DLMF §27.5, Eqs. (27.5.1)–(27.5.2)](https://dlmf.nist.gov/27.5)

<a id="dirichlet-coefficient-inverse"></a>

**Lemma (coefficient inverse) [Lean, read].** For a Dirichlet character $\chi$ modulo $N\ge1$, one has $\chi\ast(\mu\chi)=\delta$, where $\mu\chi$ is pointwise multiplication.

**Proof.** The nonzero terms of $\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(d)$ correspond to subsets of the distinct prime divisors of $n$. The signed subset sum is a product of one factor $1-1$ for each such prime. This gives $1$ at $n=1$ and $0$ elsewhere, proving $\mathbf 1\ast\mu=\delta$ for the constant sequence $\mathbf 1$. Twisting both inputs of a convolution by $\chi$ multiplies every summand by $\chi(a)\chi(b)=\chi(n)$. The complete multiplicativity holds off the units as well. If $ab$ is a nonunit modulo $N$, at least one factor is a nonunit, and its character value is zero. Consequently $\chi\ast(\mu\chi)=\chi(\mathbf 1\ast\mu)=\chi\delta=\delta$, since $\chi(1)=1$. This calculation uses multiplication by $\chi(n)$ even when that value is zero. $\square$

<a id="dirichlet-coefficient-comparison"></a>

**Lemma (absolute convergence) [Lean, read].** A bound $|f(n)|\le Cn^{x-1}$ with $C\ge0$ gives absolute convergence at $\sigma=\Re s>x$.

**Proof.** The complex exponential gives $|n^{-s}|=n^{-\sigma}$, so each term is bounded in modulus by $Cn^{-(\sigma+1-x)}$. For $q>1$, the block $2^j\le n<2^{j+1}$ contributes at most $2^j (2^j)^{-q}=2^{-j(q-1)}$ to $\sum n^{-q}$. The geometric sum of these bounds is finite. This proves the comparison, including bounded coefficients when $x=1$. $\square$

<a id="strict-half-plane-reciprocal"></a>

**Theorem (strict-half-plane reciprocal) [Lean, read].** Let $\chi$ be a complex Dirichlet character modulo $N\ge1$. Assume the continued $L(s,\chi)$ has the series agreement $L(s,\chi)=D_\chi(s)$ for $\Re s>1$. In this half-plane $L(s,\chi)D_{\mu\chi}(s)=1$, and hence $L(s,\chi)\ne0$.

**Proof.** The units modulo $N$ have finite order, so the corresponding character values are roots of unity and have modulus $1$. The other residues give zero. This bounds $|\chi(n)|$ by $1$, and $|\mu(n)|\le1$ follows from its definition. The comparison lemma gives absolute convergence for $D_\chi$ and $D_\mu$. The additional termwise inequality $|\mu(n)\chi(n)n^{-s}|\le|\mu(n)n^{-s}|$ gives absolute convergence of the twist. Applying the [multiplicative Cauchy product theorem](#multiplicative-cauchy-product) and coefficient inverse yields $D_\chi(s)D_{\mu\chi}(s)=D_\delta(s)=1$. Substitute the series agreement. A zero of $L(s,\chi)$ would make the product zero.

For the Hurwitz definition, series agreement follows by grouping the absolutely convergent series into residue classes $j=0,\ldots,N-1$. Each class consists of the positive values of $Nm+j$ for integers $m\ge0$. The factorization $(Nm+j)^{-s}=N^{-s}(m+j/N)^{-s}$ gives the finite Hurwitz combination from the Hurwitz series formula. At $j=0$ the zero term is omitted. DLMF (25.15.3) uses the range $1$ through $N-1$ for $N>1$, where the omitted residue has character value zero. The modulus-one case uses the separate convention below. For the construction and series identification, see [Chapter 1, §1.3.2](01-statement-and-fidelity.md#132-from-the-finite-hurwitz-sum-to-the-classical-dirichlet-l-function). General Hurwitz analytic continuation in `Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/HurwitzZeta.lean` enters as a named input [Lean]. $\square$ [DLMF §25.15(i), Eqs. (25.15.1), (25.15.3)](https://dlmf.nist.gov/25.15)

<a id="mobius-inverse-modulus-one"></a>

**Remark (modulus one).** The only residue is the identity of the unit group. Thus $\chi(n)=1$ for every integer, including $0$. The zeroth series term is still zero. Here $L(s,\chi)=\zeta(s)$, and the reciprocal for $\sigma>1$ is $D_\mu(s)$. The coefficient identity reduces to $\mathbf 1\ast\mu=\delta$.

<a id="mobius-inverse-absolute-convergence-boundary"></a>

**Remark (failure of absolute convergence on the line).** At $\Re s=1$ we have $\chi(1+kN)=1$ and $(1+kN)^{-1}\ge [N(k+1)]^{-1}$, so the series for $\chi$ fails to converge absolutely at every height. The reciprocal series also fails absolute convergence. On primes $p$ outside the finite set dividing $N$, its absolute terms are $1/p$, and Euler's prime reciprocal sum diverges. This uses the named input `not_summable_one_div_on_primes` [Lean]. The finite convolution identity still holds, while the two absolute sums needed for the Cauchy product estimate are infinite. The closed-half-plane declaration is listed in [§2.6.2](#262-mathlib-declarations-used-by-these-proofs).

| Scope | What the argument supplies |
|---|---|
| Positive integer coefficients, independently of $s$ | The finite convolution identity for every modulus $N\ge1$, including the principal, primitive and imprimitive cases. |
| $\Re s>1$ | Both absolute sums are finite. The product regroups by the positive multiplication fibers and gives the reciprocal of the continued $L$-function via series agreement. |
| $\Re s=1$ | Both absolute sums diverge. The coefficient identity still holds. The reciprocal proof supplies no boundary nonvanishing conclusion. |
| $\Re s<1$ | Outside the application of the character-series product and of the strict branch in this section. |

#### 2.6.3.1 Regrouping an absolutely convergent product by multiplication

The reciprocal calculation supplies a finite coefficient at each positive integer. To turn it into a product of series, we must account for every ordered pair in the product of the two sums. A rectangular cutoff limits the two coordinates separately, while a Dirichlet-series cutoff limits their product. An absolute tail estimate lets us pass between these cutoffs.

<a id="multiplication-fiber-at-twelve"></a>

**Example (the fiber at twelve).** Take $u_a=\chi_4(a)a^{-s}$ and $v_b=\mu(b)\chi_4(b)b^{-s}$, with $\chi_4$ as above and $\Re s>1$. The positive divisors of $12$ are $1,2,3,4,6,12$, so there are six ordered pairs. Each weighted term has the common factor $12^{-s}$.

| $(a,b)$ | $\chi_4(a)$ | $\mu(b)\chi_4(b)$ | Coefficient of $12^{-s}$ |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| $(1,12)$ | $1$ | $0$ | $0$ |
| $(2,6)$ | $0$ | $0$ | $0$ |
| $(3,4)$ | $-1$ | $0$ | $0$ |
| $(4,3)$ | $0$ | $(-1)(-1)=1$ | $0$ |
| $(6,2)$ | $0$ | $0$ | $0$ |
| $(12,1)$ | $0$ | $1$ | $0$ |

In every pair an even coordinate forces a zero. The fiber sum is $12^{-s}(0+0+0+0+0+0)=0$. The square cutoff at $4$ includes $(3,4)$ and $(4,3)$ from this fiber. The product cutoff at $12$ includes all six pairs.

<a id="multiplication-zero-fiber-control"></a>

**Example (retaining a zeroth term).** On $\mathbb N=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$ put $u_0=2$, $v_0=3$, and $u_a=v_a=2^{-a}$ for $a\ge1$. The geometric sum on either positive axis is $1$, so the product of the full sums is $(2+1)(3+1)=12$. The pairs with product zero consist of $(0,0)$, $(0,b)$ for $b\ge1$, and $(a,0)$ for $a\ge1$. Their total is
$$
2\cdot 3+2\sum_{b\ge1}2^{-b}+3\sum_{a\ge1}2^{-a}=6+2+3=11.
$$
The positive pairs sum to $1\cdot1=1$, as is also seen by squaring the finite geometric sums and passing to the limit. Dropping the zero fiber therefore loses $11$ even though both factors converge absolutely. This example uses raw sequences. Mathlib's Dirichlet-series term convention sets both zeroth terms to zero before multiplying.

<a id="positive-multiplication-fibers"></a>

**Definitions.** For a family $w$ on a set $I$, the unordered sum $\sum_{i\in I}w_i=S$ means that for every $\varepsilon>0$ there is a finite $E_0\subset I$ such that
$$
\left|\sum_{i\in E}w_i-S\right|<\varepsilon
\qquad(E_0\subseteq E\subset I,\ E\text{ finite}).
$$
This is the finite-subset limit expressed by Mathlib's default `HasSum`. For a summable family, `tsum` is its value. The absolute mass on any subset is the supremum of the finite sums of the norms there. Write $I=\mathbb N_{>0}\times\mathbb N_{>0}$ and $m(a,b)=ab$. For $n\ge1$, write $F_n=m^{-1}(\{n\})$. Its elements are exactly $(d,n/d)$ with $d$ a positive divisor of $n$. Indeed $ab=n$ with $a,b\ge1$ gives $1\le a,b\le n$ and fixes $b$ once $a$ is given. Thus $F_n$ is finite and its pairs are ordered. On $\mathbb N\times\mathbb N$, the zero fiber is the union of the two coordinate axes and contains infinitely many distinct pairs.

<a id="absolute-series-transport-through-fibers"></a>

**Lemma (transport through fibers).** Let $w_i\in\mathbb C$ have finite absolute mass $M$ on $I$, and let $p:I\to J$ be any map. Then every fiber has an unordered sum, the fiber sums form an absolutely summable family, and their unordered sum equals the unordered sum of $w$.

**Proof.** Given $\varepsilon>0$, choose a finite $E_0$ whose absolute mass exceeds $M-\varepsilon$. Every finite set disjoint from $E_0$ has absolute mass less than $\varepsilon$, bounded uniformly by $M-\sum_{i\in E_0}|w_i|$. Two finite sums over supersets of $E_0$ differ by at most this bound, using their symmetric difference. Completeness of $\mathbb C$ gives the unordered sum. The argument applies to each restriction. Passing to the limit in the triangle inequality bounds the norm of every fiber sum by that fiber's absolute mass. For finitely many distinct fibers the sum of their absolute masses is at most $M$. Indeed one can approximate each mass from below by a finite sum and take the disjoint union of the approximating sets. This proves absolute summability of the fiber sums.

There is a bijection from pairs $(j,i)$ with $p(i)=j$ to $I$, given by forgetting $j$, with inverse $i\mapsto(p(i),i)$. Finite sums are carried exactly across this bijection. For a finite set $B\subset J$ containing $p(E_0)$, the sum of the fiber sums over $B$ equals the unordered sum on $p^{-1}(B)$. For this finite union, take a finite witness for convergence in each fiber with error less than $\varepsilon/(1+|B|)$ and unite the witnesses. Any finite superset in $p^{-1}(B)$ then gives that error bound in each fiber, and the errors add to less than $\varepsilon$. The complementary union lies outside $E_0$, with absolute mass at most $M-\sum_{i\in E_0}|w_i|$. Adding the two unordered sums by the same finite-union argument shows that the fiber sum over $B$ differs from the full sum by less than $\varepsilon$. This is precisely the finite-subset limit on $J$. $\square$

<a id="multiplicative-cauchy-product"></a>

**Theorem (multiplicative Cauchy product).** Let $u,v:\mathbb N_{>0}\to\mathbb C$ satisfy $U=\sum_{a\ge1}|u_a|<\infty$ and $V=\sum_{b\ge1}|v_b|<\infty$. Then $c_n=\sum_{(a,b)\in F_n}u_av_b$ is absolutely summable and
$$
\sum_{n\ge1}c_n
=\sum_{(a,b)\in I}u_av_b
=\left(\sum_{a\ge1}u_a\right)\left(\sum_{b\ge1}v_b\right).
$$
The identity also holds for the zero extensions of $u$ and $v$ to $\mathbb N$, with the extra coefficient $c_0=0$. Write $T_u(K)=\sum_{a>K}|u_a|$, $T_v(K)=\sum_{b>K}|v_b|$ and $E_K=VT_u(K)+UT_v(K)$. The error from the product is at most $E_K$ for rectangles with both sides at least $K$ and for hyperbolic cutoffs $ab\le X$ with $X\ge K^2$.

**Proof.** Each finite set of pairs lies in a rectangle. The sum of the absolute values on the rectangle is the product of the two finite sums of absolute values there, so the absolute mass of the family is at most $UV$. Apply the lemma with $p=m$ to obtain the sum of the $c_n$ and its absolute convergence. The sum over the rectangle $a\le A$, $b\le B$ is $(\sum_{a\le A}u_a)(\sum_{b\le B}v_b)$ by finite distributivity. These rectangles eventually contain any given finite set, so their sums approach the unordered sum as $A,B\to\infty$. By continuity of multiplication, the limit is the product of the two series.

Outside the square of side $K$ the pairs satisfy $a>K$ or $b>K$. For a finite set of such pairs, bound the part with $a>K$ by the product of a finite $u$-tail sum of norms and a finite $v$ sum of norms. Bound the part with $b>K$ by the corresponding product with the roles reversed. This gives a uniform bound $E_K$ even if a pair is counted twice. Taking suprema gives the same bound for the absolute mass outside the square. A rectangle with $A,B\ge K$ contains the square, and so does $ab\le X$ for $X\ge K^2$. The latter set is finite since both positive coordinates are at most $X$. Its sum equals $\sum_{1\le n\le X}c_n$ by finite partition. For either cutoff the omitted sum has norm at most $E_K$, which tends to zero. In particular the two finite sums differ by at most $2E_K$ at these cutoffs.

Upon zero extension, any new pair has an index $0$ and contributes zero. Finite sums on the enlarged domain restrict to exactly the same finite sums on positive pairs, and the witnesses for convergence remain valid. Every term on the zero fiber is zero, so its unordered sum is zero despite the infinite index set. To discard this fiber in the formal argument Mathlib uses termwise vanishing. For arbitrary sequences a cancellation within the zero fiber could also make its total zero, but absolute summability alone does not imply that cancellation. $\square$

<a id="lseries-hassum-convolution"></a>

**Application to the reciprocal.** For $f,g:\mathbb N\to\mathbb C$, use their series terms $u_n=\operatorname{term}(f,s,n)$ and $v_n=\operatorname{term}(g,s,n)$. Each is $0$ at $n=0$ and is the coefficient times $n^{-s}$ at $n>0$. For $ab=n>0$, the real logarithm gives $a^{-s}b^{-s}=n^{-s}$, so $c_n=\operatorname{term}(f\ast g,s,n)$, including $c_0=0$. If the two term families have sums $A,B$ in the sense above, they are absolutely summable in $\mathbb C$. To see this implication, take a finite convergence witness for error $1$. Every finite sum outside the witness has norm at most $2$, by taking the difference of the sums over the witness with and without that finite set. Split any finite set outside the witness according to the signs of its real and imaginary parts in turn. Each sign class has absolute component sum at most $2$. Since $|z|\le|\Re z|+|\Im z|$, the total absolute mass outside the witness is at most $8$. Thus the theorem proves the actual `LSeriesHasSum.convolution` assertion with output sum $AB$. In particular, whenever the two Dirichlet series converge absolutely at $s$, this gives $D_{f\ast g}(s)=D_f(s)D_g(s)$ with absolute convergence of the convolution series.

For $\Re s>1$, the comparison and coefficient inverse from §2.6.3 give the two input sums $D_\chi(s)$ and $D_{\mu\chi}(s)$ and identify the output family with the terms of $\delta$. Its unique unordered sum is $1$. In particular, writing $h$ for the term family of $\chi\ast(\mu\chi)$, the convolution step gives
$$
\operatorname{HasSum}\bigl(h,D_\chi(s)D_{\mu\chi}(s)\bigr),
\qquad \operatorname{HasSum}(h,1).
$$
In the fixed proof `LSeries.mul_mu_eq_one` calls `LSeries_convolution'`; that declaration turns both summability witnesses into `LSeriesHasSum`, takes the convolution sum, and reads off its value using `LSeries_eq`. It then remains to rewrite by the coefficient inverse and evaluate the delta series. The strict branch then uses `LSeries_ne_zero_of_one_lt_re` and transports its conclusion along `LFunction_eq_LSeries`. [Nonvanishing.lean:398–401](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/Nonvanishing.lean#L398-L401) · [Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/Dirichlet.lean:218–224](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/Dirichlet.lean#L218-L224)

**Classical context.** The transport lemma is the grouping form of absolute rearrangement, or discrete Fubini for counting indices. One-dimensional permutation invariance follows by taking singleton fibers. Tonelli's nonnegative rearrangement corresponds to applying the same grouping to the absolute masses. Here the proof uses finite sums and completeness. The ordinary additive Cauchy product groups by $a+b$ and is the setting of Mertens' theorem, which requires absolute convergence of only one factor when the other converges. [Jiří Lebl, *Basic Analysis I*, Proposition 2.6.3 and Theorem 2.6.5](https://www.jirka.org/ra/html/sec_moreonseries.html) · [T. Tao, 245A Notes 6, Remark 37 and Corollary 46](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2010/10/30/245a-notes-6-outer-measures-pre-measures-and-product-measures/)

**Reading the fixed proof [Lean, read].** The three stages in `LSeriesHasSum.convolution` are visible in its two proof lines. First it takes the summable norms of both input families. The proof of `summable_mul_of_summable_norm` multiplies the two nonnegative norm families by summing rows first. Each row has sum $|u_a|V$, and the row sums are summable by scalar multiplication. The norm inequality compares the norm of the complex product to the product of the norms. The final passage from summable norms to summable complex terms uses completeness. The rectangle proof above gives a direct estimate for this same step over positive integer indices.

Next `HasSum.mul` starts with a sum of the summable pair family. In `HasSum.mul_eq`, every row has sum $u_a B$ by continuity of multiplication. The row grouping gives an outer sum with the same value as the pair family. Independently, multiplying the first sum by $B$ gives the outer sum $AB$. Uniqueness identifies these values. The grouping by rows here is through the first projection.

Finally `HasSum.tsum_fiberwise` changes the grouping to the multiplication map. Its proof uses the equivalence between the disjoint union of the fibers and the original index type. Each fiber has a sum by restriction of a summable family. The underlying `HasSum.sigma` works with closed neighborhoods of the target. For a given closed neighborhood, project the finite convergence witness for the total sum onto the outer indices. For any finite set of outer indices containing this projection, filter the finite partial sums to those indices. Eventually these filtered sums still contain the original witness and lie in the closed neighborhood. Taking limits inside the finitely many selected fibers gives their sum in the same neighborhood. This proves convergence of the outer finite sums. At a positive index, `term_convolution'` replaces the fiber sum by the finite sum on `Nat.divisorsAntidiagonal`. That finset scans first coordinates in $[1,n]$, retaining $(a,n/a)$ exactly when it multiplies to $n$. At zero the formal proof expands the fiber as the union of the axes and uses `term_zero` on each axis. This matches the output term at zero.

The additive grouping proofs are generated from the displayed multiplicative bodies. |

The following formal inputs retain [Lean] at this reading boundary. `Summable.norm` supplies finite-dimensional absolute summability (see `Mathlib/Analysis/Normed/Module/FiniteDimension.lean:612–628`). `Summable.of_norm`, `Summable.of_norm_bounded` and `summable_iff_cauchySeq_finset` supply the completeness step. `NNReal.summable_sigma` and `ENNReal.tsum_sigma'` underlie the nonnegative row criterion. `Equiv.sigmaFiberEquiv`, `Equiv.hasSum_iff`, `Summable.subtype`, `closed_nhds_basis`, `tendsto_finset_preimage_atTop_atTop`, and `Finset.tsum_subtype'` supply the reindexing, topology and finite-subtype infrastructure. The textbook arguments establish the required complex specializations directly. They use completeness of $\mathbb C$. A measure-theoretic Tonelli theorem is unnecessary for this proof.

For the general Banach-space finite-tail criterion used elsewhere in the book, see [Chapter 1, §1.2.2.9](01-statement-and-fidelity.md#1229-unordered-summation-finite-tail-cauchy-criteria-and-banach-completeness).

**Summary of the chapter.** Every Dirichlet $L$-function differs from its primitive $L$-function
only by finitely many Euler factors $1 - \chi^*(p) p^{-s}$, none of which vanishes on $\Re s > 0$
(Lemma 2.1) [Lean, read]. Hence non-vanishing of primitive $L$-functions on $\Re s > 1/2$
(`ZeroFreeAll`) implies non-vanishing of all Dirichlet $L$-functions on $\Re s > 1/2$
(`EveryCharacter`, Proposition 2.2) [Lean, read], and reflecting across $\Re s = 1/2$ via the
functional equations of $\Lambda(s,\chi^*)$ and of $\zeta(s)$ immediately yields all five theorems
of `src/Main.lean` (Theorems 2.6 and 2.7) [Lean, read]. The entire remainder of the book is therefore
devoted to proving `ZeroFreeAll`.
